## 📚 Paper List

- **[What Can a VLA Learn from the Agent That Calls It? Task-Level Experience Distillation for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16246)**  `arXiv:2611.16246`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinze Tao, Yuxiao Han, Kangkang Dong, Yancong Wei, Xiaojun Zhu, Bokui Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models drive the robot directly from camera views, yet a VLA alone shows three typical failures. It walks past the goal, stops before reaching it, and rarely recovers after going off track. Pairing a VLA with a large language model (LLM) planner fixes these failures, but queries the LLM throughout every episode, adding token costs and latency. We introduce Harness-to-Executor (H2E), a task-level distillation method that pairs an LLM planner and a frozen VLA as a heterogeneous training agent, keeps only sessions that reach the goal, and then trains a VLA alone on them so no LLM is used at test time. In these sessions, the planner gives sub-instructions and moves the robot with its own tools, detours are common, and the planner decides when to stop. H2E therefore labels every motion as a VLA action for the original task, whoever made it. It masks the loss on steps moving away from the goal and assigns end labels to states near it, so the VLA learns where the task ends. H2E sets a new state of the art on REVERIE-CE and NavRAG-CE. It raises an 8B VLA's success rate by 15.6% and 11.1% respectively, surpassing a method trained on 10M samples. The distilled VLA exceeds its agent on unseen episodes with zero LLM tokens and runs 3.7 times faster.
  </details>

- **[Agent on Guard: Self-Improving Runtime Intervention for Robust Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30101)**  `arXiv:2611.30101`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Gengming Cui, Mingfei Han, Jiawei Zhang, Mozheng Liao, Yunkai Guo, Minghao Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot foundation policies provide learned execution capabilities, while programming agents can reason about failures and construct recovery behaviors. Combining them effectively requires learning when intervention helps and when the policy can reliably resume. This challenge is inherently temporal: the physical world continues to evolve during reasoning, and a useful recovery may become infeasible before a decision takes effect. We introduce Agent on Guard (AoG), a hierarchical framework that improves collaboration between a frozen base policy and a programming agent through physical experience. An evolving outer planner allocates control as the physical state evolves, aligning decisions with the physical clock with attention to observation freshness, remaining recovery windows, and the time required for reasoning, recovery, and subsequent execution. Coupled inner loops jointly refine intervention timing, recovery skills, and handoff conditions. Rather than judging recovery by local correction alone, AoG uses downstream task progress, failure recurrence, final outcomes, and resource consumption to improve both recovery behaviors and scheduling. This turns temporary agent solutions into reusable collaboration capabilities that complement the frozen policy. On RoboDojo-Sim, AoG achieves a success rate of 50.57% and a task score of 57.26, exceeding the strongest evaluated baseline by 11.22 percentage points and 10.68 points, respectively. It also reduces end-to-end episode latency by 80.4% and online token consumption by 85.1% relative to the GPT-6 Astra agent.
  </details>

- **[Contact Harness: Enabling VLM Agents to Perform Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29377)**  `arXiv:2611.29377`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Shilin Shan, Yuxuan Hu, Chuhao Zhou, Yimeng Dong, Bohan Hou, Jiaqi Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents built on pretrained VLMs have demonstrated zero-shot control of real robots, but vision alone is often insufficient for reliable contact-state assessment in contact-rich manipulation. Force and tactile feedback provide complementary information, but their semantic ambiguity makes them difficult for agents to use directly. These signals must be interpreted according to their physical meaning, together with task phase and visual evidence. We propose Contact Harness, which uses reusable, optimized skills to guide a VLM agent in assessing contact states from multimodal feedback and selecting subsequent actions. A compliant execution mechanism regulates in-contact motions using real-time force feedback. The framework requires no model fine-tuning, task demonstrations, or simulation training. We conduct zero-shot real-robot evaluations using GPT-6 Astra on Rokae and Franka robots. With a single prompt skill, Contact Harness achieves a full-insertion rate of 90.7% across 15 connector types, with further evaluations on a real PC under variations in lighting, background, and position. Across four additional contact-rich tasks, including whiteboard wiping, writing, peeling, and pivoting, Contact Harness achieves an average success rate of 100%. On six simulated tasks, Contact Harness reaches 96.7% success, compared with at most 68.3% for pretrained VLA baselines fine-tuned on task demonstrations, showing the potential of agent-based manipulation for contact-rich real-world deployment.
  </details>

- **[RoboUniverse-10K: Toward the Scaling Era of Agentic Robotics via Real-to-Sim Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31002)**  `arXiv:2611.31002`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL`  
  _Jinkun Liu, Yingze Wang, Situ Wang, Deyao Hong, Mingju Gao, Wenyi Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic Robotics, in which foundation models act as coding agents to control robots through executable programs, has emerged as a compelling paradigm for embodied intelligence. Yet unlike software engineering, where massive executable environments power post-training, robotics remains constrained by small, manual benchmarks and in-distribution evaluation. To bridge this gap, we introduce RoboUniverse-10K, 10,807 interactive, resettable, and verifiable physics environments built from real-world videos via an automated Real2Sim and quality assurance pipeline. Each runs in an isolated, Harbor-compatible harness that hides privileged states and grading logic to mirror real-world sensing. From the same pipeline, we hold out 500 unseen tasks to establish RoboUniverse-Verified, an expert-verified zero-shot benchmark. Evaluating 11 closed- and open-source models reveals a huge gap: frontier models reach 85.8\% success, while open-source baselines lag at 2.6\% to 10.0\%, bottlenecked by low-level grasping, deformable-object manipulation, and post-contact physical control. Finally, we collect about 17K multi-turn trajectories totaling 0.79B tokens with GPT-6 Astra and perform full-parameter supervised fine-tuning on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, yielding RoboUniverse-35B-A3B. Our model achieves substantial zero-shot gains on RoboUniverse-Verified, lifting the success rate from 0.8\% to 21.2\%, while achieving zero-shot task completions across LIBERO-MAX, RoboCasa, RoboTwin 2.0, and a real Franka arm. We open-source the RL-ready environments and model to lay the foundation for large-scale reinforcement learning in Agentic Robotics.
  </details>

- **[Body and Soul: Decoupling Robotic Brain and Policy with RoboHarness-Zero](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30550)**  `arXiv:2611.30550`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL`  
  _Yihao Shen, Yuxuan Chen, Xuantong Liu, Jun Gao, Weijian Luo, Tianyang Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can execute individual skills but fail to complete tasks that require combining several of them. In our audit of Xiaomi-Robotics-1, an estimated 43\% of failures involve choosing the wrong target or losing track of task progress. Wrapping a frozen VLA in a vision-language model (VLM) harness is a natural remedy. Existing harnesses can gain substantially from task- and policy-specific preparation, raising the question of how much of that gain transfers to a new task or policy. An ideal harness should (i) need minimal preparation for a new task, (ii) need no redesign for a new policy, and (iii) preserve standalone capabilities the policy already has. To the best of our knowledge, our \ours{} is the first harness built this way. Its premise is that choosing targets and tracking progress are easy for a VLM such as GPT-6 without task preparation, while the frozen VLA already handles continuous, contact-rich motion. The frozen VLA therefore executes continuous control while the VLM plans zero-shot and monitors execution; when progress stalls or targets drift, the VLM first rephrases the instruction and, only as a fallback, executes short generated code over generic motion primitives before returning fine control to the VLA. The supervision rules stay fixed across policies, requiring only a thin interface adapter and an automatically profiled skill configuration per policy. On 200 RoboCasa365 scenes, \ours{} with GPT-6 Astra raises the success rate of frozen Xiaomi-Robotics-1 from \textbf{\color{blue}57.5\%} to \textbf{\color{blue}70.5\%} and preserves \textbf{\color{blue}90.4\%} of its standalone successes (104/115) while rescuing 37 of 85 failures. On a shared 50-scene subset, the same rules add 8 to 10 percentage points to each of four other frozen policies, while existing preparation-based harness systems often fail to transfer to a new policy.
  </details>

- **[RoboInF: A Benchmark for Instruction Fidelity in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30555)**  `arXiv:2611.30555`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Guo, Yu Huang, Dijiang Dong, Yue Liu, Peiran Ren, Wei Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise language control requires embodied models to follow instructions governing both task outcomes and execution. Although recent methods train policies to follow increasingly detailed commands, existing benchmarks often assess instruction following alongside perception, planning, and generalization to unfamiliar scenes. Higher task success therefore offers limited evidence of closer adherence to instructions. We introduce RoboInF, a benchmark measuring how reliably manipulation policies adapt their behavior to changes in an instruction. For each of eight tasks, six to twelve expert-validated instruction branches vary the requested goal, procedure, or manipulation detail while holding the initial scene and robot state fixed. Task-specific checkers verify the terminal goal and use recorded trajectories, contacts, and event sequences to assess compliance throughout execution. The accompanying RoboInF-4K dataset pairs 4,242 expert demonstrations with simulator-derived annotations of motion and contact. We evaluate leading manipulation policies ($\pi_{0.5}$ and OpenWAM) and inference-only agents (GPT-6 Astra and Qwen3.8-Flash). All four models exhibit a gap between task completion and faithful execution, indicating substantial room for fine-grained instruction following.
  </details>

- **[RoboThink: Let the Agent Think, Let the Policy Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30111)**  `arXiv:2611.30111`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunbei Zhang, Zijian Jin, Janet Wang, Xingchen Zhao, Zhenyu Zhang, Weirui Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot policies act in real time but fail without knowing why, whereas a reasoning agent that watches the robot can often tell what went wrong. Existing systems either hand the agent control, which takes minutes per episode, or let it send instructions. Yet a policy follows a new instruction only from a fresh start, since a half-finished task leaves it in states outside its training data. Nor can an instruction fix how the robot moves. If a drift keeps pulling the hand to the left, "push right, slightly" is not a task the policy has learned. We propose RoboThink, in which the agent thinks and the policy learns to act on the agent's guidance. An instruction channel tells the policy what to do. When the user says "the milk, not the butter" after the robot has started, the agent rewrites the instruction and the robot undoes its own motion, with no training. A feedback channel tells it how to move. It is taught from the agent alone with agent-labelled DAgger, role tokens and counterfactual feedback, and is read only by the policy's action module. On LIBERO, RoboThink with Claude Opus 5.5 raises success from 0.09 to 0.87 under an unknown constant drift and from 0.42 to 0.84 under a random one. It also completes misheard and changed requests in 0.91 to 1.00 of the episodes. Acting alone, GPT-6 Astra needs 2.1 min per success and over a million tokens per episode, whereas RoboThink needs 55 s and 8k tokens. The same recipe works across policies, for both vision-language-action models and world action models, and across robots, from one arm to two arms and a mobile manipulator. On all 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks with two arms, success rises from 0.12 to 0.74 under the constant drift and from 0.31 to 0.56 under the random one. Finally, the agent is a plug-in. A policy taught by one agent works with other agents, including open-weight ones, and grows stronger with a stronger agent, without retraining.
  </details>

- **[RoboLiveMem: Is Robot Memory Solved When the World Does Not Wait?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30047)**  `arXiv:2611.30047`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunbei Zhang, Yuyou Zhang, Janet Wang, Ming Liu, Yijiang Li, Yikai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot agents built on frontier vision-language models now solve most robot memory benchmarks. Does this mean they can remember what they need in a home, where people and machines keep moving things? Existing memory benchmarks cannot answer this question, because they pause the simulator while the model thinks: nothing can change during a decision, so whatever the robot remembers is still true when it acts. We introduce RoboLiveMem, a benchmark that runs the same memory tasks twice, once with the world paused while the model thinks and once with the world running. It contains 29 task families in three tiers, from 16 realistic BEHAVIOR-1K scenes of homes, workshops and a restaurant to controlled ManiSkill tasks. Every task mixes real changes with harmless look-alikes and runs without any change, so that no fixed strategy succeeds, and most tasks have a twin that keeps the change in view, which separates forgetting from acting too late. Testing 16 models on 34,176 episodes, we find three surprises. (1) Paused results hide the problem: when a remembered object changes in a realistic scene, the 8 Claude and GPT models succeed in 97% of episodes on average with the world paused, but in only 50% with the world running, and their ranking changes: Claude Opus 5.5, which never fails when paused, succeeds in 44%. (2) Speed is not the fix: slower models do no worse, and models given only 2 s per decision still fall at least 36 points below their paused success. (3) Models see the change but act on camera frames from before it ended. Looking again just before acting closes most of the realistic gap, yet when identical objects are swapped several times, all nine models we test still lose points to memory.
  </details>

- **[Generalizing Agentic Robotics via Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30997)**  `arXiv:2611.30997`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xinxin Liu, Anh Dao, Wenrui Bao, Rafid Ahmed, Bingxin Xu, Ming Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building general-purpose robots is a central goal in robotics. Agentic robotics pursues this goal with planners that control robots through tool calls. However, existing methods either rely on proprietary models accessed through APIs, which incur high costs and latency, or post-train a planner with reinforcement learning (RL) in a single domain, requiring a separate specialist for each domain with incompatible tools. In large language model (LLM) post-training, multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) has been used to consolidate domain specialists into a single generalist. Yet whether MOPD can build a generalist planner for agentic robotics, how well it performs, and how it compares with other consolidation methods remain unexplored. In this paper, we systematically study these questions: we introduce Robo-MOPD, which trains a single student on closed-loop rollouts in simulation under token-level supervision from matching frozen specialists, and compare it with weight merging, off-policy specialist supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and multi-domain online RL. Experiments on LIBERO-PRO and RoboCasa365 show that a single 9B Robo-MOPD planner reaches 72.9% and 44.8% success (58.9% average), surpassing all consolidated baselines in average success and both 9B specialists on their respective benchmarks. Robo-MOPD excels on long-horizon tasks, outperforming multi-domain online RL by 13.0 percentage points on LIBERO-10.
  </details>

- **[WorldLeague: Benchmarking Physical Agency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30989)**  `arXiv:2611.30989`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xinyang Zhou, Qi Li, Xinchao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI systems can now operate Blender, follow instructions in complex video games, and manipulate objects with robot arms. What range of physical goals can today's AI systems achieve autonomously? Answering this question requires testing systems on tasks that demand different combinations of perception, memory, planning, and action. We introduce \textbf{WorldLeague}, a benchmark that compares AI systems on a common set of \textbf{60 simulated robot tasks} by measuring progress toward each task's physical goal from the robot's trajectory. Robots explore scenes to gather evidence in \textbf{WorldSeek}, rearrange objects in \textbf{WorldShape}, and adapt to changes in \textbf{WorldShift}. Tying these goals to a single control interface limits which AI systems can be compared. In WorldLeague, vision-language models (VLMs) choose targets and motions, while vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs) use their native controls. For each of six capability dimensions, we measure how much progress a system makes toward its goals (Interactive Score) and how often it achieves them (physical success rate). Together, these measures form the system's \emph{Physical Agency Profile}. Among 13 representative AI systems, Claude Fable 5.2 achieves 13 of the 60 goals and earns the highest mean Interactive Score (28.58/100). Fable leads in four dimensions, while GPT-6.1-Sol and GPT-6-Astra tie for first on rule-following tasks. Even when VLMs answer every final question correctly, they receive an Interactive Score of zero in 71.8\% of those episodes. WorldLeague gives Embodied AI, Physical AI, and Agentic AI a shared basis for comparing what AI systems achieve autonomously, from partial progress toward a physical goal to full goal completion. Project Webpage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[LLM-Guided Compositional Continual Learning for Robot Manipulation with Gated Low-Rank Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30667)**  `arXiv:2611.30667`  `cs.RO`  
  _Nilay Kushawaha, Abdalla Swikir, Fares Abu-Dakka_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose manipulation robots must acquire new skills over time without forgetting prior ones or retraining from scratch. We present a continual learning framework that represents each skill as a dedicated expert sub- network in a Progressive Neural Network (PNN). New tasks reuse prior knowledge through low-rank adaptation (LoRA) modules on lateral connections controlled by learnable per-parent gates, enabling explicit attenuation of individual transfer paths while reducing the parameter cost of dense PNN laterals. For long-horizon deployment, a frozen local large language model decomposes natural-language instructions into sub-tasks, routes them to suitable experts, and requests a new expert only when the library is insufficient. Using a shared task-space action interface, we evaluate the framework on twelve MetaWorld tasks and in a Sawyer-to-Panda-to-UR5e cross-embodiment transfer experiment, showing improved task success relative to learning from scratch when demonstrations are limited. Real-robot manipulation experiments further demonstrate that the proposed framework supports compositional execution and expert orchestration.
  </details>

- **[SuperNav-1.5: Structured Spatial Memory For Agentic Navigation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29912)**  `arXiv:2611.29912`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiarui Guo, Jinkai Zhang, Yuanhong Yu, Yongchen Zai, Futang Zhu, Ruizhen Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper addresses the challenge of scene-level memory design for efficient navigation across sessions and successive object goals. Recent methods commonly represent a scene using scene graphs or image--text records. However, dense scene-graph representations impose substantial reasoning and context overhead on multimodal large language models, while image--text records cannot be directly converted into executable geometric routes. To address these limitations, we propose SuperNav-1.5, an agentic navigation system equipped with a structured spatial memory constructed by the agent. The memory consists of an area-segmented occupancy map annotated with agent-maintained, map-grounded memory nodes. This map guides the agent toward unvisited areas and provides executable routes to known locations. For multi-session navigation, we introduce agent-guided relocalization, whereby the agent infers an area prior from current observations and stored memory records to constrain geometric matching and localize the robot within the stored map. Together, these designs enable efficient navigation across session and successive object goals. Experiments show that our method outperforms baselines on single-object, multi-object, and demand-driven navigation benchmarks, with particularly strong gains in path efficiency. Multi-session evaluation demonstrates that the constructed memory can be reused, while deployment on a real quadruped robot further shows the system's applicability.
  </details>

- **[A Glimpse of Training VLMs as Foundation Models for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29338)**  `arXiv:2611.29338`  `cs.RO`  
  _Meng-Hao Guo, Yi Zhang, Zhe-Han Mo, Han-Zheng Wang, Jia-Jun Wang, Xin-Sheng Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, GPT-6 Astra demonstrates strong generalization in robot control, yet limited disclosure of its training leaves open how such capabilities can be reproduced. We introduce nanoAstra, an approach to training vision-language models (VLMs) for generalizable manipulation that combines semantic action modeling with capability-preserving supervised fine-tuning (CP-SFT). nanoAstra represents robot behavior as semantic steps composed of reusable, parameterized action primitives, guided by explicit progress descriptions. Meanwhile, CP-SFT uses an explicit robot-specific system prompt to constrain manipulation outputs to a structured action space, aiming to focus adaptation on robot-specific contexts and promote specialization while minimizing interference with broader pretrained capabilities. With this formulation, we train nanoAstra on more than 5,000 hours of robot data together with general VQA data. On RoboTwin 2.0's randomized benchmark, nanoAstra-9B achieves a 17.8% zero-shot success rate, substantially outperforming advanced VLA and WAM baselines as well as similarly sized VLMs. With inference faster than the corresponding motion execution on a single H20 GPU, our model supports action-chunk-like streaming execution that overlaps inference with motion, reducing the robot's idle time from 35.0% to 6.9%.
  </details>

- **[RoboARCade: Benchmarking Agentic Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08209)**  `arXiv:2611.08209`  `cs.RO`  
  _Satvik Sharma, Hengyuan Hu, Mihika Kamat, Samrat Sahoo, Dorsa Sadigh, Jeannette Bohg_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improvements in multi-modal capabilities have enabled agents to act directly as robot policies, a setting we refer to as agentic robot control (ARC). We introduce RoboARCade, a benchmark of long-horizon manipulation tasks across three embodiments designed to test in-context adaptation, memory, and reasoning for precise control. The tasks build on existing open-source benchmarks, which we make harder by widening the range of initial conditions. RoboARCade is easy to use and extend: all tasks run on a single simulation backend, MuJoCo, each task is a self-contained data pack, and agents connect through interchangeable interfaces, so new tasks, agents, and interfaces can be added independently. The benchmark separates leading agents: GPT-6 Astra achieves 49.8% compared with 43.6% for Claude Fable 5.1, leaving substantial room for improvement. Comparing two interfaces, we find that a richer visual interface adds only 1.5 to 6.8 points at 1.7 to 2.3 times the cost. Failures stem mostly from choosing a poor manipulation strategy or executing a good one imprecisely, rarely from misunderstanding the scene, and agents incorrectly claim success on 24% to 39% of attempted subtasks. Agents can also improve without human input: one successful demonstration in context raises Astra's score from 53.5% to 73.0% on a five-task subset, and a prompt the agent writes and revises itself reaches 72.0%. Code, videos and full traces are available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[Safe Task Completion via Graph-Based Planning for LLM-Powered Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24603)**  `arXiv:2610.24603`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ali Taheri, Alireza Taban, Qizhou Wang, Wanhua Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are used to drive robotic manipulation and planning, yet deploying them on physical systems exposes risks, including jailbreak attacks that induce unsafe behavior. Existing inference-time defenses are mostly evaluated on short instructions with a single hazard. Real tasks may mix safe and unsafe sub-tasks, or combine individually safe actions into an unsafe outcome. For such compound instructions, a single safety decision either misses the hazard or rejects the whole instruction. We introduce safe task completion, a task that requires an LLM-powered robot to refuse the harmful part of an instruction while completing its legitimate part. To address this, we propose RuleGraph, a multi-agent Graph-of-Rules framework that verifies safety at multiple stages rather than relying on a single one. Five agents ground safety principles into world-specific rules, judge each sub-task in context, and derive a safe goal from the legitimate ones. Planning toward this goal is organized as a graph in which each node handles its assigned rules. Candidate plans are verified and aggregated level by level until one plan satisfies every rule. We evaluate RuleGraph on pure-attack and compound instructions from two benchmarks, measuring Attack Success Rate (ASR) and Task Success Rate (TSR). RuleGraph reaches 0% ASR on both benchmarks and highest TSR among all methods, with 98.57% on the RoboGuard benchmark and 83.51% on our long-horizon compound instructions.
  </details>

- **[$λ$-Reachability: Geometric-Horizon Safety Bellman Equations for Humanoid Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16022)**  `arXiv:2606.16022`  `cs.RO`  
  _Rui Chen, Shangtao Li, Yifan Sun, Changliu Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce $\lambda$-Reachability, a scalable approach to Hamilton--Jacobi safety analysis for high-dimensional robotic systems. Unlike prior discounted formulations that rely on fixed one-step Bellman updates, $\lambda$-Reachability employs a stochastic multi-step estimator of the safety value, using a geometrically distributed rollout horizon together with a randomly absorbed terminal. Conceptually analogous to TD($\lambda$), $\lambda$-Reachability interpolates between local self-consistency updates and long-horizon max-over-trajectory safety targets via an interpretable horizon-control parameter. Unlike TD($\lambda$), where the terminal value is always incorporated in learning targets, the terminal safety value in $\lambda$-Reachability is only used at a probability controlled by parameter $\delta$. We formally show that for $\delta<1$, the update induces a contraction mapping that allows temporal-difference learning; as $\lambda \to 1$, the estimator recovers the undiscounted reachability objective. We apply $\lambda$-Reachability to high-dimensional safety learning problems with both simulated and real humanoid robots under balance and collision avoidance constraints. Experimental results demonstrate that $\lambda$-Reachability significantly improves both safe-set boundary classification and safety margin estimation compared to single-step temporal-difference baselines.
  </details>

- **[The Evaluation Substrate Matters: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Serving Substrates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28671)**  `arXiv:2611.28671`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haoyuan Zheng, Dongting Li, Hancheng Ye, Qinsi Wang, Mingyuan Ma, Yuncheng Yao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) usually coordinate heterogeneous models and tools to solve complex tasks, creating a growing need for reliable evaluation. However, a multi-agent workflow that is fastest in one serving environment can lose its advantage in another, even when its models, prompts, and task remain fixed. This makes a benchmark result conditional on how the workflow is executed. We introduce MASSys, a benchmark for measuring this dependence in multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems. MASSys executes the same candidate-task pairs across serving substrates and load regimes, using five workload families spanning software repair, research synthesis, financial analysis, interactive control, and diagram generation. It records task quality alongside delivery, latency, cost, resource pressure, and failure evidence. In a controlled motivation diagnostic, the Star workflow falls from first to fourth in mean-latency rank under KV-cache pressure, while Chain rises from fourth to first. The substrate sweeps further show that increasing load creates long runtime tails and timeout pressure. In several task families, quality among evaluated outputs changes less than effective quality over submitted jobs: the system delivers fewer usable results even when the surviving outputs remain competitive. Tool and runtime traces reveal that these outcomes involve both model serving and non-model execution. Together, the results motivate evaluating MAS designs over explicit operating conditions and reporting which tasks are delivered, how well they are solved, and where execution breaks down.
  </details>

- **[Active Learning for Communication Structure Optimization in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05703)**  `arXiv:2605.05703`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Huchen Yang, Xinghao Dong, Dan Negrut, Jin-Long Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing the communication structure of large language model based multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) has been shown to improve downstream performance and reduce token usage. Existing methods typically train on randomly sampled tasks. We show that this optimization is sensitive to the choice of training tasks: downstream accuracy varies widely across random task sets, and adding more random tasks does not consistently improve it. To actively identify the most valuable tasks from a pool of task-answer pairs, we propose an ensemble-based information-theoretic task selection framework. It estimates task informativeness by how much a candidate task changes the distribution over graph parameters, using ensemble Kalman inversion as an efficient and derivative-free approximation of the Bayesian update. The resulting estimator is especially suitable for black-box and noisy multi-agent systems and remains effective with ensembles much smaller than the parameter dimension. To further reduce the number of tasks whose informativeness must be evaluated, we introduce representative pre-selection and surrogate modeling. We validate the framework in benign and adversarial settings and across multiple task formats. It outperforms random training, demonstrating more effective task selection and greater overall cost efficiency.
  </details>

- **[From Local Errors to System Failures: Modeling and Benchmarking Error Propagation in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31063)**  `arXiv:2611.31063`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenlong Yu, Bingxu Lu, Qilong Wang, Qinghua Hu, Qianru Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs), errors can propagate through intermediate messages and compromise final answers. However, outcome-based evaluation obscures how agents amplify, attenuate, or correct these errors. We propose Response-Aware Error Propagation among Agents (REPA), a structural model that connects measured local responses to system-level error propagation. REPA separately estimates input assembly and agent processing across multiple error channels, while theoretical analysis relates local modeling errors to endpoint prediction error. Experiments span three Qwen base models, six MAS workflows, and ten knowledge and reasoning settings in ErrPropBench-10. REPA achieves a mean condition-level Spearman correlation of 0.84 across base models, compared with 0.47 for direct regression. Controlled experiments also demonstrate its utility for targeted recovery. REPA-guided retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves accuracy over no retrieval by an average of 19 percentage points with 16% additional tokens. By linking local agent behavior to system susceptibility, REPA provides an interpretable foundation for studying error propagation and developing targeted optimization in MAS.
  </details>

- **[Vetoes and missed agreements in language-model bargaining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30950)**  `arXiv:2611.30950`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Fuad Ali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language model played three representatives, each instructed to get the best payoff for its own group. One representative requested a plan, another chose the sole offer, and all three voted. If anyone voted no, each group received its fallback payoff. In 60 games with Qwen3-Next-80B, the representative at risk of a loss always requested a plan that would leave everyone better off. In each game, two copies of the proposer saw the same payoffs, peer request and reminder that the offer needed three yes votes. Showing the proposer how many groups gained under each plan raised mutually beneficial agreements from 6/60 to 31/60 (paired increase 41.7 percentage points, conservative 95% interval 18.8 to 58.5). Two of the four plans benefited everyone, so picking uniformly would put a mutual gain on the ballot half the time on average. In an earlier study, GPT-4.1 played 12 conflict games under two message conditions. Majority rule passed 15 of 24 offers, all at one group's expense. Unanimity passed two, both mutual gains. In the Qwen games, voters never saw the counts. They approved all 37 offers that helped everyone. On all 83 offers that would have cost one group points, its representative voted no.
  </details>

- **[Do LLM Agents Compromise Like Humans? Risk and Responsibility in Dyadic Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29069)**  `arXiv:2611.29069`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Noah S. Okada, Ketika Garg, Wenning Deng, Dean Mobbs_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they must collaborate to make joint decisions. We investigate how this collaboration unfolds under risk, asking whether agents adjust their choices to accommodate their partners, how they assign responsibility for shared outcomes, and whether responsibility attribution shapes subsequent adjustment. We designed a series of agents with systematically varying risk preferences and paired them in a repeated joint decision-making task previously conducted with humans. Like humans, agents converged toward their partners' choices. However, similar aggregate behavior arose from different dynamics: agents showed little evidence of reciprocal adjustment, tended to weigh rewards more, and their responsibility attribution was decoupled from compromise. Similarly, while they showed bias in their responsibility attribution, the outcome's valence shaped the attribution differently than in humans. Our results reveal important differences between human--human and agent--agent joint decision-making, demonstrating that similar social outcomes can emerge from different social dynamics and reasoning.
  </details>

- **[TRAE: Trustworthy Automated ESG Annotation via Multi-Agent Consensus and Confidence Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30035)**  `arXiv:2611.30035`  `cs.MA`  
  _Jaeyoung Lee, Misuk Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the growing demand for ESG disclosure and evaluation, constructing high-quality datasets for automatically extracting ESG-related information from large-scale documents has become essential. However, ESG annotation remains challenging due to strong context dependence, ambiguous label boundaries, and the need for domain expertise, resulting in high annotation cost and limited scalability. To address these challenges, we propose TRAE (TRustworthy Automated ESG annotation), a multi-agent framework for scalable and reliable ESG data construction. Unlike direct LLM-based annotation, TRAE evaluates annotation reliability by modeling agreement, confidence, and reasoning consistency among multiple agents through a structured multi-step reasoning process. Experimental results demonstrate that TRAE improves annotation performance compared with existing automated annotation strategies. Furthermore, TRAE supports a hybrid human-AI annotation strategy that assigns expert labeling only to uncertain samples, reducing annotation cost while preserving data quality. Using TRAE, we construct a high-quality ESG dataset containing approximately 20,000 instances with complete reasoning traces, intermediate agent outputs, and reliability information to enhance transparency and reproducibility. Overall, this work provides a practical and scalable framework for trustworthy ESG data construction. The code and data are publicly available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Social Interaction Breaks Replicate Independence in Controlled-Clone LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29581)**  `arXiv:2611.29581`  `cs.MA`  
  _Yandan Zheng, Haoran Luo, Anh Tuan Luu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM evaluations and social simulations create many agents from one model and treat them as independent replicates, even after the agents interact. This assumption remains untested with matched controls. We introduce SocCogEval, a controlled-clone assay for Social Cognitive Transfer (SCT). Clones share the base model, system prompt, and decoding parameters and differ only in a private brief, and a factorial protocol separates the brief, 30 rounds of interaction, and initialization noise. On Qwen3-122B-FP8, interaction with the briefs held fixed raises off-topic BFI-10 response-profile drift by $\bar{\Delta}=+0.917$ and $+0.912$ in two topically disjoint scenarios (exact $p=0.0008$ and $0.0039$). The drift is larger than under the brief alone, persists under temperature-zero re-probing, and stays positive without typed memory extraction in exploratory tests. Matched solo generation reproduces part of it, and social interaction remains highest. Across five more model variants, every scenario-averaged interaction contrast is positive. Each interacting population is therefore one replicate, and treating its agents as independent requires paired no-interaction controls. Our code and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Who Speaks for the Water? Simulating Discursive Closure and Power Asymmetries in Hydrosocial Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28755)**  `arXiv:2611.28755`  `cs.MA`  
  _Mario Lillo-Saavedra, Marcela Salgado-Vargas, Ángel García-Pedrero, Diego Rivera, Marcelo Somos-Valenzuela_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Water governance conflicts stem not only from material scarcity, but from power asymmetries, institutional distrust, and the discursive frameworks through which actors assign meaning to water, infrastructure, and legitimate authority. Conventional simulation tools represent these dynamics via numerical opinion variables, missing the argumentative and narrative dimensions structuring real deliberation in hydrosocial territories. We present the Hydrosocial Agent-Based Model with Large Language Models (HSABM-LLM), a hybrid framework enabling heterogeneous agents to generate, evaluate, and adapt natural language arguments conditioned on institutional roles, interaction histories, and territorial context. Grounded in semi-structured interviews, a water-user survey, and 456 press and official documents from the Longaví River sub-basin (Maule Region, Chile), the model simulates deliberation over a contested large-scale reservoir proposal across four experimental scenarios. Opinion convergence toward unanimous opposition emerged within 7-14 months, operating through discursive closure driven by distributive equity and water security framings rather than argumentative dominance. Governance actors accelerated convergence by activating pre-existing institutional distrust rather than direct persuasion, while peer-based smallholder interactions drove 77-79% of all opinion changes. The formal authority of state institutions proved structurally decoupled from deliberative influence, revealing a legitimacy deficit that formal inclusion alone cannot resolve. These findings suggest participatory governance design must attend to the discursive conditions under which institutional actors enter deliberative spaces, rather than assuming inclusion is inherently consensus-building. HSABM-LLM offers a novel platform for integrating political ecology insights into computational social science.
  </details>

- **[SYLVA: Self-Correcting Multi-Agent Markets over Frozen LLMs via Escrow Settlement, Step-Level Fault Isolation, and Contextual Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28148)**  `arXiv:2611.28148`  `cs.MA`  
  _Hui Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open agent standards like the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and Agent2Agent (A2A) connect independent AI tools, yet open multi-agent markets fail under three real-world hazards: local rules shift silently outside pretraining, copycat wrappers and confident hallucinators game ratings, and uniform chain scoring punishes accurate specialists whenever another stage fails (blame collapse). We present SYLVA, a market protocol turning frozen foundation models (no weight updates, $\Delta W = 0$) and human-maintained services into a self-correcting ecosystem. SYLVA combines an on-chain clearinghouse for escrow settlement and configurable creator royalties (85%/15% in our experiments), a boundary verifier for penalty-free out-of-domain (OOD) abstention and 3-peer step replay, a verifiable context-aware router compiling proven chains into 1-call skills with drift circuit breakers, and plain-English rule updates. We prove sufficient conditions for honest abstention, specialist protection against blame collapse, and wrapper elimination, and validate SYLVA across a 64-agent simulation, 6,543 live deepseek-flash API calls, and a Rust MCP SDK (rmcp) + Cairo clearinghouse deployed on Starknet Sepolia (19 live transactions). Without updating weights, SYLVA reaches 82.1% completion in 64-agent simulations and 98.3% on live LLM workflows (vs. 0.0% single frozen LLM and 45.6% static MCP/A2A), cuts false specialist penalties by 6.79x-10.8x, retains 86.2% creator revenue under wrapper/Sybil floods, and recovers rapidly after rule drift. Furthermore, 1-call skill compilation saves 78.3% of real LLM tokens, while our Starknet contracts verify zero-oracle quorum slashing and 32.32-bit fixed-point (Q32.32) routing on-chain. Code and contracts are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Precision-Aware Learning Rate Transfer Across Block Count in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31166)**  `arXiv:2611.31166`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Nan Chen, Soledad Villar, Soufiane Hayou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The post-training of pretrained large language models, including supervised finetuning (SFT), direct preference optimization (DPO), and reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR), is very sensitive to the learning rate, and tuning it through grid search is costly in both computation and memory. In this work, we propose \textbf{TipSweep}, a method to transfer the learning rate across the number of trainable transformer blocks. To do so efficiently, we search for the best learning rate by training only the last $k_0$ blocks (in our experiments, typically $k_0=1$), and provide scaling rules that predict the optimal learning rate when training any set of $k$ blocks, including when $k=L$ (the full transformer). Across experiments with models ranging from 1 to 7 billion parameters, we observe that the optimal learning rate follows precision-dependent scaling rules: the optimal rate scales approximately as $k^{-1}$ with float32 (FP32) master weights, but as $k^{-1/2}$ when updates are applied directly to bfloat16 (BF16) weights without a master copy. We explain both rules using an analytical model that accounts for update rounding, under assumptions supported by profiling experiments. Finally, we develop two prototypes of the learning rate search for a 6.7-billion-parameter model. The first fits the search on a single consumer GPU with 16 GiB of memory. The second speeds up the search on a high-end GPU by evaluating all candidate learning rates in a single pass over the data, so that a five-candidate search takes about half the GPU hours of one full-depth ($k=L$) run.
  </details>

- **[Lowering the Bar for Everyone: Hidden Validation-Seeking Goals in Empathetic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30702)**  `arXiv:2611.30702`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinche Zhang, Shanghong Xie, Zhiyuan Ma, Jinhao Li, Sen Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When RL learns from the reactions of many users, the policy usually cannot see what each user wants. We ask what it learns from users who privately want validation: to validate them alone (targeting), or to lower its bar for endorsing everyone (a threshold shift). We answer this in one setting, empathetic RL against LLM-simulated users (RLVER, GRPO, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct): we vary only the share of validation-seeking users (0--50%; RLVER's default: 24.6%) and evaluate on 1,000 Reddit AITA posts with community fault verdicts.The policy shifts its threshold. Endorsement of at-fault posters rises by 3--4 percentage points per 10 points of share; without such users the Qwen model stays at base, and the effect persists at matched KL and under a goal-blind reward. Signal-detection analysis shows that the criterion for endorsing falls while discrimination between at-fault and not-at-fault posters does not decline. An explicit request to be told if one is wrong gives no measurable protection: at-fault posters who make it receive nearly the same rise as those who do not (+0.11 vs. +0.13). When the goal is stated during training, the policy instead largely targets, endorsing seekers regardless of fault.Standard sycophancy and empathy benchmarks miss the shift; five of six usable released empathetic-RL checkpoints also endorse at-fault posters who ask for honesty more than their base models, though a pre-registered prediction for two held for only one. The user mix is a training hyperparameter to audit and report.
  </details>

- **[When Can a Rewiring Control Test LLM Weight Readers? C2 and an Eligibility Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29120)**  `arXiv:2611.29120`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Vaishak Girish Kumar, Sanika Vilas Najan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can predict the outputs of small neural networks from raw weights, but success could reflect recognition of weight-space regularities (fingerprints) rather than computation, and function-preserving controls cannot tell these apart. We propose C2, a counterfactual that permutes the columns of a network's final linear readout: every other parameter, and the readout's singular values, norms and entry multiset, are unchanged, and the function can change in an exactly recomputable way; we verify that each evaluated permutation does. C2 separates fingerprinting from computation only under three conditions, which we state as an eligibility protocol: a strategy restricted to C2-invariant summaries must already predict the target; C2 must change a property specific to the rewiring; and the reader's identity must be attested. No natural setting we tested is eligible. On tiny MLPs a frontier reader route followed the rewired function on all 24 networks, but invariant baselines were near chance and its backend could not be attested. In two public CNN zoos the conditions traded off: weight statistics predicted CIFAR-10 accuracy in-distribution, but C2's effect on accuracy was not specific to the swap, while per-image targets were specific but not predictable from invariant summaries. On a constructed population where a planted fingerprint rule works, all conditions hold and C2 separates a rule-following reader from an executor. A self-hosted Qwen3.8-27B followed the rewired function rather than the shortcut, and showed no evidence of using the weights with thinking disabled. A registered replication on fresh networks confirmed both findings (tracking minus stuck 0.875, 95% interval [0.750, 1.000]), and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-14B also followed the rewiring (tracking minus stuck 0.729 [0.552, 0.885]). The transforms, audits and raw records are available from the authors.
  </details>

- **[Physical regime changes expose formula misuse in large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28669)**  `arXiv:2611.28669`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Maohao Ran, Yanting Zhang, Chendong Ma, Jun Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly employed for scientific computing across the Earth and environmental sciences, but problems in these fields often involve distinct physical regimes. LLMs may fail to recognize these regime-dependent constraints, applying formulas outside their domains of validity and producing plausible but physically incorrect results. We systematically investigate these failures through controlled experiments and assess whether post-training can improve regime-aware reasoning. Across 72 physical mechanisms, we find frontier LLMs frequently misapply formulas outside their regimes of validity, reducing accuracy by 13-57 percentage points. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards enables Qwen3.5-9B to match DeepSeek V4 Flash (284B) on regime-dependent tasks, but its effectiveness is limited by the formulas available in the base model. Combining supervised fine-tuning on a few worked examples with reinforcement learning improves accuracy by 61 percentage points, while distillation from a teacher supplied with the correct formula raises accuracy from 0% to 71%.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Forgetting: Representation Misdirection Elicits Controllable Side Behaviors and Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21702)**  `arXiv:2601.21702`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Tien Dang, The-Hai Nguyen, Dinh Mai Phuong, Nguyen Minh Phuong, Anh Bui, Hoang Thanh-Tung, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We consider Representation Misdirection (RM), a simple unlearning mechanism for large language models (LLMs) that achieves unlearning by redirecting the latent representations of forget samples toward a target vector. Despite being important, the role of the target vector used in RM remains insufficiently explored. Here, we approach and revisit RM through the lens of the Linear Representation Hypothesis (LRH). Specifically, if one can identify a one-dimensional representation corresponding to a high-level concept, the LRH enables linear operations on this concept vector within the representation space of the forget samples. From this perspective, we hypothesize that, beyond forgetting, machine unlearning via RM elicits controllable side behaviors and capabilities corresponding to the high-level concept. We empirically validate this hypothesis across a wide range of concepts and tasks, including controlling unlearned models' truthfulness, sentiment, stereotypical bias, refusal, language, and instruction-following. Our findings reveal that this phenomenon could be either a hidden risk if misused or a mechanism that can be harnessed for developing unlearned models that require stronger capabilities and controllable behaviors.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Model Unlearning Under the Microscope: A Benchmark Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07626)**  `arXiv:2510.07626`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Chongyu Fan, Changsheng Wang, Yancheng Huang, Soumyadeep Pal, Sijia Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning for large language models (LLMs), known as LLM unlearning, aims to remove undesired data, knowledge, and behaviors while preserving model capabilities. Despite rapid progress over the past two years, research in LLM unlearning remains fragmented, with limited clarity on what constitutes effective unlearning and how it should be rigorously evaluated. In this work, we present a principled taxonomy of twelve recent stateful unlearning methods, grouped into three methodological families: divergence-driven optimization, representation misalignment, and rejection-based targeted unlearning. Building on this taxonomy, we revisit the evaluation of unlearning effectiveness (UE), utility retention (UT), and robustness (Rob), focusing on WMDP for hazardous knowledge and associated model capability removal. Our analysis shows that current evaluations, dominated by multiple-choice question (MCQ) accuracy, offer only a narrow perspective, often overstating success while overlooking model generation behavior. To address this gap, we introduce open question answering (Open-QA) metrics to better capture generative performance and reveal the strengths and limitations of different unlearning method families. Furthermore, we demonstrate that robustness requires finer-grained analysis. For example, vulnerabilities differ substantially between in-domain relearning and out-of-domain fine-tuning, even though both fall under model-level attacks. Through this study, we provide a full-stack re-examination of LLM unlearning and actionable guidance for designing and evaluating future methods. Code and model checkpoints are publicly available on GitHub and Hugging Face.
  </details>

- **[Direction-Conditioned Policies via Compositional Subgoal Scoring for Online Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16515)**  `arXiv:2606.16515`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _S K Swaminathan, Damiya Gondha, Theyanesh Eswaramoorthy Rajahkrishnan, Aritra Hazra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman theory implies that the optimal goal-conditioned action depends on the goal only through the gradient of the goal-reaching distance at the current state, yet standard online GCRL still conditions the actor on the raw goal -- a signal that is geometrically uninformative when the goal is far from the data distribution. We propose Direction-Conditioned Policies (DCP), a fully online method that decomposes goal-reaching into two components sharing one InfoNCE representation $\psi$: a subgoal-scoring step that selects a visited state $z_t$ aligned with the final goal $g$ in $\psi_g$, and a direction-conditioned actor that consumes the unit direction $d_t$ and magnitude $r_t$ from $\psi(s_t)$ to $\psi(z_t)$. The two components train jointly, factor cleanly at deployment (subgoal scoring is removed, while direction conditioning remains with g in place of $z_t$), and admit independent modification at the same $(d_t,r_t)$ interface. We prove three results. First, direction sufficiency under HJB: the optimal action under control-affine dynamics depends on the goal only through the value gradient. Second, a quantitative bound showing that, under mild conditions on the learned representation and assuming the scoring rule returns an on-path $z_t$, the actor's conditioning input at training and at deployment coincide up to representation error and geodesic slack. Third, a controllable-subspace characterization of when directional conditioning fails. Across nine environments, DCP improves over Contrastive RL on most final metrics, with the largest gains on manipulation and obstacle-interaction tasks; a qualitative analysis of the learned $\psi$-distance landscape shows the contrastive representation behaves as an online quasimetric encoding environment topology, and the single failure case (AntSoccer) localizes to a learned-gradient pathology that the theory anticipates.
  </details>

- **[Can Latent Reasoning Drive LLM Outputs Without a Trace?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31015)**  `arXiv:2611.31015`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Tomer Ashuach, Zorik Gekhman, Avi Caciularu, Ori Ram, Jonathan Herzig_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs typically solve complex tasks with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, which is also the main tool for monitoring them for misaligned behavior. However, standard transformers can also solve tasks with complete latent reasoning (CLR), carrying out the whole task internally and outputting only the final answer. Theory bounds the depth of CLR, the number of sequential steps it can carry out, and recent work measured it to be shallow. In this work, we ask whether CLR is deep enough for a model to pursue a hidden objective that requires reasoning. Such a model would generate a CoT that appears to solve the given task, while a latent multi-step computation decides its answer based on the hidden objective. First, we show that a simple auxiliary loss allows models to solve a multi-step task latently at least 5x deeper than previously reported. Then, in two common tasks, decision-making and code generation, we train a model to pursue such a hidden objective, which decides a loan or plants a vulnerability. The model's CoT justifies the output with no trace of the hidden objective, and a strong LLM monitor does not flag it. Finally, using activation patching, we find that the latent computation is completed before the model generates its CoT, which takes no part in it. Altogether, we show that a hidden objective that requires reasoning can go unseen by a monitor that reads only the output.
  </details>

- **[Uncovering the Tells: Efficient Black-Box Audits for LLM Data Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30639)**  `arXiv:2611.30639`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Dominik Bernard Lau, Filip Pawlicki, Jan Dubiński, Sebastian Cygert_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing methods for assessing training data contamination in Large Language Models achieve high accuracy, but fundamentally rely on gray-box access, rendering them inapplicable to fully black-box environments. This limitation forces auditors to rely on full-sequence sampling or rigid heuristics, both of which incur prohibitive inference costs and struggle to isolate the underlying memorization signal. To overcome this, we propose a generation-based black-box framework that infers context-induced information gain from targeted token discrepancies. We introduce a budget-constrained auditing regime, building on the empirical observation that the membership signal is highly sparse and concentrated in a few highly informative tokens. We formalize this sparsity, showing why uniform sampling needs many more samples than a policy that targets informative positions. Empirically, our approach achieves state-of-the-art black-box separation accuracy while needing about 3x fewer queries than uniform position sampling. Finally, we apply this methodology to closed frontier models, exploring its capacity and limits in surfacing potential training data overlaps on commercial APIs.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Self-Editing: LLMs Program Their Own Weight Edits for Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29513)**  `arXiv:2611.29513`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Linghua Zhang, Yunhe Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement asks a system to improve the very system that improves it. Today's language models improve themselves through data, training recipes or scaffolds. Whenever their weights change, gradient descent writes the update, not the model. We let the model write its own weights. In SELFWRITE, each generation the current model studies its own reasoning on fresh training problems, inspects its own weights and activations in a persistent Python session, and writes a program that constructs a low-rank weight edit for itself from white-box primitives (singular directions, activation contrasts between its right and wrong answers, rank-one projections, tensor computations), with no training or gradient tool. Every candidate is graded once on a hidden gate set against the current model; the best strictly improving edit is merged into the weights, and the edited model writes the next edit. On Qwen3.8-27B and math problems from DAPO-Math, five generations of SELFWRITE raise gate accuracy from 73.95% to 76.45% and accuracy on a held-out test split that the loop never sees from 76.30% to 77.75% (avg@4) and from 82.60% to 84.80% (pass@4), without a single gradient step. A language model can author useful changes to its own weights without gradients, and the edited model keeps writing useful edits.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Accuracy: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Hypothesis-Driven Experiment Planning in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29467)**  `arXiv:2611.29467`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ryo Kuroki, Amin Mansouri, Philippe Schwaller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating large language model (LLM) agents on scientific tasks typically reduces to final-task accuracy, which obscures where reasoning succeeds or breaks down. We introduce a benchmark and diagnostic evaluation framework for hypothesis-driven adaptive experimental planning in closed-world system identification, instantiated in kinetic mechanism identification. The framework goes beyond final accuracy in two ways. First, we evaluate exploration behavior by quantifying the extent to which agents exhibit space-filling or corner-seeking patterns in the experimental design space. Second, we decompose the reasoning process into four interpretable steps: belief update, hypothesis discrimination, discriminative experiment design, and predictive reasoning under interventions, each scored separately on reasoning traces. Across ten LLM agents, we find that stronger agents outperform both adaptive (Bayesian optimization) and non-adaptive (Latin hypercube) baselines and exhibit exploration patterns inconsistent with naive heuristics. Decomposing reasoning reveals that the stronger agents score consistently well across all four steps, whereas weaker agents often succeed at belief update and hypothesis discrimination but are less reliable at translating them into actionable designs and sharp contrastive predictions. Independent expert annotations support predictive reasoning under our stated validation criteria, while evidence for discriminative experiment design is positive but less consistent. The results suggest a gap between recognizing plausible hypotheses and translating them into contrastive predictions, with more tentative evidence for a corresponding experiment-design bottleneck. The benchmark website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Surprising Success, Repeated Failure: Entropy-Guided Credit Assignment for Exploration in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33781)**  `arXiv:2609.33781`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Woongyeong Yeo, Minki Kang, Chanuk Lee, Sangwoo Park, Jinheon Baek, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) enhances reasoning in large language models (LLMs) through outcome-level feedback, yet recent approaches to finer-grained credit assignment often require auxiliary models, additional sampling, or privileged information. Although policy entropy provides a readily available signal, prioritizing uncertain positions under both reinforcement and penalization concentrates penalties where failed responses still retain alternatives for recovery, which can suppress opportunities for exploration. To address this, we introduce Entropic Advantage Policy Optimization (EAPO), an entropy-guided credit assignment method that treats success and failure asymmetrically. Specifically, motivated by the observation that success under uncertainty is less repeatable while confident failures tend to recur, EAPO couples normalized policy entropy with the sign of the response advantage to reinforce surprising success and correct repeated failure. It assigns stronger reinforcement to high-entropy decisions in successful responses and stronger penalties to low-entropy decisions in failed responses, while attenuating penalties at uncertain positions to preserve opportunities for recovery. By redistributing the response advantage across tokens, EAPO derives token-level credit directly from existing rollout signals without additional supervision. We validate EAPO on a range of reasoning tasks across both base and reasoning backbones, demonstrating that it achieves the best overall performance. We further show that EAPO promotes more effective exploration, broadening problem coverage and diversifying successful reasoning strategies.
  </details>

- **[Unsolvability Ceiling in Multi-LLM Routing: An Empirical Study of Evaluation Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07395)**  `arXiv:2605.07395`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Saloni Garg, Amit Sagtani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient routing across multiple LLMs enables cost-quality tradeoffs by directing queries to the cheapest capable model. Prior work attributes routing headroom to an "unsolvability ceiling", queries no model in the pool can solve. We present a large-scale study of multi-tier LLM routing with 206,000 query-model pairs across six benchmarks (MMLU, MedQA, HumanEval, MBPP, Alpaca, ShareGPT) using the Gemma 4 and Llama 3.1 families. Evaluating with both LLM-as-a-judge and exact-match metrics, we show that a substantial portion of reported unsolvability stems from evaluation artifacts: (i) systematic judge biases favoring verbosity over correctness, (ii) truncation under fixed generation budgets, and (iii) output format mismatches. Through dual-judge validation and exact-match grounding, we reduce measured unsolvability across tasks. We introduce a decomposition framework attributing failures to these artifacts, revealing consistent patterns across domains and model families. These artifacts also distort router training signals: standard routers collapse to majority-class prediction (~79% smallest-tier optimal), confirmed via random-feature and shuffled-label controls, incurring a 13-17 percentage point opportunity cost. We provide actionable recommendations including dual-judge validation, exact-match anchoring, and cost-sensitive objectives. Our findings suggest existing routing headroom estimates are substantially inflated, underscoring the need for reliable evaluation protocols in multi-LLM systems.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Patching: Consistent Circuit Discovery via Gradient Spectral Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30939)**  `arXiv:2611.30939`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yujie Zhang, Tianyi Zhao, Guanghui Min, Chen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mechanistic interpretability seeks to reverse-engineer neural networks by identifying small, faithful subgraphs--circuits--that implement specific behaviors. The dominant paradigm scores each unit by replacing its clean activation with a corrupted counterpart as causal intervention. While effective, this approach rests on a brittle prerequisite: the structure prompt pairs are task-specific, expensive to engineer, and silently couple the discovered circuit to prompt-design choices. In this work, we propose Spectral Circuit Analysis (SCA), a gradient-spectral pipeline that achieves consistent circuit discovery along both axes: stable across input formulations of the same task, and natively exposing structure shared across tasks. Our contributions are threefold. First, we provide a counterfactual-free reframing of circuit discovery: causally relevant directions are recoverable from the spectral structure of forward gradients across paraphrases, with leading directions concentrating task signal and trailing ones absorbing paraphrase noise, yielding circuits tied to the task rather than the prompt. Second, we introduce SCA, a block-wise SVD pipeline that extracts a task subspace per architectural block and redistributes spectral energy onto constituent units via a consistency-weighted share, producing per-unit rankings competitive with attribution-patching baselines under strictly less supervision. Finally, on twelve circuit-discovery tasks in GPT-2 Small and Pythia-160M, SCA performs comparably or exceeds EAP-family methods under the standard recovery-score protocol and recovers IOI canonical heads with Precision@K = 1.0 in top-10 slices on GPT-2 Small; the same machinery extends to a unified cross-task analysis classifying units as universal, family-shared, or task-specific and exposing compositional structure invisible to per-task analyses.
  </details>

- **[Why Units Die in the Layer After a Frozen Random Encoder, and What the Number of Live Units Measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29752)**  `arXiv:2611.29752`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Scott M. Norton_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement-learning agents can learn Atari games through a convolutional encoder whose random weights are never trained. They reach expert play in Pong and, without random action repeats, in Breakout, but training takes far longer and few ReLU units in the layer after the encoder stay alive. A dead unit outputs zero for every input and cannot learn. In earlier work we took the number of live units to measure the game's complexity. Here we ask why units die and correct that conclusion. We find that more units die as the layer's inputs lie farther from zero relative to how much they vary. We call this ratio the offset. Earlier work showed this in sparse autoencoders at the start of training. We saw it after training in agents, image classifiers, a reservoir computer and sparse autoencoders on GPT-2. A random encoder leaves the offset of its input roughly unchanged, and a formula predicted its median offset within 25 percent in all 44 cases. So a game with a bright, mostly unchanging screen gives the next layer a large offset. In Pong, subtracting the average screen raised the number of live units from between 3 and 6 of 64 to between 59 and 63, so the number alone cannot show a game's complexity. On unaltered screens none of the agents reached a training score of 18 in 50 million steps. With the subtraction all did within 5 million, about as fast as a trained encoder on unaltered screens. In Breakout the subtraction left more units alive, but average training scores did not clearly improve, and a large gap to a trained encoder remained. So we do not treat their number as something to raise for its own sake. The evidence is from small networks and three games, and in agents and image classifiers changing the offset also changed the size of the inputs. We suggest measuring the offset before training a layer on a fixed front end and, if it is large, trying to subtract the mean input.
  </details>

- **[QASparse: Query-Adaptive Sparse Attention with Tailored Budgets and Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29280)**  `arXiv:2611.29280`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyuan Chen, Zihao Yang, Quanjiang Li, Haijiang Li, Jiancheng Pan, Qichao Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-sequence inference in large language models is constrained by the high cost of prefilling, where dense attention scales quadratically with context length. Exploiting similarity across attention heads offers a way to reduce redundant computation. Prior approaches in this direction have relied on localized query observations to determine head-level computation reuse or budget allocation. However, inter-head similarity does not imply uniform query requirements, leaving adaptation to varying budget needs and selection preferences across query regions a key challenge. We introduce QASparse, a Query-Adaptive Sparse attention mechanism with hardware-efficient execution. QASparse preserves the efficiency of shared cross-head importance estimation while adapting computational budgets and token selections to query requirements, and it executes these dynamic sparse decisions through dedicated kernels. Its query adaptivity stems from two complementary innovations: Local Budget Regression models local variations in computational demand along query positions to estimate budgets for different query regions and attention heads; Boundary Voting lets queries within each head vote over candidates near the selection cutoff using actual query/key scores, refining the retained set while preserving the initial selection size. To reduce the execution overhead of dynamic budgets, we further design length-specialized selection kernels that efficiently support query-adaptive sparse patterns across context lengths. Together, these designs efficiently combine shared estimation with local decisions while adapting both how much attention is computed and which interactions are retained. Experimental results demonstrate improved benchmark scores and faster prefill attention computation compared with existing sparse attention baselines.
  </details>

- **[MolReJev: Decision Models Are In-Context Molecular Predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28965)**  `arXiv:2611.28965`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kaiyuan Lu, Jiatong Li, Yatao Bian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular prediction helps prioritize compounds for screening. Specialized predictors are trained for a fixed set of tasks, limiting cross-task generalization. Language models can condition on task descriptions and have recently shown promise in molecular prediction. However, generating answers token by token adds inference cost, and general-purpose models often lack task-specific molecular knowledge. We thus propose MolReJev, which combines Jev-style candidate scoring with in-context learning (ICL) on a language model. MolReJev retrieves similar labeled examples from existing task data and scores candidate labels jointly in one forward pass. ICL improves predictions from the released general-purpose Kev checkpoint and the separately trained MolReJev. For the trained model, gains span all evaluation groups, including held-out tasks, and extend to compounds absent from training. MolReJev generally outperforms MolecularGPT on matched evaluations and nearest-neighbor prediction with the same examples. With four examples, MolReJev and a trained text baseline achieve similar ROC-AUC, with higher measured throughput for MolReJev. These results show that decision models can serve as in-context molecular predictors for compounds, drug pairs, and reactions.
  </details>

- **[Hidden Sensitivity in LLM Pre-training: A Dynamic System View](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28830)**  `arXiv:2611.28830`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bo Liu, Yuan Xie, Junan Chen, Fujun Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Large Language Model (LLM) pre-training, loss curves can conceal substantial instability, i.e., even small perturbations may lead to dramatically different predictions while leaving loss nearly unchanged. To investigate this phenomenon and reveal the underlying mechanisms, we first formulate LLM pre-training as a non-autonomous dynamical system that jointly evolves model weights and Adam optimizer states. Based on this formulation, we introduce a normalized sensitivity score in optimizer-relative coordinates to identify sensitive training checkpoints. At one such checkpoint, a weight perturbation of only 10^(-5) leads to 35.7% disagreement in top-1 predictions and 16.2% disagreement in benchmark answers after 200 training steps, with only 0.8% of greedy generations remaining identical despite similar validation losses. Furthermore, we find that perturbations to Adam's first and second moments induce comparably large effects, with similar sensitivity patterns observed in OLMo-1B. Finally, guided by our diagnostic, targeted stabilization reduces top-1 disagreement to 0.83% and increases generation exact match to 88.3%, at the cost of a 0.11-nat increase in validation loss. Our findings reveal a hidden form of optimizer-state-dependent sensitivity in LLM pre-training that conventional loss-based evaluations may overlook, while demonstrating its potential for diagnosis and mitigation.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Teacher-Student Capability Gaps with Recurrent Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28603)**  `arXiv:2611.28603`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zeyi Huang, Jiuxiang Gu, Eli Shechtman, Yong Jae Lee, Zongze Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation transfers capabilities from large language models to smaller students, yet stronger teachers do not always yield better students. Recent studies report diminishing or even negative returns from teacher scaling. We hypothesize that these limitations arise in part from insufficient student capacity to absorb the teacher's knowledge. We therefore investigate loop and recurrent computation as a way to expand the student's effective capacity by modifying its computation graph while keeping the backbone parameter count fixed. Under teacher-forced KD during pretraining, weight-shared looping improves performance but retains teacher-size saturation, whereas cross-token recurrence avoids the observed reversal with larger teachers. In mathematical post-training under both teacher-forced KD and on-policy distillation (OPD), recurrent students scale better with increasing teacher size than looped and vanilla LLMs do. Crucially, we show that a pretrained LLM can be readily fine-tuned into a looped or recurrent architecture jointly with OPD, without repeating pretraining. Our recurrent Qwen model improves with larger teachers and consistently outperforms looped and vanilla LLMs. This suggests a new post-training paradigm that integrates architectural conversion into distillation, avoiding a separate conversion stage while substantially improving student performance and maintaining near-vanilla inference latency.
  </details>

- **[PAMS: Preconditioned Activation-Metric Subspace for Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28476)**  `arXiv:2611.28476`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pradeep Kumar Ragu Chanthar, Saksham Sahai Srivastava, Jaewoo Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
