  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization provides a computationally efficient approach to fine-tuning large language models (LLMs), but conventional methods typically rely on isotropic perturbations that ignore the anisotropic structure of neural-network parameter spaces. We introduce Preconditioned Activation-Metric Subspace (PAMS), a ZO optimization framework that uses forward-pass activation statistics to construct structured, layer-wise perturbation geometries without computing gradients or forming dense curvature matrices. PAMS tracks a low-rank approximation of the dominant activation subspace and its associated spectrum online, and uses the spectrum to softly reweight perturbation variance while preserving exploration in directions outside the retained subspace. A trace normalization separates the perturbation geometry from its overall scale, enabling consistent perturbation magnitudes across layers. We show theoretically that the resulting perturbation covariance induces right-side preconditioning of the gradient of the corresponding Gaussian-smoothed objective. We further characterize the estimator's smoothing bias and second moment and derive a nonconvex convergence guarantee that captures the effects of the perturbation geometry and smoothing radius. The proposed low-rank tracking procedure requires $\mathcal{O}(n_l r)$ persistent memory for a layer with input dimension nl and retained rank r, avoiding the $\mathcal{O}(n_l^2)$ memory required by a dense activation covariance. Experiments on transformer-based language-model fine-tuning tasks across GLUE and SuperGLUE demonstrate that PAMS improves the effectiveness and stability of zeroth-order optimization while retaining its gradient-free and objective-evaluation-based advantages.
  </details>

- **[On-policy distillation as a Backdoor Propagation Channel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19207)**  `arXiv:2611.19207`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zane Fu, Yanzhao Zheng, Ziting Qin, Qianshan Wei, Yi Yu, Gongli Xi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is emerging as an important paradigm for the post training of large language models, improving student models by leveraging supervision generated online by teacher models. However, whether this process also transfers latent security risks from teacher models to student models remains insufficiently studied. In this work, we reveal a new security risk in which an attacker first implants a conditional backdoor into the teacher model through supervised fine tuning (SFT), after which OPD can transfer the backdoor to the student model with only an extremely small proportion of trigger prompt exposure. More concerningly, we find that such backdoor transfer is substantially stronger under OPD than under conventional distillation methods, suggesting that OPD may provide an efficient channel for propagating backdoors across models. Our mechanistic analysis shows that the backdoor signal is primarily transmitted through anomalous differences in the conditional probabilities produced by the teacher model for triggered and clean inputs, and is gradually internalized into the internal representations of the student model during training. Furthermore, we investigate how to suppress such anomalous conditional probability signals at the source of distillation supervision and, based on this analysis, propose an OPD training method tailored to this conditional backdoor propagation setting to mitigate the risk of backdoor transfer induced by untrusted teacher supervision.
  </details>

- **[Advancement or Acceleration? How Data Curation Improves LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36983)**  `arXiv:2610.36983`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaxing Wang, Zicheng Zhang, Deping Xiang, Jin Xu, Yong Li, Chao Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While data curation is widely credited with improving large language model training, a basic question remains unresolved: does curation expand what a model can ultimately learn, or reach the same solution faster? In this paper, we formalize this distinction between advancement (improving the attainable performance) and acceleration (reaching the same performance with less data). Through an analytic characterization and real-world LLM experiments, we show that advancement requires a correctable train-target gap. When the baseline training and target objectives coincide, no curation policy improves the endpoint, and its benefit is acceleration. This reframes advancement as a property of the curation task rather than the curation method. Under a common gap-centric view of data mixture and selection, we operationalize this condition as the $\Delta$-diagnostic, an observable discrepancy between the uncurated baseline's training and target losses that is computable prior to expensive curation. Across extensive experiments, an appreciably nonzero $\Delta$ predicts persistent gains, whereas gains near $\Delta=0$ tend to diminish as the training budget grows. Beyond individual methods, our findings recenter data curation on the task itself and point to target-set construction as a promising direction for shaping what curation can achieve.
  </details>

- **[OpenRFM: Democratizing Frontier Predictive Intelligence on Relational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04320)**  `arXiv:2606.04320`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhikai Chen, Junyu Yin, Jialiang Gu, Siheng Xiong, Xiaoze Liu, Ruowang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relational databases hold most enterprise data, and predicting on them has long required hand-written feature joins and a model trained for each task. Relational foundation models (RFMs) remove this per-task engineering by predicting on an unseen database from a few labelled examples in one forward pass. However, the strongest RFMs are closed, and open RFMs pre-trained on synthetic data lag far behind. Prior work attributes this gap to the lack of real pre-training data. We find that much of this gap comes from an incomplete synthetic prior and from a sparse context. Existing synthetic priors lack history-dependent labels, so models pre-trained on them do not learn the temporal and relational dynamics behind real tasks. A bounded context also often misses the evidence that a task needs. We present OpenRFM, an open recipe for building an RFM from synthetic databases alone, whose data generator, model and training code are all open. OpenRFM has three components. (1) A relational adapter, pre-trained on synthetic event streams with history-dependent labels, encodes the sampled context of each entity into a feature row and performs in-context learning along the relation axis, over the history of the entity and its related entities. (2) A frozen tabular foundation model predicts from labelled support rows and performs in-context learning along the batch axis. (3) An optional harness, which an LLM agent builds for each task, rewrites the schema so that the evidence a task needs enters the bounded context. On 32 benchmark tasks, OpenRFM achieves the best mean rank, 3.00 against 3.63 for RelAgent and 5.34 for KumoRFM-2. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Bilevel Optimization of Synthetic Trajectories for Multi-Turn LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24743)**  `arXiv:2605.24743`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shresth Verma, Mauricio Tec, Cheol Woo Kim, Kai Wang, Milind Tambe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While LLMs excel at single-turn generation, they struggle with long-horizon, multi-turn interactions. Offline reinforcement learning (RL) offers a scalable approach, yet its performance hinges on the availability and quality of multi-turn trajectory data. A common remedy is to augment training with synthetic trajectories generated by LLMs or simulators, but synthetic data is highly heterogeneous in quality, and naively treating all trajectories as equally informative can degrade performance. We propose BOOST, a bilevel optimization framework where the inner level trains the LLM on reweighted data and the outer level trains a lightweight reweighting head on held-out real validation tasks, assigning continuous trajectory-level weights without requiring an external judge. To ground this approach, we derive a PAC-Bayesian bound revealing a three-way trade-off: synthetic data increases diversity but risks task-shift, while concentrating weight on high-quality trajectories improves empirical performance at the cost of effective sample size. Empirically, our method consistently outperforms multiple baselines. Analysis reveals it upweights synthetic trajectories that align with the real data distribution and exhibit higher qualitative merit.
  </details>

- **[The Override Gap: A Magnitude Account of Knowledge Conflict Failure in Hypernetwork-Based Instant LLM Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23750)**  `arXiv:2604.23750`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuaizhi Cheng, Xiang Shi, Zhiwei Zhang, Mingwei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hypernetwork-based methods such as Doc-to-LoRA internalize a document into an LLM's weights in a single forward pass, but struggle when the document contradicts pretraining knowledge. We study this override gap through the competition between the pretrained answer margin and the shift induced by the generated adapter. On 194 Gemma conflicts, baseline accuracy falls from 65.3% in the weakest-prior quartile to 16.3% in the strongest. We introduce two training-free methods: Selective Layer Boosting (SLB), which scales the adapter at its highest-activity layers, and Conflict-Aware Internalization (CA), which chooses between standard and stronger adaptation using a short base-model probe. Strong boosting raises Gemma deep-conflict accuracy from 46.4% to 71.0%, with conflict gains also observed on Mistral-7B and Qwen-4B. Matched-norm controls show that both update magnitude and allocation matter: SLB benefits from the generated layer and direction structure, while global scaling is competitive at the same increment budget. CA offers a workload-dependent recall-latency tradeoff, reaching 98.4% novel recall on Gemma while retaining the strong path's conflict accuracy. KID-Bench provides 486 questions spanning novel recall, mixed knowledge, and prior-graded conflicts, with answer-level semantic evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Agentic AI for Intent Management and Control in Autonomous Communication Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31223)**  `arXiv:2611.31223`  `cs.LG`  
  _Burak Demirel, Yu Wang, Pablo Soldati_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence (AI)-driven automation will help future mobile networks translate high-level network intents into concrete network optimization or control actions. However, existing approaches often focus on intent management and orchestration, leaving the adaptation of underlying network optimization and control functions largely unaddressed. This article proposes a triadic agentic AI framework that co-designs intent management and intent fulfillment through three agents: an interpreter, powered by a language model, that translates and refines intents into machine-readable optimization templates; an optimizer that adapts controller policies to intent specifications; and a controller that performs fast decision-making. We discuss the design space of each agent and how the workflow can be embedded across network architectural domains. Finally, we develop a concrete realization combining a language-model interpreter with Bayesian optimization for the optimizer and multi-objective reinforcement learning for the controller, and evaluate it in a radio resource management case study for differentiated connectivity. Results demonstrate how the agentic AI framework can connect high-level intents with runtime network control, showing adaptability of intent specifications and control policies to service requirements and changing network conditions.
  </details>

- **[Evaluable Language-Model Treatment Policies for Sepsis Resuscitation via Offline Value-Guided Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30951)**  `arXiv:2611.30951`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yue Pei, Shining Bo, Yingying Zhang, Qinggang Ge, Zhiling Zhao, Jian Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline treatment learning must balance imitation, which stays close to logged evidence but ignores outcomes, against value maximisation, which uses outcomes but drifts toward treatments the records cannot verify. Numerical reinforcement learning strikes this balance during training. For large language models (LLMs), directly using sparse retrospective outcomes for fine-tuning raises challenges of delayed credit assignment and limited evidence for alternative treatments. We present R-LLMC, which strikes the balance at inference for sepsis resuscitation. A behaviour-supervised LLM, decoded into an exact distribution over 25 vasopressor-fluid actions, proposes clinician-anchored candidates; an offline critic selects among them by estimated long-term value. The policy has a closed form for off-policy evaluation, and its rationale is written after selection, so explanation cannot steer treatment. In experiments on held-out MIMIC-III patients, R-LLMC exceeded the clinician reference in doubly robust value by +0.214 (95\% CI 0.091-0.300), with fitted Q evaluation agreeing.
  </details>

- **[The KL Divergence Is Never Negative, Except in PyTorch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30835)**  `arXiv:2611.30835`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhen Yang, Wei Tang, Chu Chen, Rui Qian, Yixuan Chen, Fan Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence is never negative, yet PyTorch users have reported negative values for years, and the standard advice is to clamp them at zero. The sign is only the visible part of a silent failure. Many machine-learning applications compare nearby distributions, where conventional KL formulas lose accuracy. On 8.5 million inputs with certified references across 18 closed-form KL families and F.kl_div, we find widespread errors in values and gradients on CPU and GPU. Among scalar families in ordinary parameter ranges, over 85% of FP32 values have at least 1% relative error near KL 10^(-7) (FP16: 10^(-3)), and about 30% become negative at smaller divergences. More precision is not the cure: evaluated one precision higher, the same formulas still leave 7--51% of FP32 values significantly wrong for 22 of 23 configurations. Stable closed-form decompositions, compensated arithmetic, and analytic gradients remove over 97% of significant errors in the working format. Strict repairs eliminate significant errors on 7.2 million scored closed-form samples and correctly round all 567 million scored F.kl_div components. The errors affect parameter fitting, Bayesian decisions, identical-start distillation (up to 97% top-1 disagreement), KL-based layer rankings in a quantized LLM, and 19--59% of GPT-2 circuit edge scores. With the repaired code they all but vanish. Code and data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Near-Far Attention: Region-Aware Sparse Retrieval for Long-Context LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30554)**  `arXiv:2611.30554`  `cs.LG`  
  _YuanShuo Wang, Jianchao Tan, Lei Gong, Teng Wang, Wenqi Lou, Xuehai Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse attention reduces the cost of long-context decoding, but token selection introduces additional scoring and ranking overhead. Moreover, uniform page scoring overlooks differences in relevance between near and far context. We propose Near-Far Attention (NFA), which adapts retrieval to regional attention patterns while keeping candidate scoring lightweight. During prefill, the Lightweight Gate Estimator identifies regions that can be skipped during decoding, adding less than 1% to dense-prefill time. For the remaining regions, the Calibration-Free Subspace Extractor supplies compact features shared by page-level and token-level scoring without offline calibration. The Distance-Aware Two-Level Selector then scores near and far pages differently and jointly ranks candidate tokens under a shared budget. Evaluations across model families, parameter scales, and GQA/MLA architectures show aggregate task quality comparable to full attention. On Qwen3-8B at 512K context length, NFA achieves up to $5.49\times$ operator speedup and $2.97\times$ whole-model decoding speedup over FlashInfer, including selection overhead.
  </details>

- **[Optimistic Online Learning for Coding Agents with Aggregate Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30405)**  `arXiv:2611.30405`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hao Luan, Hao Qiu, Zhi Zheng, See-Kiong Ng, Zhiyong Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) coding agents operating on Web development platforms can interact with repositories, issue trackers, and continuous integration services to carry out long-horizon software engineering tasks. However, behavioral drift remains a major bottleneck in such multi-turn reasoning and action trajectories. To mitigate this, activation steering has emerged as a promising approach for regularizing agent behaviors by intervening on the LLM's transformer residual streams. Nonetheless, existing methods typically apply a static steering policy across all tasks, overlooking the fact that a single policy is rarely effective across heterogeneous tasks and varying execution turns. This limitation motivates dynamic online selection among candidate steering policies. Yet, doing so in long-horizon agentic settings is challenging: verifiable task-level feedback is only revealed upon task completion, leaving the contributions of individual steering turns unobserved. To address this challenge, we formulate online steering-policy selection as a multi-armed bandit problem with delayed task-level feedback and turn-level predictive hints derived from residual-stream activations. Within this formulation, we introduce Online Optimistic Activation Steering, a meta-framework that uses hints to guide within-task steering policy selection and leverages terminal task-level feedback to correct cumulative reward estimates. Theoretically, against the best fixed policy in hindsight, we prove regret bounds that tighten with increasing hint accuracy and establish a matching lower bound with respect to prediction error and candidate policy count. Empirical evaluations on realistic coding benchmarks demonstrate that our framework outperforms both static steering policies and naive online adaptive baselines.
  </details>

- **[Learning Teacher Output Preferences from Final Responses for Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30338)**  `arXiv:2611.30338`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hongli Shen, Shaopeng Fu, Qinbo Zhang, Jian Li, Di Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many large language models (LLMs) use chain-of-thought (CoT) to solve complex problems, but hide their CoT traces to prevent reasoning capabilities from being distilled. While recent studies have shown that hidden CoTs can be reconstructed, existing methods typically require training a separate attacker for each target LLM. This paper studies model-agnostic CoT reconstruction, aiming to reconstruct hidden CoT traces from arbitrary target LLMs using only input prompts and visible final responses. We propose Final-Output Preference Learning (FOPL), which trains a single reconstruction model that generalizes to unseen LLMs without target-specific adaptation. To achieve this, the reconstruction model must learn which reasoning patterns match a given final response without knowing the target LLM's identity. We address this using preference optimization, training the reconstruction model to prefer native CoTs over synthetic alternatives for the same question and final response. By learning these preferences from LLMs with accessible CoTs, the reconstruction model learns to distinguish reasoning patterns based on visible responses. Experiments show that reconstructed CoTs capture distinct reasoning patterns across LLM families, including a family unseen during training. Moreover, students trained on reconstructed CoTs match or outperform those trained on native CoTs across several reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[AdaptRouter: Adaptive Routing across Evolving Tasks and Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30162)**  `arXiv:2611.30162`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jingpu Yang, Fengxian Ji, Tianhao Li, Juanfan Wu, Yufeng Wang, Shuxin Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model routing assigns each query to a suitable foundation model, but tasks and available models keep changing, and existing routers are either untrained for the pool at hand or fit once and forget earlier tasks when updated. We present AdaptRouter, an adaptive router trained by policy learning: a compact multimodal decision model reads each question with cards describing the current experts and learns from native task feedback, without routing labels, to select one frozen expert. Open-weight and API experts share the router, its inputs, and the quality-cost trade-off but use different losses: cheap open-weight experts are explored with RL, whereas paid API experts are queried once and their cached feedback is reused as soft labels. New tasks are learned without replay, new experts enter as cards, and an open-weight quality-first advantage provably keeps cost from overriding quality. Across four task stages, AdaptRouter loses at most 0.4 points on earlier tasks, whereas feedback-trained routers lose up to 7.0, and as its pool grows from three to ten experts, its accuracy rises from 67.2% to 72.1%. On five API models, it exceeds GPT-6.1 Sol and GPT-6 Astra (low) by over 22 points at under a third of their cost.
  </details>

- **[STMemoLLM: A Spatial-Temporal Memory-Augmented LLM for Robust Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30148)**  `arXiv:2611.30148`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jian Zhou, Binqing Wu, Zongjiang Shang, Wanghui Qiu, Dongliang Cui, Chenxi Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic forecasting is essential for intelligent transportation systems. However, traditional deep-learning models often rely heavily on high-quality observations. Recent LLM-based approaches incorporate pretrained knowledge to improve forecasting, but they remain sensitive to input perturbations and struggle to preserve long-term patterns. To address this, we propose STMemoLLM, a spatial-temporal memory-augmented LLM for robust traffic forecasting. STMemoLLM introduces memory prototypes to compress inputs into compact summary tokens while extracting reliable dynamics from residual signals, achieving a balance between stability and variability. Specifically, a memory-enhanced tokenizer encodes prototypes and sensor characteristics into attention biases to guide adaptive compression of correlated sensors into semantic summary tokens. A memory-calibrated router further evaluates contextual deviation, compression deficiency, and prototype alignment to identify reliable residual signals, which complement the summaries while suppressing noise. Extensive experiments on eight real-world datasets show that STMemoLLM achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional experiments examine point masking, additive Gaussian noise, structured missingness, and limited training data, demonstrating strong robustness. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LAF-ECG: Multi-Resolution Lead-Aware Fusion of Time-Series and Language Foundation Models for ECG Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29721)**  `arXiv:2611.29721`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kiana Taktehrani, Maryam Hosseini, Amir Keynia, Aram Montazami, Arash Mohammadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated electrocardiogram (ECG) interpretation has advanced substantially with deep learning, yet many methods remain task-specific and label-dependent. Large language models (LLMs) and time-series foundation models (TSFMs) offer complementary pretrained representations, but integrating them is challenging because of mismatched feature spaces and the lead- and scale-dependent structure of ECG signals. We propose LAF-ECG (Lead-Aware Foundation Fusion for ECG), a dual-foundation framework that extends MedTsLLM with a lead-aware interface to a frozen TSFM backbone while retaining its reprogramming pathway. The proposed framework captures multi-resolution temporal context, fuses temporal and language-aligned ECG representations through adaptive residual integration, and employs branch-specific supervision with cross-branch alignment. LAF-ECG achieves 71.10% accuracy and 62.43% macro-F1 on PTB-XL, and 89.73% accuracy and 65.94% macro-F1 on Chapman-Shaoxing, outperforming MedTsLLM on both datasets. These results highlight the value of preserving lead-specific temporal structure when combining temporal and language foundation representations for ECG classification. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From States to Dynamics: Detecting Rule Violations in OthelloGPT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29715)**  `arXiv:2611.29715`  `cs.LG`  
  _David L. Cao, Sai Sumedh R. Hindupur, Demba Ba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sequence models trained on game moves have been shown to encode world models, with structured linear probes revealing the underlying game states. World models, however, are inherently dynamic: in deterministic board games, a set of rules constrain how states evolve over time. We therefore ask if and how OthelloGPT, a transformer trained only on Othello move sequences, manifests the rules of the game in its activation space. We divide the rules of Othello, and symmetrically their violations, into three classes: placing a disc on an empty square (R1), flanking an opponent's disc (R2), and moving in turn (R3). We find that linear probes accurately separate legal from illegal moves, and also distinguish between the three kinds of rule violations. We introduce an unsupervised rule-violation detector based on the step length of activations between consecutive moves, which identifies illegal moves above chance. We further improve our detection metric by computing step length in the latent space of select sparse autoencoders. Together, we find that OthelloGPT follows game rules of Othello to a large extent, and that we can detect rule violations using structured detectors on model activations.
  </details>

- **[Exact on Paper: Which LLM Serving Shortcuts Flip Graded Answers, Measured Against the Noise of Reordering the Same Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29663)**  `arXiv:2611.29663`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sharven Rane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Teams enable serving features such as prefix caching, chunked prefill and speculative decoding after checking that benchmark accuracy is unchanged, because the engine documents them as exact. Accuracy is an aggregate, so a feature can hold it while flipping which requests are answered correctly. We serve one 4B instruction model in vLLM under nine single feature configurations and five replicas of a reference configuration, grade 3,380 requests from five benchmarks in every run, and compare each candidate with a restart floor (fresh process) and a scheduling floor (same requests in a different order). With default kernels every configuration keeps accuracy within one point, and the seven exact features flip the graded outcome of 1.47% (95% CI 1.13 to 1.85) to 1.78% (1.39 to 2.20) of requests, none above the scheduling floor of 1.72%, while the FP8 KV cache flips 3.36% (2.80 to 3.96), as many as FP8 weights. With batch invariant kernels all seven exact features, speculative decoding included, reproduce the reference text on every request, while the FP8 KV cache still flips 3.91% (3.28 to 4.53) of graded outcomes. A gate that compares against a single replica pair rejected an unchanged configuration, so we recommend certifying a serving change under batch invariant kernels, where the floor is zero, or against a scheduling floor measured from at least three replicas.
  </details>

- **[BenchMint: From Real-World Tasks to Executable Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29367)**  `arXiv:2611.29367`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zining Huang, Zuo Wang, Mengyao Zhang, Yilan Piao, Zhe Wang, Shuohan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) need evaluations of everyday reasoning and tool use, such as planning itineraries under user constraints and grounding numerical answers in evidence. We introduce BenchMint, an automated benchmark-construction workflow, and Daily-Life, its collection of 100 selected tasks across 11 real-world domains. BenchMint jointly constructs tasks and scoring criteria from the same source documents. Language-model judges assess possible answers against these criteria using tool traces and private evidence. We report 26 model/harness configurations, including GPT, Claude and Gemini families; 17 have complete domain profiles. These are descriptive configuration results, not a controlled model ranking. In one selected travel task, we corrected the reference total from 320 to 380 minutes while keeping both answers unchanged. The incorrect answer's arithmetic verdict switched from pass to fail; the correct answer's switched from fail to pass. With one ballot per cell, this exposes a reference error without establishing repeatability or collection-wide validity. Incomplete construction comparisons establish no general advantage of joint authoring or feedback-guided revision.
  </details>

- **[Compared to What? Calibrating Machine-Unlearning Diagnostics with Present, Absent and Suppressed Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29285)**  `arXiv:2611.29285`  `cs.LG`  
  _Arsalan Kaleem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlearning methods for language models are judged mainly by behaviour, but a model can stop producing an answer while still encoding it. Internal diagnostics such as linear probes, the logit lens and relearning speed are used to tell removal from suppression, yet none has been checked against cases where the answer is known. We build such cases. A present anchor learned a set of synthetic facts; an absent anchor is a never-taught oracle retrained without them; and suppressed anchors are unlearned with their lower layers frozen, so the facts stay encoded below the frozen block by construction. We compare these anchors with NPO, RMU and gradient difference at a matched operating point on GPT-2 Small and Qwen3-0.6B-Base, scoring every diagnostic per entity with AUROC against the oracle. Behaviour cannot separate the suppressed anchors from the oracle (AUROC 0.51 to 0.58), while probes, the logit lens and relearning gain can (0.65 to 1.00), and the oracle seeds stay near 0.5. Applied to NPO on both models and RMU on Qwen3, the validated diagnostics disagree: probes put the facts at the oracle's level, while the models still relearn them much faster than a never-taught model does. Every unlearned model, anchor or method, also assigns the forgotten answer a far lower log-probability than the oracle does, a black-box signal that flagged all of them. A drift-ratio specificity rule fails on known cases. Code, data and every run are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AgriManager: A Framework and Benchmark for LLM-RL Generalization in Agricultural Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28844)**  `arXiv:2611.28844`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chi Gui, Junwen Zheng, Ritwik Nigam, Qianlan Yang, Meagan Lang, Vardhan Dongre, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agricultural management is heterogeneous along two axes. The first is distributional shift within a fixed task interface: weather, crops, and prices vary across years and locations while sensors, actions, and objective stay fixed. The second is structural shift across interfaces: different farms expose different observed variables, available actions, and management objectives. We formalize the second axis as schema shift: variation in the policy-facing schema $\Sigma = (\Sigma_O, \Sigma_A, \Sigma_R)$ between training and deployment. We test whether LLM-RL generalizes across both axes with AgriManager, to our knowledge the first framework that trains LLM policies with RL across multiple process-based crop simulators, unifying DSSAT, WOFOST, and Cycles. We design a three-tier generalization ladder: Tier 1 within-schema distributional shift, Tier 2 single-axis schema shift along $\Sigma_O / \Sigma_A / \Sigma_R$, Tier 3 compositional schema shift across simulators, all scored on a frozen held-out test suite with three training seeds per method. Within fixed schemas, Qwen3-4B trained by RL with explicit reasoning exceeds neural-network PPO trained with the same interaction budget; without reasoning, training stalls near no-action where the reward penalizes input use. Under single-axis schema shift, $\Sigma_O$ transfer needs decision-critical state, $\Sigma_A$ results are consistent with joint action-menu coverage, and with reasoning a policy trained on yield alone transfers to unseen objectives. Under compositional schema shift, single-source training mostly hurts zero-shot transfer across simulators, and a unified policy trained only on other combinations of the same components recovers about 86% of the gain of a specialist trained on the target combination on DSSAT and stays below the specialists on the WOFOST and Cycles profit targets. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Pixel Auto-Encoder: Reconstructing Language Model Activations Through Pixel Art](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28822)**  `arXiv:2611.28822`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haru Furuichi, Phan Xuan Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpreting the high-dimensional internal representations of large language models (LLMs) remains a challenging problem. Recent approaches have explored translating these representations into human-understandable forms, particularly natural language. In this work, we explore the use of images as an alternative target modality for interpreting LLM activations. As a first step toward this goal, we introduce Pixel Auto-Encoder, which converts intermediate LLM activations into $32 \times 32$, 32-color pixel art and reconstructs them for subsequent computation within the model. By encouraging the intermediate representations to resemble predefined concept images, we investigate whether LLM activations can be expressed visually while preserving the model's language capabilities. We find that the model can produce target-aligned images for some concepts, and that replacing pixel representations with concept images can influence next-token predictions. However, we also observe a trade-off between visual alignment and language-model performance, as well as sensitivity to pixel-level changes that are difficult to interpret visually. These findings demonstrate the feasibility and limitations of image-based representations of LLM activations.
  </details>

- **[Synthetic EHR Generators Should Be Audited Like LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28804)**  `arXiv:2611.28804`  `cs.LG`  
  _Inigo Stibbe, Padmanabhan Ramnarayan, A. Aldo Faisal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic electronic health record (EHR) generation is increasingly adopting large autoregressive models, yet privacy evaluation has not kept pace, relying heavily on similarity-based heuristics and aggregate metrics that can obscure patient-specific memorisation. We argue that these models should additionally undergo LLM-style memorisation audits at the level of individual patient records. We support this position by comparing membership-inference evaluations across four prominent autoregressive EHR generators, highlighting inconsistent evaluation practices in patient representations and attack procedures. We evaluate trajectory reconstruction from partial context and introduce randomly assigned patient-level canaries to distinguish memorised associations from clinical generalisation. We examine these methods in a GPT-2-style model deliberately overfit to 1,000 MIMIC-IV patient trajectories, creating a controlled setting in which memorisation should be readily detectable. The model systematically recovers training patients' canaries, and their trajectory reconstruction approaches perfect accuracy with increasing context. Held-out patients show no systematic canary recovery, while reconstruction remains low and improves only marginally with additional context. These experiments establish a positive-control baseline for further evaluation of these methods in realistically trained models.
  </details>

- **[CLAIR: Consistent LLM Adaptation via Index-free Reprogramming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28721)**  `arXiv:2611.28721`  `cs.LG`  
  _Dongkuk Kim, Jungkyu Shin, Yoonhyuk Choi, Jong Wook Kim, Beakcheol Jang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series forecasting with pretrained large language models (LLMs) uses contextualized patch representations, raising the question of how to align them. Matching patches at the same temporal position is natural, but self-attention allows each patch state to incorporate information from elsewhere in the input window, suggesting that useful correspondences can extend across positions. This flexibility, however, does not necessarily translate into consistent forecasts: similar patch states may play different roles when used in their original temporal order. To address both issues, we propose CLAIR (Consistent LLM Adaptation via Index-free Reprogramming), a teacher-guided framework that combines patch-level transport alignment (PTA) and forecast-level consistency (FC). A clean-view exponential moving average (EMA) teacher guides a mildly perturbed student, with PTA establishing soft, representation-dependent correspondences and FC encouraging agreement at corresponding future steps. The forecasting head retains the original patch order. We test this combination through controlled comparisons: while PTA and index-wise alignment perform similarly without FC, PTA+FC under EMA supervision reduces horizon-averaged mean squared error (MSE) by 2.1% on ETTh1 and 2.6% on ETTm1 relative to Index+FC. The same preference for PTA+FC emerges in comparisons with alternative matching strategies on Weather and ETTh2. Beyond these controls, CLAIR achieves the lowest horizon-averaged MSE on five of six long-term benchmarks and the lowest aggregate overall weighted average (OWA) on M4 among evaluated methods, with competitive few-shot performance. As the teacher and transport are used only during training, inference requires only the student.
  </details>

- **[Interpreting Diffusion Model Activations in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28621)**  `arXiv:2611.28621`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yukinori Yamamoto, Kotaro Yoshida, Ryotaro Shimizu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpreting the intermediate activations of diffusion models (DMs) is key to understanding how they generate images. Existing interpretability methods for DMs provide limited support for open-ended interrogation, making it difficult to extract human-interpretable insights. We extend LatentQA, the task of answering natural-language questions about Large Language Model activations, to DM activations. We introduce the Diffusion-specific Activation Oracle (DAO), which translates DM activations into natural-language answers to open-ended questions about the ongoing generation. Our experiments show that DAO can effectively describe the content of the final image from activations at early denoising steps. Additionally, we demonstrate that DAO captures the structure of the DM's activation space well enough to steer image generation toward target attributes. Furthermore, we show that attribution patching through DAO identifies DM components that contribute to visual attributes specified in natural language. Our code is available \href{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[When a Permitted Action Completes a Forbidden Workflow: Designing Consequence-Oriented Approval for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28454)**  `arXiv:2611.28454`  `cs.LG`  
  _S M Asif Hossain, Ruksat Khan Shayoni, Md Kishor Morol, M F Mridha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language-model agents increasingly ask people to approve consequential actions, yet an approval prompt shows only the next step. A request to send a file to an approved recipient can look harmless even when that file was assembled, several steps earlier, from data the user said must never leave. We call this a cumulative authorization failure and argue that it is an interaction design problem as much as a security problem. The evidence a reviewer needs is spread across the trajectory, and listing it in time order leaves the reviewer to reconstruct the causal chain. We present the Cumulative Consent Ledger (CCL), an approval interface built on four design principles that restate the user's intent, bind the review to the exact action, compose the cumulative consequence, and keep every claim checkable. A trusted monitor that tracks artifact lineage supplies the evidence. In a between-subjects study with 80 participants and 960 approval decisions, CCL was associated with fewer unsafe approvals than an evidence-matched chronological log (24.2% vs. 32.5%), higher comprehension, and faster decisions, although the adjusted difference in unsafe approvals remained uncertain (-7.9 points, 95% CrI [-19.7, 3.8]). In a system evaluation, the monitor detected 89.2% of 120 violating checkpoints and, used as an execution gate, reduced executed unsafe effects from 43.3% to 5.0% for one agent at a small cost to benign completion. We derive implications for designing consent around consequences rather than individual calls.
  </details>

- **[Separating Interpretability and Reconstruction in Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25165)**  `arXiv:2611.25165`  `cs.LG`  
  _Anh Tien Nguyen, Dimitris Samaras, Mahdi S. Hosseini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) are effective tools for mechanistic interpretability by decomposing neural representations into sparse and interpretable features. However, a single sparse representation in traditional SAEs is responsible for both reconstruction fidelity and interpretability, which are not guaranteed to align. To separate these objectives, we propose DualSAE, a modular framework that augments standard SAEs with a plug-in Residual Branch. A standard SAE serves as the Sparse Branch, learning a sparse code that contains the dominant semantics of the input. The added Residual Branch models the non-semantic information omitted by the Sparse Branch. We ensure that the contributions of the two branches towards the overall reconstruction have minimal overlap through a decorrelation loss. We also adopt a two-stage training strategy that first stabilizes sparse representation learning before coupling with the Residual Branch. Across diverse encoder-SAE configurations on two vision datasets and two language corpora, DualSAE achieves mean relative improvements of approximately 29% in reconstruction fidelity and 34% in interpretability (measured by monosemanticity score). Moreover, with more interpretable representations, DualSAE yields consistent performance gains in the downstream applications of topic modeling, hypothesis generation, and LLM steering. Qualitative analysis further suggests that the Sparse Branch indeed preserves dominant semantic content, whereas the Residual Branch captures non-semantic information to improve reconstruction fidelity.
  </details>

- **[Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation: Evaluating Hierarchical Policies from Non-Hierarchical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10543)**  `arXiv:2611.10543`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunfu Deng, Josiah P. Hanna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-policy evaluation (OPE) estimates the expected return of a target policy from previously collected data without additional environment interaction. While OPE methods for flat Markovian policies are well studied, little work has addressed evaluating hierarchical policies in which a high-level policy selects temporally extended options that generate primitive actions until a termination condition is met. The primary existing approach applies per-decision importance sampling at the option level, but requires option-annotated trajectories and exhibits variance that still grows with the horizon. For non-hierarchical policies, fitted Q-evaluation (FQE) typically achieves lower error than importance sampling by leveraging the classic Bellman equation for policy evaluation; however, a naive extension of FQE to hierarchical policies results in a biased policy value estimate. In this paper, we introduce Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation (IO-FQE), which estimates a value function in the augmented state-option space; this approach enables OPE of hierarchical policies even when we lack annotations of what option was run in the data. We develop two continuous-action instantiations of IO-FQE and show on long-horizon hierarchical continuous control tasks (AntMaze navigation and OGBench Puzzle manipulation) that IO-FQE eliminates the bias incurred by a naive application of FQE to hierarchical policies and substantially lowers mean squared-error compared to option-level importance sampling.
  </details>

- **[TripletAD: Weakly Supervised Anomaly Detection in Embedding Space with Rare Anomalies and Scarce Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10352)**  `arXiv:2611.10352`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sumit Wadhwa, Souvik Nath_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI and agentic systems automate more processes with less human review, data is increasingly represented, and passed between systems, as embeddings produced by pre-trained models. Detecting rare deviations in these embeddings is becoming a critical guardrail, yet anomaly detection methods were largely developed for hand-built tabular features, where gradient-boosted trees remain the method of choice. We propose TripletAD, a weakly supervised detector for embedding spaces in which anomalies are rare. TripletAD learns a triplet-margin embedding in which every triplet contains an anomaly, so the few anomalies are reused across the entire training set instead of being diluted by it, and calibrates a linear scoring head with a partial cross-entropy on anomalies and a relative-contrast hinge that separates the mean scores of anomalous and normal samples. Across 28 embedding datasets spanning text, images, speech and industrial inspection, TripletAD is consistently among the best-performing methods, matching or exceeding established state-of-the-art weakly supervised detectors (DevNet, PReNet and DeepSAD), supervised contrastive learning and gradient-boosted trees, and it outperforms them when anomalies are rare. On naturally rare speech data it is 43% above the strongest baseline and more than twice the established detectors; at a 1% anomaly rate it has the highest mean AUCPR of seven methods, 20% above DevNet; and pooled over 74 rare-anomaly settings it has the best average rank, with statistically significant gains over every baseline. In a case study on AI-agent traces, it locates the failing step more often than all six baselines, with accuracy on par with or above that reported for frontier LLM judges, at 0.17 ms per step. The method needs no per-dataset tuning and is insensitive to its loss weights and training length.
  </details>

- **[LatentWeave: Test-Time Latent Optimization for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39557)**  `arXiv:2610.39557`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fanci Meng, Kun Wang, Guibin Zhang, Xinlei Yu, Jiaming Zhang, Ming Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in test-time optimization have shown that the multimodal reasoning performance of large multimodal language models (MLLMs) can be improved during inference without modifying backbone model parameters. However, existing approaches primarily operate at the prompt or output level, leaving the latent reasoning process largely uncontrolled and insufficient to prevent error accumulation during autoregressive generation. To address this limitation, we propose LatentWeave, a test-time framework that treats multimodal reasoning as a controllable latent trajectory. LatentWeave performs structured Latent Weaving, progressively refining intermediate hidden states segment-wise to correct emerging deviations, and further introduces Reasoning Anchors via lightweight WEAVE tokens to stabilize reasoning structure and guide answer-oriented generation. These mechanisms are designed to enable fine-grained, step-aware intervention in the latent reasoning process while keeping the main MLLM backbone parameters fixed. Extensive experiments across diverse multimodal reasoning benchmarks report improvements over the evaluated baselines; the contribution of latent optimization, anchor training, and additional test-time computation is analyzed separately in the ablation and efficiency studies.
  </details>

- **[Muon Sublates the Edge of Stability in LLM Pretraining: What Persists and What Changes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34915)**  `arXiv:2609.34915`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yanzhe Chen, Qifang Zhao, Xiaoxiao Xu, Fanghui Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon is increasingly used for language-model pretraining, yet its large-step dynamics are not captured by the classical edge-of-stability (EoS) picture of gradient descent (GD). We study stochastic (no-momentum) Muon through theoretical analysis, controlled networks, and language models from 22M to 1B parameters, distinguishing three diagnostics: generalized sharpness, loss balance, and update alignment. We theoretically and empirically demonstrate that loss balance differs from its generalized sharpness and cannot determine update alignment across various learning rates in 130M pre-training as well as 1B runs. Besides, our experiments also show that increasing the batch size encourages update alignment towards to total reversal. Furthermore, checkpoint interventions in the 22M and 130M models show that increasing the learning rate can initially raise the loss but a lower validation loss at the end of the prescribed warmup-stable-decay continuation. These results support a split EoS picture: generalized-sharpness saturation persists in the measured runs, but loss balance and update geometry require separate characterization, allowing for learning-rate schedules design. The source code for reproducing the experiments can be found inthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Surrogate Fidelity: When Can Open LLMs Explain Closed Ones?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.32008)**  `arXiv:2606.32008`  `cs.LG`  
  _Philippe Chlenski, Zachariah Carmichael, Ayush Warikoo, Chia-Tse Shao, Yingxiao Ye, Aobo Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mechanistic interpretability (MI) requires full access to model internals, yet the APIs for most widely deployed language models at best expose log-probabilities over output tokens. This creates a surrogate problem: when do measurements made on open models allow us to make claims about a closed model? We evaluate *surrogate fidelity* at the prediction, attribution, and mechanistic levels. For classification tasks, log-odds provide an API-compatible scalar readout of the model's representation space, and leave-one-out attributions provide insight into model behavior. Across eleven models spanning four families (Llama, Qwen, GPT, and Gemini), pooled prediction fidelity substantially exceeds attribution fidelity: models that agree on *what* the answer is often disagree on *why*. This gap narrows when conditioning on the ground-truth answer, even reversing when both models answer correctly, suggesting pooled prediction fidelity partly reflects agreement on answers and not fine-grained confidence. We document an *access-validity inversion:* white-box signals like attention patterns are highly stable across models but only weakly predictive of causal attributions, which black-box input ablations capture by design. Code and results are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Understanding the Power and Limits of the Muon Optimizer: A River-Valley Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21514)**  `arXiv:2606.21514`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianqi Shen, Runze Shi, Jinji Yang, Jianhao Ma, Jiaye Teng, Ziye Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Momentum Orthogonalized by Newton-Schulz (Muon) has emerged as a promising optimizer for large language model (LLM) training, yet when and why it outperforms established alternatives such as AdamW remain insufficiently understood. This gap matters in practice, as a sub-optimal choice of optimizer or even hyper-parameters can slow progress throughout expensive pre-training and waste substantial computational resources. Existing theories largely adopt a local-improvement perspective, emphasizing singular-value flattening or implicit curvature adaptation, but they neither determine which optimizer is preferable nor explain why Muon is typically fast only during early training. To better explain Muon's complicated behavior over the entire training trajectory, we develop a trajectory-level characterization of Muon dynamics based on a new mixed-spectrum matrix sensing (MSMS) model motivated by the river-valley view of LLM loss landscapes. Under simplifying assumptions, our continuous-time analysis reveals a qualitatively different convergence mechanism: Muon advances piecewise linearly toward the solution, whereas gradient descent and Adam-like flows approach it asymptotically along curved paths. With finite step sizes, however, the same normalized update magnitude makes Muon prone to overshooting near the solution, so its admissible step size shrinks with the residual scale. This links Muon's early advantage and late-stage limitations to a common mechanism and explains its sensitivity to learning-rate scheduling. Language model pre-training experiments support our discovery and suggest that switching from Muon to AdamW can preserve rapid initial progress while improving late-stage refinement.
  </details>

- **[ZEBRA: Zero-shot Budgeted Resource Allocation for LLM Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20485)**  `arXiv:2605.20485`  `cs.LG`  
  _May Hamri, Inbal Talgam-Cohen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As autonomous agents increasingly execute end-to-end tasks under fixed monetary budgets, the pressing open question shifts from whether the budget is respected, to how to spend it effectively. Existing budget-aware methods typically control reasoning step-by-step within a single agent, or learn resource allocation policies via RL. None address how to split a budget across the composing phases of a multi-agent pipeline at inference time. We propose ZEBRA, a zero-shot framework that reduces multi-phase budget allocation to a continuous nonlinear knapsack problem: an LLM controller estimates per-phase utility curves, and a water-filling search on the Lagrange multiplier returns the per-phase split. Additive and multiplicative aggregations are unified under the same solver. On a 150-task APPS coding benchmark, both ZEBRA variants outperform LLM-direct (budget allocation directly by an LLM) on every aggregate metric. At a budget of $\alpha = 0.5$ of the unconstrained spend, ZEBRA recovers 94.4% of unconstrained quality, versus 88.1% for LLM-direct. The advantage is statistically significant and transfers beyond coding: on a 3-phase HotpotQA pipeline, ZEBRA beats LLM-direct by 9.5pp, with allocations empirically robust to curve-estimation noise. On HotpotQA, ZEBRA arrives at a different budget split (near-balanced) compared to the APPS one (skewed towards a refinement phase), showing adaptation to the pipeline structure. More broadly, we show that lightweight algorithmic guidance at inference time can improve the economic behavior of autonomous multi-agent systems.
  </details>

- **[Visual Latents Know More Than They Say: Unsilencing Latent Reasoning in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02735)**  `arXiv:2605.02735`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xin Zhang, Qiqi Tao, Jiawei Du, Moyun Liu, Joey Tianyi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous latent-space reasoning offers a compact alternative to textual chain-of-thought for multimodal models, enabling high-dimensional visual evidence to be integrated without explicit reasoning tokens. However, we identify a previously overlooked optimization pathology in existing latent visual reasoning methods: although visual latents become semantically enriched during training, their contribution to final answer prediction is systematically suppressed. Within the shared parameter space, the autoregressive objective favors shortcut reliance on direct visual input, driving latent tokens toward transition-like states rather than informative reasoning content. We term this phenomenon Silenced Visual Latents. To address it, we disentangle the two conflicting objectives by directly optimizing the latent reasoning at inference time, keeping backbone parameters frozen. In Stage I, visual latents are warmed up via query-guided contrastive latent--visual alignment, improving semantic quality while preventing latent collapse. In Stage II, the latent reasoning is further optimized via a confidence-progression reward, which incentivizes predicted token distributions along the latent span to become progressively more concentrated, routing predictions through the latent reasoning rather than bypassing it. Experiments across eight benchmarks and four model backbones show that inference-time latent optimization, without any parameter updates, effectively unleashes the suppressed reasoning capacity of visual latents.
  </details>

- **[When and Why Does Multi-Agent Debate Fail and Does It Really Underperform?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20963)**  `arXiv:2510.20963`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yongqiang Chen, Gang Niu, James Cheng, Bo Han, Masashi Sugiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent debate (MAD) was proposed as a promising approach for ensembling the wisdom of multiple large language models (LLMs) to improve reasoning and provide effective supervision to superhuman LLMs. However, increasing empirical evidence suggests that MAD may not outperform or even underperform single-agent approaches (SA), raising doubts about the benefits of MAD. In this work, we investigate this issue by analyzing the incentive structures of popular MAD paradigms: (i) competitive MAD (CopMAD) where agents compete by holding opposing positions; (ii) consensus-seeking MAD (CosMAD) where agents are driven to seek consensus. We show that both paradigms suffer from debate hacking: CopMAD reduces to a cheap-talk game, where agents produce misleading messages to win the game, while CosMAD filters out informative disagreements for premature consensus. Consequently, agents in both CopMAD and CosMAD fail to jointly resolve the ambiguity and seek the truth. To this end, we introduce ColMAD, a collaborative protocol that reframes MAD as a non-zero-sum game to encourage agents to provide informative while truthful messages. Through extensive benchmarking on challenging tasks such as error detection, we show that ColMAD consistently outperforms previous MAD protocols under multi-seed evaluation across heterogeneous LLM pairs. Under the same budgets, ColMAD effectively brings non-trivial improvements over SA methods, implying that the protocol design is critical to realizing the potential of MAD. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Improving constraint-based discovery with robust propagation and reliable LLM priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23570)**  `arXiv:2509.23570`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ruiqi Lyu, Alistair Turcan, Martin Jinye Zhang, Bryan Wilder_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning causal structure from observational data is central to scientific modeling and decision-making. Constraint-based methods aim to recover conditional independence (CI) relations in a causal directed acyclic graph (DAG). Classical approaches such as PC and subsequent methods orient v-structures first and then propagate edge directions from these seeds, assuming perfect CI tests and exhaustive search of separating subsets -- assumptions often violated in practice, leading to cascading errors in the final graph. Recent work has explored using large language models (LLMs) as experts, prompting sets of nodes for edge directions, and could augment edge orientation when assumptions are not met. However, such methods implicitly assume perfect experts, which is unrealistic for hallucination-prone LLMs. We propose MosaCD, a causal discovery method that propagates edges from a high-confidence set of seeds derived from both CI tests and LLM annotations. To filter hallucinations, we introduce shuffled queries that exploit LLMs' positional bias, retaining only high-confidence seeds. We then apply a novel confidence-down propagation strategy that orients the most reliable edges first, and can be integrated with any skeleton-based discovery method. Across multiple real-world graphs, MosaCD achieves higher accuracy in final graph construction than existing constraint-based methods, largely due to the improved reliability of initial seeds and robust propagation strategies.
  </details>

- **[Unlearning That Lasts: Utility-Preserving, Robust, and Almost Irreversible Forgetting in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02820)**  `arXiv:2509.02820`  `cs.LG`  
  _Naman Deep Singh, Maximilian Müller, Amit Peleg, Francesco Croce, Matthias Hein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlearning in large language models (LLMs) requires precisely removing specific information from a pre-trained model, e.g. to delete private data or harmful knowledge acquired during pre-training. However, existing unlearning methods often fall short under thorough evaluation. This paper makes three contributions. First, we introduce JensUn, a novel unlearning approach which leverages the Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) as the training objective for both forget and retain sets. We provide theoretical arguments why JSD outperforms KL-Divergence, and show in extensive experiments that JensUn achieves the best forget-utility trade-off and demonstrates strong resilience to benign relearning, outperforming existing methods. Second, for a precise unlearning evaluation, we introduce LPF, a curated dataset of less prominent facts that provides a realistic unlearning scenario. Third, to comprehensively test unlearning methods, we $\textit{(i)}$ employ an LLM as semantic judge instead of the standard ROUGE score, and $\textit{(ii)}$ evaluate worst-case unlearning over various paraphrases and input formats. Our improved evaluation reveals that many unlearning methods are less effective than previously thought.
  </details>

- **[GlanceNav: Efficient Vision-and-Language Navigation with Streaming Memory and Counterfactual Rewind](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30900)**  `arXiv:2611.30900`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Anh Dao, Quan-Dung Pham, Danh Vinh Le, Tuyen Pham Le, Quan Nguyen, Yuzhang Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong policies for vision-and-language navigation (VLN). However, most of them encode a sample of past frames again at every decision and are trained on isolated decisions, so computation is redundant and the usable history is short. They also learn from their own mistakes through outcome rewards that give one scalar to a whole episode. We ask whether a navigator can encode each frame only once, at a constant cost per step, and still remember what matters and learn efficiently from its mistakes. We present GlanceNav, a streaming navigator built on a hybrid MLLM. Its Gated DeltaNet layers carry a recurrent state through the whole episode, while its attention layers keep a short window of recent observations and a small bank of keyframes, selected by how much each frame changes the recurrent state. This constant-size memory keeps the cost per step fixed and allows training on whole episodes with the same memory as at inference. Because the entire memory can also be copied in constant time, we further propose Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), a reinforcement learning stage that rewinds a drifting rollout to its closest approach to the goal, lets an expert correct a few actions, and credits decisions by the resulting change in outcome. Trained only on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE training splits, with 1,000 instructions for reinforcement learning, a 4B GlanceNav reaches success rates of 71.9% and 73.6% on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen. On R2R-CE, it outperforms the strongest previous single-RGB method trained without external data by 9.1 points, while running at 104 ms per step.
  </details>

- **[TowerBridge: Bridging the Dual-Encoder Modality Gap via Weight-Space Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31233)**  `arXiv:2611.31233`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Hani Alomari, Md Atabuzzaman, Chris Thomas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CLIP-style dual encoders map images and text through two separate towers into a shared embedding space, where the two modalities occupy distinct regions separated by a modality gap. Retrieval fails when wrong matches, or hard negatives, outrank the correct one, and the gap is the usual suspect. Yet removing it post hoc degrades retrieval on every CLIP checkpoint we test. We introduce a paired statistic that identifies directions where the two towers agree more on hard negatives than on matched pairs. Mildly attenuating these directions improves retrieval while largely preserving the gap. We arrive at this correction through weight-space programming: guided by the statistic, an LLM agent inspects both towers and writes closed-form weight edits without backpropagation. Free to edit any component, the agent improves COCO test retrieval on all 13 CLIP checkpoints and edits an output projection in 12 of the 13. We distill and refine its recurring correction into TowerBridge, a closed-form recipe motivated by a first-order analysis of the hard-negative margin. Computed in seconds from 25,000 labelled pairs, it requires no model-specific tuning and leaves the architecture and inference cost unchanged. TowerBridge improves COCO test retrieval by 16.8 RSUM and Flickr30k test retrieval by 6.6 on average across all 21 evaluated CLIP checkpoints, including 8 unseen by the agent, while limiting any drop in ImageNet zero-shot accuracy to 0.5 points. It also reduces hubness on all 21 checkpoints and improves retrieval on 4 of 5 dual encoders from other families.
  </details>

- **[TriSegAgent: Context-Efficient 3D Annotation with MLLMs via Tri-Planes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30240)**  `arXiv:2611.30240`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Qingqiao Hu, Jialin Li, Meilong Xu, Kehan Qi, Yipeng Zhang, Lin Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive segmentation models turn a few clicks into a segmentation prediction across domains, so annotators only decide where the prediction still needs correction. Annotation thus becomes a loop of inspecting the prediction and clicking again. Each step of this loop is a visual judgment, which suits general-purpose multimodal large language model (MLLM) agents. In this work, we focus on letting these agents run this loop on 3D volumes. However, the MLLM in such an agent takes only 2D images as input, so the agent must observe the 3D volume in 2D. Since the loop calls the MLLM many times, this observation must be token-compact enough to keep every call cheap, yet informative enough to show error regions in the 3D prediction. We meet both needs with tri-planes, the three orthogonal planes that cross at one point in the volume. We show, both theoretically and empirically, that tri-planes are context-efficient yet keep sufficient detail to show error regions in the 3D prediction. Building on tri-planes, we introduce TriSegAgent, an MLLM agent that refines the 3D prediction iteratively. Within the agent, the MLLM only judges the prediction on tri-planes, while a visual harness turns each judgment into a 3D action in code. To make the loop reliable, the harness places each click at an exact voxel in the volume and checks whether to accept the 3D prediction. To evaluate the agent, we build a diverse benchmark of 690 regions of interest (ROIs) from 35 public medical datasets across three imaging modalities. With frontier MLLMs, TriSegAgent reaches an overall Dice about 10 points above the strongest baseline, and its lead is even larger on targets below 3 cm. With flash-level MLLMs, it outperforms a recent 3D segmentation agent built on a frontier MLLM.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Beyond Imitation: Observation Conditioned Distillation for Video LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29728)**  `arXiv:2611.29728`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Junzhou Chen, Jindong Wang, Gang Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning distillation offers a promising approach to transfer temporal reasoning capabilities of powerful Video-LLMs to open-source student models. However, teacher and student Video-LLMs may construct different temporal observations from the same video due to the adoption of distinct video sampling pipelines. A reasoning trajectory valid under the teacher's observations may rely on evidence inaccessible to the student and thus cannot be directly executed based on the student's observations. We formalize this as a conditional target mismatch, thereby revealing the Reasoning Acquisition-Execution Gap. To bridge this gap, we propose an acquisition-to-execution framework that first distills teacher trajectories into a student reasoning prior and then further optimizes the execution capability of this prior across multiple observations. We further introduce Execution-Aware Reference Regularization, which retains the existing prior when it is executable under current observations, while allowing a greater degree of adaptive adjustment when it is not. Experiments across multiple Video-LLM backbones and six video reasoning benchmarks show that explicitly optimizing observation-conditioned execution consistently outperforms supervised distillation and standard RL-based optimization, with gains extending to held-out out-of-domain benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Auxiliary Clues Aware's Geometry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30617)**  `arXiv:2611.30617`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Qigong Lei, Qingsong Wang, Wang Lin, Qianyi Yang, Chang Yao, Jingyuan Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geometry Problem Solving (GPS) serves as a rigorous test of the complex reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), particularly when solutions require auxiliary constructions absent from the input diagram. However, explicit diagramming methods suffer from unstable image generation quality and cascading errors, while existing implicit attention mechanisms primarily focus on visible elements, limiting their ability to support reasoning that requires additional geometric constructions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{AuxCA} (Auxiliary-Clues-Aware GRPO), a reinforcement learning framework that helps models learn transferable auxiliary-construction strategies that can be applied beyond the training domain without explicit diagram generation at inference time. Specifically, we introduce Auxiliary Clues Dependency (\textbf{ACD}) to quantify the influence of auxiliary visual clues on token generation and integrate it into GRPO through fine-grained advantage weighting. Furthermore, we incorporate an auxiliary incentive reward to encourage the effective use of auxiliary geometric clues in correct solutions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AuxCA-8B outperforms the explicit-plotting baseline Bagel-Canvas and achieves competitive performance with leading proprietary models on auxiliary-dependent plane geometry problems. Improvements on analytic geometry, solid geometry, and external benchmarks further suggest that the learned strategies can transfer beyond the training domain. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[OpenViVE: New Benchmark and Dual-Stream Co-Attention Framework for Vietnamese Visual Entailment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30161)**  `arXiv:2611.30161`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Bao Quoc Trinh, Tin Van Huynh, Kiet Van Nguyen, Ngan Luu-Thuy Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual Entailment (VE) determines whether a textual hypothesis is entailed, contradicted, or unverifiable from an image. However, existing VE benchmarks are largely English-centric and do not capture Vietnamese-specific linguistic distinctions or culturally grounded visual contexts. To address this gap, we introduce OpenViVE, the first large-scale native Vietnamese VE benchmark, containing 62,268 balanced image-hypothesis pairs from 10,378 culturally grounded images. OpenViVE is constructed using an Artifact-Aware Hybrid LLM-Human Pipeline that combines constrained generation, human validation, and PMI-guided refinement. The pipeline substantially reduces hypothesis-only shortcuts, with Contradiction accuracy dropping from 96% to 66% after artifact refinement. We further evaluate a dual-stream PhoBERT-ConvNeXt framework with bidirectional co-attention to align visual evidence with Vietnamese hypotheses. The framework achieves 84.84% accuracy and 84.90% macro-F1 on the test set, while PhoBERT consistently outperforms multilingual XLM-R variants. These results highlight the importance of language-specific encoding and explicit cross-modal interaction for Vietnamese VE.
  </details>

- **[Counting Symbols on Technical Drawings: A Benchmark and Verified Rewards for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29309)**  `arXiv:2611.29309`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Junxian Li, Ruixuan Yang, Zhenhua Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Today's MLLMs can count objects in natural images. However, when asking them how many bolt holes appear in a mechanical drawing or how many nitrogen atoms are in a chemical structure, they often struggle. Counting in technical drawings remains challenging, as objects can be small, densely packed, or even implied by drawing conventions. To study this problem, we introduce CountDrawBench, a benchmark including floor plans, electrical schematics, mechanical drawings, and molecular structures, with 1,574 counting questions and instance-level annotations where available. Besides evaluation, we introduce Code-Verified RL, a reinforcement learning framework that teaches MLLMs to count with executable feedback. Our method combines three complementary rewards: a count reward that checks the final answer, an instance-matching reward that verifies object locations, and a zoom-based reward that checks counting consistency across local image regions. All three are integrated into a unified GRPO objective. Extensive experiments reveal that even leading MLLMs struggle on CountDrawBench, with the best evaluated model achieving only 59.3% accuracy. Our method improves Qwen3.5-9B from 49.3% to 54.3% accuracy, outperforming all evaluated open-source baselines. These results establish CountDrawBench as a challenging testbed for technical drawing understanding and demonstrate the value of code-verified rewards in these tasks. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[S-Mind: Internalizing Multi-View Geometry for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28649)**  `arXiv:2611.28649`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Muxin Fu, Peike Yu, Guibin Zhang, Wenrui Zhou, Runze Liu, Lang Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial intelligence, the ability to reason about 3D space from visual observations, becomes essential as multimodal large language models (MLLMs) move into the physical world. Beyond perceiving each view, it rests on two cornerstones of human cognition: fusing multiple views into one coherent 3D scene and mentally transforming that scene to new viewpoints as reasoning unfolds. Yet our spatial probes reveal that MLLMs largely lack both capabilities, even after spatial fine-tuning. We therefore present S-Mind, which internalizes multi-view geometry within the MLLM through a compact set of spatial tokens. It supervises these tokens to hold the fused 3D scene and re-emits them to re-express this scene from every viewpoint the reasoning adopts. Across four spatial benchmarks, S-Mind improves its Qwen3.5 backbones by up to 22.26 points and surpasses frontier general models such as GPT-6-Astra (+0.45), Kimi-K3.1-Pro (+1.97), and GLM-5.3-Flash (+7.80) in average accuracy. Moreover, the same probes confirm that it acquires both capabilities, reaching fusion and transformation accuracies of 65.11% and 82.17%, against at most 16.02% and 37.28% for the baselines. At inference, S-Mind also runs about 100x faster than a tool-based agent on the same backbone. Instilled through these spatial tokens during training, spatial capability becomes intrinsic to the backbone and persists even when the tokens are withheld, offering a promising path toward spatial intelligence in MLLMs.
  </details>

- **[VidRouteBench: A Benchmark for Budgeted Video-Generation Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28099)**  `arXiv:2611.28099`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Dayu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-video generators differ by two orders of magnitude in price, and a cheap configuration of a strong model is often good enough for a prompt. Routing prompts across such arms (model x sampling configuration) should buy quality at fixed budget, but no dataset supports studying it: public video-preference sets contain only closed models, one configuration each.We release VidRouteBench: 16 arms from 7 open families spanning 121x in measured cost ($0.0025-$0.298 per clip), 27,110 clips (1,421 A800-hours) over 1,625 grouped prompts, a quality score frozen after calibration against human preferences (68.7% CV accuracy vs 66.2% for the best single scorer), per-clip scores on 9 identifiable quality dimensions, repeated seeds, an encrypted test split, and a sealed newest family for cold start. The evaluator compares a router against the best prompt-agnostic mixture of arms, not the best single arm. Per-prompt specialisation here is real and predictable (oracle gap 0.642, 47% reproducible across seeds, prompt-arm interaction F=4.47 at p=1.6e-63), unlike LLM-routing arms, where the same pipeline finds none.We then propose OPMU (one probe, many utilities): label a new arm on K probe prompts, predict its 9 dimensions once, and serve any scenario utility by scalarising afterwards. Over 215 utility directions OPMU Pareto-dominates the prompt-agnostic baseline for 94% of directions versus 90% for per-scenario routers, with no per-scenario retraining. Three baselines fail instructively: bag-of-words features lose almost all routing gain in the low-data regime (0.009 vs 0.168); crediting a new arm with its probe average is harmful (-3.32 recovery) although it is the strong baseline on RewardBench-style arms (0.786); Chebyshev scalarisation does not help, our cost-utility front being convex.
  </details>

- **[Pop-Up Distractions Reveal Bag-of-Events Behavior in Video Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27101)**  `arXiv:2605.27101`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Oscar Chew, Serhii Honcharenko, Qian-Hui Chen, Patricia Lu, Dishant Zaveri, Khoa D. Doan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A key capability for video understanding is reliably linking subjects to events across time, yet whether Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) actually achieve this remains unclear.
