Current benchmarks typically isolate these dimensions, being either multilingual but text-only, or multimodal but monolingual. While recent red-teaming efforts attempt to bridge this gap by rendering harmful prompts as images, their overreliance on typography-style visuals and lack of semantically grounded image-text pairs fail to capture realistic cross-modal interactions under multilingual and multimodal conditions. To address this, we introduce Lingua-SafetyBench, a comprehensive benchmark of 100,440 harmful image-text pairs spanning 10 languages. Crucially, Lingua-SafetyBench explicitly partitions data into Image-Dominant and Text-Dominant subsets to precisely disentangle sources of risk. Extensive evaluations reveal that current VLLMs retain non-negligible vulnerabilities under these joint inputs. Furthermore, analyzing modality-language interactions uncovers a striking asymmetry: on average, Image-Dominant Risk poses greater threats in High-Resource Languages (HRLs), whereas models are more vulnerable in Non-High-Resource Languages (Non-HRLs) under Text-Dominant Risk. Finally, a controlled study on the Qwen series demonstrates that while model scaling and iterative upgrades improve overall safety, they disproportionately benefit HRLs. This exacerbates the safety disparity between HRLs and Non-HRLs under Text-Dominant Risk, highlighting that achieving robust safety requires dedicated language- and modality-aware alignment strategies beyond mere scaling. Our data and code are available atthis https URL. Warning: This paper contains examples with unsafe content.
  </details>

- **[6 Fingers, 1 Kidney: Natural Adversarial Medical Images Reveal Critical Weaknesses of Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04238)**  `arXiv:2512.04238`  `cs.CV`  
  _Leon Mayer, Piotr Kalinowski, Caroline Ebersbach, Marcel Knopp, Tim Rädsch, Evangelia Christodoulou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly integrated into clinical workflows. However, existing benchmarks primarily assess performance on common anatomical presentations and fail to capture the challenges posed by rare variants. To address this gap, we introduce AdversarialAnatomyBench, the first benchmark comprising naturally occurring rare anatomical variants across diverse imaging modalities and anatomical regions. We call such variants that violate learned priors about "typical" human anatomy natural adversarial anatomy. Benchmarking 25 state-of-the-art VLMs with AdversarialAnatomyBench yielded three key insights. First, when queried with basic medical perception tasks, mean accuracy dropped from 71% on typical to 28% on atypical anatomy. Even the best-performing models, GPT-5, Gemini 2.5 Pro, and Llama 4 Maverick, showed performance drops of 39-51%. Second, model errors closely mirrored expected anatomical biases. Third, neither model scaling nor interventions, including bias-aware prompting and test-time reasoning, resolved these issues. These findings highlight a critical limitation in current VLMs: their poor generalization to rare anatomical presentations. AdversarialAnatomyBench provides a foundation for systematically measuring and mitigating anatomical bias in multimodal medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. The code and dataset are available atthis https URLandthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Wave-Particle (Continuous-Discrete) Dualistic Visual Tokenization for Unified Understanding and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01593)**  `arXiv:2511.01593`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yizhu Chen, Chen Ju, Zhicheng Wang, Shuai Xiao, Xu Chen, Jinsong Lan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The unification of understanding and generation within a single multi-modal large model (MLLM) remains one significant challenge, largely due to the dichotomy between continuous and discrete visual tokenizations. Continuous tokenizer (CT) achieves strong performance by bridging multiple independently-trained understanding modules and generation modules, but suffers from complex multi-stage pipelines and substantial engineering overhead. Conversely, discrete tokenizers (DT) offer a conceptually elegant idea by quantizing each image into a primitive, but inevitably leading to information loss and performance degradation. To resolve this tension, we question the binary choice between CT and DT, inspired by the wave-particle duality of light, and propose the Continuous-Discrete Dualistic Visual Tokenizer (CDD-VT). We treat visual data as a flexible composition of image primitives derived from quantized codebooks, with the crucial insight that the primitive number assigned to each visual sample is adaptively determined according to its complexity: simple instances use a few primitives, emulating discrete tokenization, while complex instances use many, approximating continuous tokenization. Two core components are designed: Diverse Quantitative Primitives, which encourage primitives orthogonality to better populate information space, and Dynamic Primitive Allocator, which assesses sample complexity to determine the optimal set of primitives. Extensive experiments on reconstruction, retrieval and classification show that CDD-VT achieves superior performance over to specialized CT and DT, effectively getting strong result within a concise and scalable MLLM.
  </details>

- **[Unlocking the Power of Loops in Pretrained LLMs with Loop Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31094)**  `arXiv:2611.31094`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xuanlei Zhao, Hailun Xu, Kai Wang, Yang Luo, Yingxuan Ren, Zirui Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers iterate a shared block of layers to increase computation without adding parameters, offering stronger performance and test-time scaling. However, this power has so far required training from scratch and yielded only modest gains for LLMs. Finetuning existing models into loops is far cheaper, yet also brings only marginal gains over SFT. We propose LoopRes, an effective, scalable, and simple approach that turns loops into significant and consistent gains for pretrained LLMs. Building on Attention Residuals, LoopRes lets each looped layer attend directly to the outputs of earlier loops. Since these outputs are iterates of the same layers and become harder to tell apart, we give each a trainable loop-aware key that encodes its structural identity. Attending to all earlier loops also lets layers skip intermediate ones, so we restrict each layer to a residual window of the latest W loops, forcing every loop to build incrementally on recent ones. Across 6 pretrained LLMs from 3 model families and 9 benchmarks, LoopRes delivers significant and consistent gains: at 2x compute, it improves Qwen3-1.7B-Base by 29.1% over SFT, more than doubling the gain of the state-of-the-art baseline. The gains hold on larger models up to 30B and across all three families. More importantly, from 1.7B to 8B, LoopRes scales more steeply than SFT as compute grows, reaching a higher ceiling while needing less compute on long outputs. These results establish LoopRes as an effective looping approach, delivering both better performance and better test-time scaling.
  </details>

- **[Laya-IsoToken: Day-One Clinical Codebook Switching via Closed-Form Operator Compilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30196)**  `arXiv:2611.30196`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zeqiang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Healthcare systems periodically revise diagnostic and procedural classifications (ICD-9 to ICD-10/11), update hospital reimbursement groupers (MS-DRG), and deploy clinical coding across languages. Supervised coding networks bind clinical text encoders to fixed per-code classifier weights, forcing hospitals to re-annotate tens of thousands of records before switching codebooks, whereas standalone large language models incur high latency and hallucinate invalid codes. Here we show that day-one clinical codebook switching becomes feasible without target-labeled notes when local clinical evidence reading is decoupled from a strictly frozen multilingual concept space. Laya-IsoToken trains a clinical span reader once on source records with zero per-code classifier parameters, separating text evidence from historical disease prevalence. Given only public target code descriptions, closed-form matrix compilation aligns distributional shifts, compensates for paraphrased definitions, and deduplicates sibling codes in under three seconds, while a pre-deployment geometric residual routes unfamiliar concepts to offline language model verification. Across 32,804 MIMIC-IV ICD-9 discharges, Laya-IsoToken matches published supervised baselines (62.6% Micro-F1) while reducing active calibration error by 32%. Switched with zero target training notes to 13,783 unseen-patient ICD-10 discharges, MS-DRG groups, and multilingual cohorts, it outperforms biomedical dense retrievers, semantic crosswalks (+5.4 F1 points on ICD-10, paired $d_z = 0.49$, $p < 0.001$), and official US CMS equivalence mappings (+3.5 points, $p < 0.001$) at 9.4 ms per admission. Pairing this reader with an offline 31B verifier across 1,000 full discharge summaries lifts zero-training ICD-10 coding to 40.6% Micro-F1 (57.0% category F1, $d_z = 0.66$, $p < 0.001$) with 0.0% invalid code hallucinations.
  </details>

- **[Reading the Tea Leaves, Again: Can Large Language Models Predict Language Model Training Runs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30141)**  `arXiv:2611.30141`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xinran Zhao, Xiang Li, Tong Chen, Jinchuan Tian, Zichun Yu, Tongshuang Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents now propose research ideas and run the experiments that test them, but they train every run to its end, whereas a researcher reads a run's configuration and the beginning of its curve and stops the runs that show no promise. Reading a run this way takes more than describing or implementing a training method: it takes knowing what a design choice does to a run. We build Tea Leaves to measure this knowledge on 682 real training runs across pretraining, fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning (RL): a forecaster sees the first 20% of a run with its configuration, description, and code, and predicts the rest. Across 11 LLMs and a range of forecasting baselines, the strongest LLMs match or exceed the best curve fitter on smooth loss curves (skill 0.66 against 0.62) and lead on noisy RL reward curves (0.43 against 0.13). What bounds every forecaster is what the log carries: on sparsely logged community runs all methods lose most of their skill, and none reliably flags a failing run in advance. Beyond the curve, information helps and effort does not: related runs in the prompt cut the error on loss curves by 39%, and knowledge of training dynamics lets the strongest LLMs order all 36 pairs of runs that differ by a described code change, which the early curves do not separate, whereas tools add little. This knowledge can be learned from logged runs: a small open model trained on public runs matches the best LLM on loss curves. Applied to the progress curves of research agents, the same reading ranks tasks by their remaining headroom, although for a single agent run it does not improve on the last value. We release the benchmark, the trained readers, and their training data to test and train LLMs' understanding of training and to help research agents decide which runs deserve their compute.
  </details>

- **[Parameter-Efficient Vocabulary Expansion via Cross-Lingual Embedding Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30123)**  `arXiv:2611.30123`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Andrew Ivan Soegeng, Muhammad Reza Qorib, Weishi Wang, Daniel Dahlmeier, Hwee Tou Ng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual pretraining with vocabulary expansion is often used to make large language models (LLMs) more effective and efficient at handling new languages. However, when LLMs are extended to many languages simultaneously, the number of model parameters increases significantly. In addition, the newly added tokens do not receive the same number of training steps as the tokens in the original vocabulary, which sometimes causes vocabulary expansion to yield only limited performance improvements. We propose leveraging the semantic equivalence between words in the model's primary language and those in the extended vocabulary to transfer learned representations. In this paper, we introduce MATE, a parameter-efficient vocabulary expansion method that enables effective cross-lingual transfer with 8 times fewer additional parameters per token. MATE reduces the average tokenization compression ratio and delivers improvements on multiple benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[FOLD: Position-Free Far Memory with Local Order for Long-Context Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20392)**  `arXiv:2610.20392`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shuxuan Li, Jialiang Kang, Yanghao Zhou, Ziye Zhu, Zhilin Zhao, Xiao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates LLM generation through lightweight drafting and parallel verification. It is particularly promising for long inputs, where KV-cache access costs can be amortized over multiple accepted tokens. Yet most drafters are trained on short sequences and degrade on long inputs, while long-context training is costly. Existing training-free approaches restrict the context window, rescale positions, or augment recent context with retrieved distant entries. Drafters, however, read target hidden states that already encode contextual and positional information, and their lightweight architecture makes full-history attention affordable. We propose FOLD, a training-free method that preserves exact local positions and treats all distant entries as far memory at a shared position. To counter attention dilution as the memory grows, we sharpen attention within it to emphasize relevant entries while separately controlling its total weight relative to the local window. Across six target-drafter pairs and diverse benchmarks of up to 256K tokens, FOLD predominantly delivers the highest speedup, with the best case more than doubling the speedup over autoregressive decoding, from 2.08$\times$ to 4.22$\times$.
  </details>

- **[MILES: Modular Instruction Memory with Learnable Selection for Self-Improving LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06974)**  `arXiv:2607.06974`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruilin Tong, Dong Gong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly improve their reasoning at test time via additional computation, yet most existing works treat each problem in isolation. When problems arrive sequentially, accumulating reusable experience across them can further improve performance. Existing memory-based methods either store whole-solution templates that generalize poorly to novel problems or use heuristic step-level selection that is not optimized for final-answer correctness. Learning selection policies requires large-scale training data and fixed action spaces, making such approaches unsuitable for test-time settings where memory expands incrementally and only limited supervision is available. We propose MILES (Modular Instruction Memory with LEarnable Selection), a framework that dynamically expands step-wise memory and applies correctness-optimized memory composition under realistic test-time constraints. MILES maintains modular memory units consisting of asymmetric pairs of sub-goal embeddings and sub-instructions, each associated with a learnable selection head. This memory structure enables a coarse-to-fine retrieval mechanism: The coarse level enables memory expansion and collects supervision for training selection heads from confident samples, while the fine stage applies learned selection heads to rerank coarse-level candidates and guide reasoning for uncertain samples. MILES achieves self-improvement by using its own confidence as the learning signal at test time, or given feedback such as labeled data in an offline setting. MILES consistently matches or outperforms prior methods while achieving superior accuracy-efficiency tradeoffs. Extensive experiments demonstrate its effectiveness, robustness, and transferability.
  </details>

- **[Calibrating LLMs with Semantic-level Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15588)**  `arXiv:2605.15588`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Fengfei Yu, Ruijia Niu, Dongxia Wu, Yian Ma, Rose Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) are deployed in consequential settings such as medical question answering and legal reasoning, the ability to estimate when their outputs are likely to be correct is essential for safe and reliable use, requiring well-calibrated uncertainty. Standard reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains models with a binary correctness reward that is indifferent to confidence, providing no penalty for confident but wrong predictions and thereby degrading calibration. Recent work addresses this by training models to produce verbalized confidence scores alongside answers and rewarding agreement with correctness. However, verbalized confidence is calibrated at the token level and thus exhibits inconsistency across textual variations with same semantic meaning. We propose \textbf{Calibration with Semantic Reward (CSR)}, a framework that calibrates language models directly in semantic space without a verbalized confidence interface. CSR combines the correctness reward with a novel semantic calibration reward that encourages exploitation among correct rollouts by promoting semantic agreement, and exploration among incorrect ones by discouraging spurious consistency. Experiments across three model families on HotpotQA (in-distribution) and TriviaQA, MSMARCO, and NQ-Open (out-of-distribution) show that CSR consistently achieves lower ECE and higher AUROC than verbalized-confidence baselines across nearly all settings, reducing ECE by up to $40\%$ and improving AUROC by up to $31\%$ over verbalized-confidence baselines, with calibration behavior generalizing robustly across all four evaluation settings.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking the Evaluation of Contrastive Decoding for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30460)**  `arXiv:2611.30460`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Ting Chen, Geng Li, Guohao Chen, Hanjia Lyu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contrastive decoding (CD) mitigates object hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by contrasting token distributions under different conditions to adjust token scores. However, existing generative and discriminative benchmarks primarily measure final task scores, which can be confounded by candidate filtering, changes in generation trajectories, and response biases, making it difficult to isolate the effects of the underlying contrastive adjustments. To address these limitations while complementing existing evaluations, we introduce CONCOR (Contrastive Correction Evaluation), a controlled benchmark that directly evaluates the competition between ground-truth and different types of hallucinated candidates using the Contrastive Grounding Margin (CGM). CGM also enables analysis of directional consistency, composition, and reliability of contrastive corrections. Our evaluation reveals that existing CD methods exhibit distinct correction behaviors: some consistently favor ground-truth candidates, whereas others produce strong corrections but also introduce more errors. Crucially, these differences help explain their contrasting responses to increased contrast strength, which improves some methods while degrading others. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating the contrastive correction itself, rather than relying solely on final-output performance, and CONCOR provides a controlled and interpretable framework for understanding and improving CD methods for hallucination mitigation.
  </details>

- **[How Reliable is AI-Generated Audio Description? A Multilingual Expert Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29658)**  `arXiv:2611.29658`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Annette Rios, Lukas Fischer, Sarah Ebling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audio description (AD) makes visual media, such as video, accessible to blind or low-vision (BLV) audiences, but the high cost of professional production limits coverage. Due to recent advances in AI, numerous approaches to automatically generate intermediate AD scripts and/or final AD audios have been proposed. We present an expert evaluation of AI-generated AD scripts for movies in Italian and German that compares two AI systems, a GPT-4o- and a Qwen-based pipeline, against human-authored descriptions. Professional audio describers rated description quality and annotated errors using a detailed error taxonomy that includes content, character, fluency and coherence errors. The results show that while GPT-4o outperforms the Qwen models, both AI systems still fall short of human professional quality: the generated descriptions show substantially higher error rates and lower quality ratings. Based on the error annotations, we identify two key areas that require improvement: 1) character references, which includes both issues with identification and coherent naming, and 2) content errors and hallucinations, where the AD misrepresents or invents events. These error types are particularly critical, as they directly affect the viewer's ability to follow the narrative.
  </details>

- **[Reducing Permutation Sensitivity in Multimodal Large Language Models via Shared Positions and Attention-Only Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28612)**  `arXiv:2611.28612`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yifei Cheng, Xiaolin Zhang, Yu-Xuan Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we conduct a systematic study on improving permutation robustness while preserving accuracy. First, we categorize permutation bias phenomena into four representative scenarios. Then, we take the multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) dataset as an example under text-option reordering scenario to analyze the root cause of permutation bias. Based on this root cause analysis and set encoding strategy, we investigate shared positions with blocked cross-unit attention (SP-B) and extend this design to bidirectional cross-unit attention (SP-V) for modality exchange. However, these structural changes may reduce predictive accuracy. To recover model capability across different scenarios under the SP structure, an attention-only distillation from frozen native models is proposed. Shared positions reduce permutation sensitivity, and attention-only distillation improves accuracy under the shared-position structure. Across all three models, our method is comparable to or better than majority voting in macro-average all-permutation accuracy, and outperforms supervised fine-tuning by 1.95-4.20 percentage points. This work provides valuable insight into permutation bias mitigation and model capability recovery.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Self-Consistency: Recursive Self-Improvement at Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30965)**  `arXiv:2611.30965`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jianyu Wu, Yizhou Wang, Encheng Su, Jinouwen Zhang, Pengze Li, Lintao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) that continue improving after deployment (e.g., recursive self-improvement) offer a promising path toward general intelligence. Current test-time methods pursue this goal on unlabeled questions, primarily relying on self-consistency: they generate independent solutions and use majority voting to select an answer or construct training targets. This leaves earlier reasoning unused at inference and can reinforce an incorrect majority during training. Humans instead build on earlier attempts to preserve useful reasoning, correct mistakes and improve their problem-solving strategies. Following this principle, we propose Test-Time Recursive Self-Improvement (TT-RSI), a label-free framework for improving LLMs at test time through two mutually reinforcing components: (1) In-Context RSI repeatedly re-solves each question using the full previous solution as a reference, letting the model decide whether and how to use it so that inference compute builds on earlier reasoning. (2) In-Weight RSI distills successive refinements into the model's weights, enabling progress beyond an initially wrong majority and theoretically bringing the first attempt toward the model's infinite-step refinement limit. The updated model answers better and refines more effectively, generating stronger references for subsequent training. Extensive experiments show that TT-RSI improves average accuracy by +13.89% over the base model and outperforms the strongest self-consistency-based method by +4.79% under the same test-time scaling budget.
  </details>

- **[When 1+1 $\neq$ 2: Benchmarking Civil-Time Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29972)**  `arXiv:2611.29972`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Melika Shirian, Kianoosh Vadaei, Arshia Hemmat, Mohammad Hossein Shaker Ardakani, Ali Mamanpoosh, Matin Moqadas, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Calendar arithmetic is non-linear. When 1:59 AM + 1 minute occurs during daylight saving spring-forward, the result is 3:00 AM, not 2:00 AM, as the hour is skipped. We introduce CivilTime-Bench, a benchmark for civil-time reasoning covering daylight saving transitions, non-hour offsets, date-line crossings, and ISO week boundaries. With 60,011 training and 10,500 evaluation examples under strict output-contract evaluation, we benchmark seven LLMs (six instruction-tuned models and Llama-3.1-8B base) and find: zero-shot exact match varies substantially across models, contract-aware prompting often improves format compliance but yields inconsistent accuracy gains, few-shot in-context learning provides model-dependent improvements, and fine-tuning achieves 74.6--86.6% EM on synthetic evaluation but only 36.6--45.2% on HumanTime-500 for models with valid results on both splits, highlighting substantial limitations in generalization. We release the benchmark and code.
  </details>

- **[Which Loopholes Get Found? Predicting Reward Hacking of LLM Judges Before RL Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29651)**  `arXiv:2611.29651`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xuanyi Lyu, Yuezhou Zhao, Qing Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A policy trained against an LLM judge can learn to exploit its loopholes. Static probes show that such judges accept degenerate answers like a lone "{" or "Thought process:", but not which flaw a policy will find. In GRPO the first loophole to be exploited can be predicted before training from each pattern's exposure, the product of the starting policy's propensity to produce it and the judge's false-positive rate (FPR). To test exposure causally, we seed the patterns into fine-tuning at three doses in a Latin square. Across 36 GRPO runs with seven judges, the first exploited pattern had the highest exposure in all 12 runs with exploitation and the highest FPR in only 6. Predictions recorded before training held in three of four runs with one pattern masked by rule. When only two patterns were seeded, at 3% to 3.8% of the fine-tuning data and chosen so that exposure disagreed with FPR alone or propensity alone, exposure named the first exploited pattern and its onset window in all six runs. Exploitation occurred only on hard prompts, where an accepted exploit gets a large group-normalized advantage. Auditing exposure before RL therefore identifies the loophole a policy will likely exploit first.
  </details>

- **[Population-Based Post-Training for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29014)**  `arXiv:2611.29014`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yixuan Even Xu, Aleksei Petrenko, Raja Giryes, Jackson Hamburger, Kevin Chen, Fei Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is one of the most popular post-training paradigms for large language models. RLVR typically trains a single model under hyperparameters chosen before training begins, while researchers tune hyperparameters across consecutive runs. We introduce Population-Based Post-Training (PBPT), which trains several models in parallel and lets the training process do the tuning through population-based training. PBPT periodically evaluates models on a validation set. High-performers are duplicated and their hyperparameters perturbed to explore new configurations. Low-performers stop training and are replaced by these copies. On reasoning benchmarks (Enigmata, Nemotron) and interactive tool-use environments (Gaia2, AppWorld), PBPT produces stronger agents than compute-matched groups of independent RLVR learners. Furthermore, PBPT is able to recover from poor initial hyperparameters, automatically discovering better settings over the course of training.
  </details>

- **[When Rubrics Do Not Decide: Silent Value Completion in LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28940)**  `arXiv:2611.28940`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuangzhuang Pan, Yan Xia, Kaiwen Lei, Chee Seng Chan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-criterion LLM judges often evaluate responses without specifying how conflicting criteria resolve, leaving the winner undetermined even under exact criterion scores. Issuing a strict verdict without declaring the required trade-off premise constitutes silent value completion. We introduce TradeoffBench to isolate this failure by separating rule execution from response scoring. On authoritative criterion vectors, six open-weight non-reasoning judges pass all dominance and equality controls yet pick unsupported winners on 9%-81% of 1,345 conflicts. All 577 order-stable choices follow an explicitly banned majority rule. Reasoning-enabled configurations drop anonymous-vector violations to 2 in 9,720 judgments under an explicit rule, yet cannot bridge omitted trade-offs or divergent component scoring. On raw TradeoffBench text with unspecified trade-offs, GPT-6.1-sol omits its completion premise in 29.8% of 400 verified conflicts. Even under an explicit rule on four-rater-consensus MultiPref conflicts, four reasoning judges still issue strict verdicts on 16/45 (GPT-6.1-sol) up to 35/46 and 25/29 (open-weight judges) of valid presentations. We propose identified-first evaluation, decoupling specification support, explicit completion, and target agreement. In MultiPref human-score audits, peer-mean substitution raises directional disagreement from 0.6% to 28.8% on 2,707 shared targets. A single-peer ablation confirms a 29.38-point gap on 2,175 targets, ruling out averaging as the cause. In HUMAINE, declared partial trade-offs add 2.5 percentage points of coverage, yet newly decided pairs incur 3.9% disagreement versus 0.9% for Pareto. Reliable LLM judging, therefore, requires auditing, specification support, declared trade-offs, and human target agreement separately.
  </details>

- **[Distributionally-Verifiable Rewards for Human-Behavior Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28048)**  `arXiv:2611.28048`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruoyun Ma, Shuang Liu, Peiye Liu, Ao Qu, Baichuan Mo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to simulate survey respondents in social-science research. A useful simulator should predict individual responses, reproduce population response distributions, and capture demographic differences. Common training approaches struggle to balance these goals: exact-match rewards encourage the model to concentrate on the most frequent human response, which is contradictory to the goal of distributional alignment. Meanwhile, aggregate distribution matching can look good even when demographic information is ignored. To this end, we introduce Distributionally-Verifiable Rewards (DVR), a reinforcement-learning reward that uses demographic group distribution gaps to guide individual responses, designed by aligning policy generated frequencies with human response frequencies within each question-group margins. We evaluate the method using the OpinionQA dataset and find that comparing with accuracy-only RLVR, DVR preserves per-response accuracy while reducing JSD by 34.5% in-distribution and 14.6% on held-out questions, with higher DPC point estimates on both splits. Comparing KL-SFT with the demographics-blind marginal further shows that low JSD is insufficient to establish fidelity to population heterogeneity, highlighting that accuracy, distributional fit, and demographic structure should be evaluated together.
  </details>

- **[FOCUS & RePAIR: Mitigating Text Degeneration via Token-Level Guidance for Pruned Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26676)**  `arXiv:2608.26676`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Junyoung Lee, Sehyeon Park, Shinhyoung Jang, Seonha Ryu, Hojeong Kim, Hyunsei Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pruning is a practical approach to compress large language models (LLMs), but it can amplify text degeneration, especially repetition loops, even when perplexity and task accuracy remain largely unchanged. In this work, we present a token-level analysis of this failure mode by viewing decoding as a dynamical process that enters and persists in a small set of recurrent contexts. Our analysis decomposes degeneration into loop entry risk and loop persistence, and shows that persistence is controlled by the escape mass assigned to plausible alternatives within the token sampling set. Motivated by these findings, we propose two token-level guidance objectives for post-pruning fine-tuning. FOCUS reweights distillation toward high-confidence teacher regions to suppress leakage, while RePAIR uses onset-centered positive/negative continuation pairs with a margin loss to promote plausible alternatives and prevent early commitment to repetition loops. Experiments on open-ended continuation and instruction-based generation show that both methods consistently reduce repetition and improve generation quality.
  </details>

- **[Post-training is (Massive) Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07527)**  `arXiv:2606.07527`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Michael Hassid, Yossi Adi, Roy Schwartz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The prevailing paradigm for training LLMs has evolved to rely on a massive post-training phase consisting of SFT and RL. In this position paper, we argue that this methodology effectively marks a reversion to the ``pre-train then fine-tune'' approach of the BERT era, explicitly tailoring models to the desired behaviors and specific benchmarks on which they are evaluated. We begin with a historical overview of LLMs, describing the different phases of the LLM evolution. We argue that the current landscape is remarkably similar to the early days of LLMs, where task performance heavily relied on fitting the models to in-distribution datasets. To empirically demonstrate this, we compare pre-trained models to randomly initialized ones, by fine-tuning both variants on modern reasoning datasets and evaluating them on competitive math and code benchmarks. We show that models post-trained from scratch yield highly non-trivial performance. Our findings suggest that current post-training methodologies function primarily as a distribution-fitting mechanism. We finish by positing that developing generally capable models and systems requires moving beyond extensive post-training for predefined behaviors, shifting instead toward training procedures where models ``learn how to learn''.
  </details>

- **[Token Noise and the Illusion of Preference: A Shapley Value Framework for Auditing LLM-Simulated Choice Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.01332)**  `arXiv:2404.01332`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Behnam Mohammadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to simulate human respondents in choice experiments, yet their answers depend on the exact tokens of a prompt rather than on the offered products alone. I show that invisible characters, blank lines, or minor wording changes shift or reverse simulated choice probabilities. This sensitivity, which I term token noise, creates an illusion of preference and exposes studies to prompt hacking. I introduce a Shapley value audit that divides a model's choice response among the pieces of its prompt and reads each share beside the effect of removing that piece. The edit game divides the effect of several wording and formatting changes beside an actual price change. The word game divides a prompt's response across its words or tokens and summarizes the credit earned by the product attributes as the attribute share. Across four open-weight models and three product categories, wording and formatting edits could rival a substantive price change, while product attributes consistently received a minority of the word-level credit, sometimes less than the answer instruction. Framing cues could take credit from the very price they emphasized and frequently vanished or reversed across equivalent wordings of the question. A persona (even a bare statement that the respondent is a consumer) also shifted choice probabilities and the attributes' credit. Declared with its "players," reference, scale, and uncertainty, my audit separates what a simulated respondent prefers from how the question was asked, the distinction a study must draw before it reads a simulated choice as a preference.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal LLMs Know the Game but Assume the Real World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28745)**  `arXiv:2611.28745`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Jianxin Gao, Bohan Chen, Runze Li, Qi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models can state the rules of a video game, but do they apply them when they only see the game? We introduce RuleCraft, 1,240 image questions over 155 Minecraft rules with outcomes verified in the game engine, each asked in text, on the image with the game named, and on the bare image. Six widely used models know the rules and recognize the game, yet answer the bare image by real-world physics: naming the game raises their accuracy on rules where the game departs from reality by 8.2-19.8 points on a main set and by 33.8-52.7 on a hard stress-test set. Their errors pick the real-world outcome, the gap is largest where the game leaves a scene unchanged, and reasoning traces state the game's rule, then answer by reality. On the main set, naming the game mostly swaps the real-world default for the game's. The pattern holds with reasoning, recurs in two more games, and persists on questions the models themselves take to be about the game; one neutral sentence asking for the rules of the world shown recovers much of the gap. Evaluations that name the game measure knowledge of its rules, not world selection.
  </details>

- **[Deneb-Vritra: Descriptions, Edges and Names Examined by Blocking Via Restoring Token Representations and Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31139)**  `arXiv:2611.31139`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Akhil P, Shahina Shaik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Laya is a 421M-parameter encoder that answers a question about a text in one pass: each answer option, written as a name plus a description, gets a marker token, and one score is read from the network at each marker. We first confirm that such models can follow an option's name when it contradicts the description; the question is where in the network this happens. We locate it. On 951 synthetic questions we move each name onto another option's description and count how often the model picks the option with the right name (the name shortcut): 53% for the released checkpoint, 59% for our fine-tune v1, 11% for a fine-tune with name randomisation. On a 320-item sample, blocking attention from each marker to its own name tokens in layers 14 to 17 of 28 cuts v1's shortcut from 67% to 16% (19% on two schemas held out from component selection) while accuracy with the original names stays at 90%; blocking marker edges to the question, other options or text has no effect. No single head matters (largest effect 9 points), and restoring clean marker states undoes the swap from layer 16. Attention mass tracks the window by layer but not by head. Repeated on 5 checkpoints (released, fine-tuned, name-randomised, and a pair trained without two schemas), the knockout finds similar layers; in the name-randomised ones, blocking the description edges instead brings the shortcut back to 56-58%, so the read persists and is outvoted. Marker states decode the name from layer 0; a one-direction probe erasure changed nothing but was weaker than a random edit. Copying layers 0-13 or 14-27 of a name-randomised sibling into a fine-tune recovers 66% and 65% (v1); 21% and 45% (pair A) of its shortcut reduction. 9 of the 12 predictions we could score, written before running, failed. Head sweeps cover 5 of 5 checkpoints; data are synthetic and LLM-labelled; one architecture.
  </details>

- **[PRIMEAPE: A Multilingual Benchmark for Fine-Grained Meta-Evaluation of Judge Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30925)**  `arXiv:2611.30925`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Vladislav Mikhailov, Sarfraz Ahmad, Momina Ahsan, Zoia Butenko, Vladimir Dobrovolskii, Preslav Nakov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly serve as judges of other models, shaping benchmark rankings and model development. Yet, a judge whose rationale consistently supports its verdict may still disagree with human preferences. To study this, we introduce PRIMEAPE, a multilingual benchmark for evaluating judge verdicts and rationales. PRIMEAPE provides 2,205 response pairs covering 243 instructions in English, French, Spanish, Urdu, Hindi, Norwegian Bokmål, and Yoruba, with 11k human annotations of preferences and rationales across relevance, naturalness, truthfulness, safety, and overall quality. Our evaluation of twelve open- and closed-weight judges reveals that aggregate performance conceals differences across languages and criteria, while requesting rationales does not consistently improve agreement with human preferences. Judges also favor the first position, longer responses, and responses from related models, with effects that vary across criteria and languages. Crucially, judges can justify verdicts that disagree with human preferences while maintaining consistency between their verdicts and rationales. These findings show that consistency with a judge's own verdict does not establish alignment with human judgment. PRIMEAPE enables assessment of both, providing a foundation for more reliable multilingual judges. We release our benchmark and all annotation materials under the CC BY-NC 4.0 license atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[Semantic-aware Decoding for Diverse Text-to-SQL under Schema Ambiguity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30873)**  `arXiv:2611.30873`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Jiang, Anna Mitsopoulou, Apostolos Koukouvinis, Yannis Velegrakis, Georgia Koutrika_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world text-to-SQL systems often face schema ambiguity, where a question can map to multiple plausible schema elements. To support downstream disambiguation, SQL generation systems should include diverse interpretations among their top-k predictions. However, large language models (LLMs) typically favor a single interpretation, producing highly skewed outputs. We propose Semantic-aware Logit Adjustment (SLA), a training-free decoding method that promotes diverse SQL generation by adjusting token logits based on semantic similarity between schema elements. Unlike prior approaches that rely on reasoning-intensive prompting or task-specific fine-tuning, SLA is efficient, model-agnostic, and easy to deploy. Experiments show that SLA consistently improves interpretation coverage over standard decoding and remains competitive with existing diversity-oriented methods at substantially lower inference cost.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Persona Consistency in LLM User Simulators Without External Reference Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30280)**  `arXiv:2611.30280`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Anping Zhang, Yang Tan, Yipeng Liu, Clive Bai, Blaze Chen, Yang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard evaluations assess LLM-simulated personas against an external ground truth, such as community trait annotations, but that reference is unavailable when the persona is inferred only from a short, task-oriented chat. We ask how persona consistency can be evaluated without such a reference, and propose a self-anchored internal-consistency diagnostic: the persona describes itself once and acts twice. A Likert self-report supplies a soft internal anchor; two behavioral profiles come from constrained scenario choices scored without a judge and from open-ended responses judged by an LLM. Consistency is the correlation between the self-report and each behavioral profile, in a shared six-facet BFI-2 space. The framework compares persona-construction strategies by assessing how consistently their elicited responses reflect their self-reported traits. Across ordinary-user chats (WildChat and LMSYS) and well-documented personas with community annotations (the Personality Database, PDB), profile-enriched representations agree with the anchor more closely than a raw-dialogue baseline. On PDB, this self-anchored ordering reproduces the community-annotation ordering. The pattern is stable across answering and judge models.
  </details>

- **[PCC: Pivotal Channel Compensation for Layer Pruning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30183)**  `arXiv:2611.30183`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiangqian Wang, Yucheng Zhang, Zhaohong Jia, Kun Jing, Chunchun Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Treating independent layers as the minimal pruning unit allows layer pruning to efficiently eliminate many redundant parameters and achieve high compression rates. Layer pruning has become a promising and prevalent structured pruning technique for large language models (LLMs). However, multi-layer removal can lead to significant performance degradation for LLMs. Although some works have explored various compensation methods for pruned layers, they are inefficient and costly. Observing that the expression of a large proportion of information is concentrated on a small number of pivotal channels, we propose a novel post-pruning compensation method for layer pruning, called pivotal channel compensation (PCC). PCC can achieve significant performance improvement by compensating for a small number of channels through learnable linear transformations at the pruning interface. Besides, we propose a new evaluation metric, called channel-wise average performance improvement (CAPI), which can measure the efficiency of channel-wise compensation. Our experimental results across various LLMs demonstrate that PCC can achieve significant and consistent improvements and high CAPI on language modeling and zero-shot question answering for the state-of-the-art cross-layer and consecutive-layer pruning methods. Compared with existing compensation methods, PCC can achieve competitive results with fewer additional parameters and less training cost.
  </details>

- **[FiRE: Fine-grained Ranking Evaluation for Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30168)**  `arXiv:2611.30168`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenyang Gao, Yinghao Yang, Xi Jin, Jing Li, Yue Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing reliable machine translation (MT) systems hinges on our ability to distinguish superior translations from inferior ones. However, existing evaluation paradigms, whether limited to coarse overall rankings or misaligned with human preferences, fail to deliver interpretable, fine-grained feedback in reference-free settings. We present a Fine-Grained Ranking Evaluation method (FiRE) that leverages off-the-shelf large language models to perform criterion-driven pairwise comparison across three complementary dimensions: faithfulness, fluency, and consistency of style, instead of producing a single holistic judgment. To enable rigorous meta-evaluation of evaluation paradigms in the absence of any suitable testbed, we construct the first human-annotated, reference-free benchmark for fine-grained ranking evaluation, achieving substantial inter-annotator agreement. Through meta-evaluation on this benchmark and existing MQM datasets, FiRE demonstrably outperforms regression-based and error-analysis metrics in aligning with human comparative judgments, while providing more informative insights into translation quality. Finally, our examination of LLM evaluator biases (position and self-enhancement) and their handling of tied cases offers guidance for more nuanced MT evaluation. Code and benchmark resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ESGPT: A Data-Centric ESG Language Model with Unified Instruction and Benchmark Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30052)**  `arXiv:2611.30052`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jaeyoung Lee, Misuk Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  ESG analysis requires complex language understanding across diverse sources, including corporate reports, news, and disclosures, for tasks such as ESG relevance detection, action interpretation, impact analysis, and question answering. However, existing ESG datasets are fragmented by task and source, with heterogeneous formats and annotation schemes, limiting their use for training general-purpose ESG LLMs and unified evaluation. In this work, we construct ESG-Instruct, a 30,000-instance ESG instruction dataset built from public ESG resources, and ESGU-Bench, an ESG understanding benchmark covering six representative ESG tasks. Based on these resources, we further develop ESGPT, an 8B ESG-specialized LLM, and evaluate it against diverse open-source and API-based LLMs. Experimental results show that general-purpose LLMs exhibit substantial performance variation across ESG tasks, while ESGPT, despite its relatively small scale, achieves the best performance on multiple ESG tasks. This work provides unified data and benchmark resources for training and evaluating ESG-specialized LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Do More Roles or Larger Models Improve LLM Fact-Checking? A Controlled Study on Fixed Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29687)**  `arXiv:2611.29687`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Martin Pavlíček, Martin Kotyrba, Eva Volná, Tomáš Filip, Petr Sosík_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fact-checking system can spend more computation on a claim by using a larger model, by asking the same model several times, or by a multi-role Tribunal: two roles propose quoted findings, code checks the quotations, an auditor that does not see the proposed relation judges each one, and code derives the verdict or abstains. We compare these options on identical fixed evidence, without retrieval: 300 AVeriTeC claims, each with its annotated question--answer items mixed with items written for other claims, read by judges of four open-weights models from three families, a vote of five, and six assignments of models to the Tribunal's roles. The 9B judge was right on 186 claims and the 27B judge of the same family on 156; the larger judges declined to decide far more often and were no more accurate when they decided. The vote reached 196 and the 9B judge with its verdict replaced by Tribunal certificates 195, at a similar cost; the Tribunal alone reached 153 to 164, because it abstained on almost half of the claims. A hearing's outcome followed the auditor's model and was nearly indifferent to the proposers'. A larger auditor, or one of another family, made certificates neither more often correct (77% to 81%) nor more independent of the judge. As a replacement, certificates improved every judge by 3 to 10 points and overturned four to five in a hundred of its correct verdicts. With the annotated evidence withheld, judges and Tribunal still decided on 7% to 14% of the claims.
  </details>

- **[From Vanishing Gradients to Recursive Self-Improvement: Evaluating the Most Influential Papers in Large Language Model Evolution (1997-2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29303)**  `arXiv:2611.29303`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Saadh Jawwadh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid proliferation of Large Language Model (LLM) research makes it increasingly difficult to distinguish incremental refinements from true paradigm shifts. This review evaluates 22 of the most influential publications spanning nearly three decades (1997-2026), organized through a causal, chronologically anchored taxonomy centered on the author's birth year (1999). Beginning "before 1999" with how recurrent neural networks (RNNs) forgot earlier context (much like forgetting Chapter 1 by the time a reader reaches Chapter 9), we trace how Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks introduced a "bouncer" mechanism of forget, input, and output gates to selectively preserve long-term memory. We then examine how Word2Vec turned language into vector mathematics (king - male + female ~ queen), why sequential RNNs hit a parallelization wall, and how the Transformer's all-to-all attention weights launched the modern LLM era alongside a quadratic bottleneck. Next, we analyze why scaling parameters alone leaves capacity under-trained unless data scales equally (Chinchilla), how Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) left alignment superficial, and how that gap compounded into adversarial jailbreaks and single-direction refusal subspaces (J-space), where instructing a model not to think of a restricted concept surfaces it much like the human "red ball" paradox. Finally, we synthesize the current state of AI (2024-2026), encompassing recursive model collapse, autonomous reasoning-model jailbreak agents, machine-speed agentic intrusions, and closed-loop recursive self-improvement (AIDE^2).
  </details>

- **[Seeing Is Not Acting: WARD Diagnoses the Evidence-to-State-to-Action Gap in Longitudinal Clinical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29243)**  `arXiv:2611.29243`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Ding, Yidong Jiang, Junzhi Ning, Juncheng Wu, Yuhan Wang, Renjie Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models for longitudinal clinical care must revise their assessments and actions as patient information accumulates. However, benchmarks centered on isolated responses or final task outcomes provide limited insight into intermediate failures in evidence use, state updating, and action selection. We introduce WARD, a benchmark for evaluating how clinical agents translate accumulating evidence into patient-state judgments and timely, safe actions. WARD comprises 4,560 clinical tasks across seven clinical output domains, organized into 240 checkpoints from 40 longitudinal clinical trajectories spanning 95 hospitalizations. Its Longitudinal Track evaluates these tasks under controlled cumulative evidence. Its Interactive Track evaluates end-to-end, tool-mediated clinical management across 271 verifiable evidence items and 141 reference transitions. Across 12 models with scorable checkpoint outputs, evidence recognition consistently outpaces downstream state and action decisions, with linked-evidence nonresponse reaching 74.47--90.90\%. Models also struggle to retain and update information over time, while in the Interactive Track only 11.46\% of executed actions advance a reference transition. These results reveal a persistent evidence-to-state-to-action gap and highlight the need for process-level evaluation of longitudinal clinicalthis http URLand data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ContextFact: Remembering Facts in Context for Long-Term Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29155)**  `arXiv:2611.29155`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiayu Huang, Yichao Cai, Xinyu Li, Zhen Zhang, Javen Qinfeng Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents built on large language models must maintain continuity across repeated interactions with users. However, long-term conversations scatter facts across turns and sessions, making it difficult to retrieve the right evidence with enough context. Memory for such conversations must therefore preserve facts with their context while keeping evidence selectively accessible. We first study how memory construction and retrieval-unit boundaries affect answerability and evidence access. Reading the entire memory bank reveals gaps that cannot be resolved by retrieval selection alone, showing that some context must be preserved during memory construction. Adjacent-turn grouping improves evidence access at matched unique-turn budgets, while wider windows do not consistently help. This motivates keeping fact-specific context local while organizing cross-session evidence separately. We propose ContextFact, which links context-preserving fact memories with source-grounded entity representations. Within sessions, it gathers the context needed to interpret each fact. Across sessions, it aligns recurring referents and revisits the source dialogue to reconstruct coherent entity representations linked to the supporting facts. At query time, ContextFact retrieves fact and entity views, reranks them independently, and uses entity-linked expansion to surface related facts; complete entity representations then provide organized cross-session evidence. With Qwen3.5-27B as the backbone, ContextFact achieves state-of-the-art performance while using only $21\%$ of the evidence tokens consumed by the strongest evaluated baseline, obtaining $86.49\%$ overall judge accuracy on LoCoMo and $91.00\%$ on LongMemEval-S. Controlled ablations support context-preserving fact boundaries and source-grounded entity reconstruction, while entity readout improves multi-hop accuracy beyond adding more facts.
  </details>

- **[Medical Specialty Prediction from Bangla Patient Narratives: A Comparative Study of ML, DL, Transformers, and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29151)**  `arXiv:2611.29151`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tasnin Tabassum Ali, Faisal Ahmed, Muhammad Anwarul Azim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Timely access to appropriate medical specialists remains a bottleneck in Bangladesh's healthcare system, as patients describe symptoms in informal Bangla rather than clinical terms, leading to ineffective referrals. This paper compares four model families for predicting medical specialties from Bangla patient narratives: six machine learning (ML) models using hybrid TF-IDF features, four deep learning (DL) models using Word2Vec embeddings trained on the corpus, four fine-tuned transformers, and five large language models (LLMs) evaluated zero-shot. From a corpus of 3798 patient records covering 20 specialties, we retained the five with the most records (Medicine, Gynecology, Dermatology, Pediatrics, Gastroenterology) and split the resulting 2730 records 80:20 into training and test sets shared by all families. The best transformer was XLM-RoBERTa (86.26% accuracy, 86.21% macro-F1), the best ML model was SVM (84.98%, 84.92%), the best DL model was Bidirectional GRU (80.77%, 80.83%), and the best LLM was Gemini (90.29%, 90.15%). Holm-corrected paired McNemar tests show SVM significantly outperforming Bi-LSTM (adjusted p = 0.004), whereas the Gemini-versus-Grok gap does not survive correction (adjusted p = 0.104), and neither the strongest transformer versus the weakest LLM (Grok) nor SVM versus the strongest transformer reaches significance. A tuned classical model is thus statistically indistinguishable from the strongest transformer, so accuracy rankings alone should not be read as evidence of one family's superiority. The findings provide a comparative baseline for Bangla medical text classification.
  </details>

- **[Group-Balanced Supervised Fine-Tuning for Knowledge-Intensive Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29127)**  `arXiv:2611.29127`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mark Heimann, Bruno Jacob_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) weights every supervised token equally, so on knowledge-dense corpora the loss budget follows token count rather than importance: on a legal QA benchmark, citation strings absorb 81% of the supervised loss weight budget while the answers being taught absorb 5%. We present Group-Balanced SFT (GB-SFT), which equalizes the loss budget across the semantic roles of tokens within a target sequence. With one tunable dial and one structural switch, GB-SFT contains standard SFT, class- and span-level balancing as corners whose geometry we characterize exactly. On benchmarks in law, medicine, code, and mathematics, we show across model families that: (i) GB- SFT injects new knowledge better than uniform SFT, pointwise token weighting, memory editing, and matched-budget data mixing; (ii) it matches the injection of masking out everything but the knowledge payload while retaining the citation recall that masking destroys (0.98 vs. 0.38); and (iii) role-aware grouping carries the gain: label-free partitions recover at most a third of it, an LLM tagger nearly all where the groups are lexically distinctive; and and (iv) the budget governs what fine-tuning breaks as well as what it learns, acting as a monotone lever on the safety-guardrail erosion SFT causes, at matched task accuracy. We release two new contamination-controlled knowledge-injection benchmarks with validated ground truth.
  </details>

- **[Decoding Transformer Dynamics via Koopman Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29113)**  `arXiv:2611.29113`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Subhradeep Kayal, Ali Dashti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We apply Koopman operator theory and Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) to characterise how large language models (LLMs) process different input topics. Treating the layer-to-layer evolution of hidden states as a dynamical system, we fit a linear Koopman operator and take the subspace spanned by its dominant modes as a per-topic spectral fingerprint. Four findings emerge. First, the depth map is near-linear: a single linear operator predicts each next-layer state to within approx. 18% relative error (interior variance explained approx. 0.99). Second, over the 57 MMLU subjects the fingerprint is topic-discriminative, matching the MMLU taxonomy at 0.72 nearest-neighbour purity and each subject's semantic neighbourhood at 0.75 any-hit@3. Third, it is a reproducible signature (split-half self-retrieval approx. 0.97$). Finally, these findings are consistent across seven base models (Qwen2.5 0.5-7B, Llama-3 1-8B), with a suggestive trend of alignment rising with MMLU accuracy. The spectral fingerprint thus provides a probe of how transformers process different topics across depth. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[NoHairCache: Calibrate-Once KV Cache Eviction with One Power-Law Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29006)**  `arXiv:2611.29006`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuyang Xiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The decay exponent $\alpha$ in KV-cache eviction is an intrinsic property of the model: calibrated once on a single corpus, it transfers directly across domains. Attention-based accumulation (H2O) and recency-based sliding windows (StreamingLLM) are two dominant eviction frameworks, often treated as unrelated, but we show that they are the two endpoints of a single power-law family: by scoring each token as $s_i = A_i \cdot (t-i)^{-\alpha}$, where $A_i$ is cumulative attention and $\alpha$ controls the age penalty, H2O and StreamingLLM correspond to $\alpha=0$ and $\alpha\to\infty$, respectively (with StreamingLLM additionally hard-coding an attention-sink patch).The no-hair mechanism of \citet{xiang2026nohair} bounds the admissible range of $\alpha$, and the measured exponents agree: calibrating once on WikiText yields $[0.556, 0.822]$ for a representative model (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct). The per-model ranges are consistent across five models (410M--1.5B) and independently match the optimal regions from perplexity sweeps.\textsc{NoHairCache} achieves the lowest perplexity across three datasets (WikiText, PG19, and arXiv) without per-dataset tuning, outperforming both H2O and StreamingLLM ($p < 0.05$ on all three datasets, with $0.06$--$0.10$ PPL gains over StreamingLLM at budget $512$). On downstream four-way multiple-choice QA, it matches or outperforms StreamingLLM across all nine (task, budget) cells, retaining $98.2\%$ of full-cache accuracy on NarrativeQA at $25\%$ retention versus $91.1\%$ for StreamingLLM; per-cell McNemar tests are not significant, so QA is corroborating only. We release our calibration script and drop-in scorer upon publication.
  </details>

- **[KMed-31B Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28719)**  `arXiv:2611.28719`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _KMed Team at NAVER_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present KMed-31B, a medical large language model post-trained from an open-weight model for on-premises deployment with limited hardware. Post-training a model for diverse medical tasks remains difficult, because improving one kind of task can lower performance on another. We attribute this to differences between tasks in expected output and available feedback. Medical question answering and diagnosis prediction have reference answers, whereas clinical inquiry has no single correct response and is judged with rubrics. We therefore divide medical training tasks into four categories by these two properties, train one specialist per category with a reward that its feedback supports, and integrate the specialists into a single student with Multi-teacher On-policy Delta Distillation (MOPD$^2$). For clinical inquiry, we build Synthetic Health Dialogue, a simulated corpus of consultations with lay users and clinicians whose patient cases span common clinical presentations in primary care. Relative to its base model, KMed-31B raises HealthBench Hard from 22.95 to 41.87 and HealthBench Professional from 37.92 to 50.20 in non-thinking mode, and from 23.80 to 51.48 and from 41.97 to 59.18 in thinking mode, while its scores on four multiple-choice medical benchmarks are maintained or slightly improved. KMed-31B has the highest unadjusted HealthBench Hard score among the compared models, including proprietary ones, and scores higher on HealthBench Professional than every open-weight model we compare, including models with more than ten times as many total parameters. Applied to another base model, Qwen3.8-27B, the same training recipe also raises both HealthBench scores. We describe the training recipe, data construction, and settings in detail so that institutions requiring on-premises deployment can develop medical models from open-weight foundations.
  </details>

- **[From Reward Engineering to Data-Centric Reinforcement Learning for Effective Agentic Visual Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28558)**  `arXiv:2611.28558`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jin Cui, Xinyue Long, Jiepeng Zhou, Boran Zhao, Pengju Ren, Hao Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly use external visual tools to acquire evidence during reasoning, yet learning when to invoke these tools remains challenging. Existing tool-use reinforcement learning (RL) methods often rely on call-contingent rewards to encourage beneficial tool use, but such reward engineering can distort the task objective and lead to degenerate calling behaviors. We argue that effective tool-use learning requires identifying not merely which questions are difficult, but which failures are attributable to missing visual evidence and can therefore be corrected through tool observations. We propose AOVO, a data-centric framework that enables MLLMs to learn effective tool use through perception attribution and policy-adaptive data sampling (PADS), without introducing tool-specific rewards. It counterfactually probes the model's reasoning states to identify observation-sensitive questions, distinguishing them from reasoning-dominated ones. PADS then periodically estimates category-level observation effects from recent policy rollouts and dynamically reallocates the training distribution toward questions where visual observations provide informative learning signals, while optimizing solely for final-answer correctness. Experiments across diverse visual perception and multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over competitive tool-use MLLMs, with causal analysis further validating the data-centric perspective for learning effective visual tool use.
  </details>

- **[Grammar-as-a-Draft: More Tokens per Speculative Decoding Step on Grammar-Constrained Structured Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28349)**  `arXiv:2611.28349`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _John Zhang, Silky Arora_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Under structured outputs (JSON schemas, regexes, tool calls), speculative drafters do not see the grammar constraint and repeatedly propose invalid keys, delimiters, and indentation, while the grammar engine already knows which tokens must come next.We present Grammar-as-a-Draft (GaaD), which treats the grammar as a second drafter. Before each verify step, the scheduler merges the drafter's proposal with grammar-forced tokens into one draft row: matching tokens stay as GRAMMAR, the proposal is cut where it leaves the forced path, and forced tokens fill the rest of a row wider than the drafter's proposal. The verify step accepts GRAMMAR drafts without computing their logits, composing with any drafter with no new kernels or compilation on the critical path. Because grammars fix text rather than tokens, committing canonical forced tokens changes the output distribution relative to grammar-masked target decoding.We implement GaaD in vllm-torchtpu and an independent Rust serving runtime, evaluating on TPU v7x with MTP, DFlash, and n-gram drafters on a JSON character-profile benchmark. GaaD raises average acceptance length from 2.06 to 5.43 (MTP, Qwen3.5-0.8B), 1.99 to 5.31 (MTP, Qwen3.5-35B-A3B), 1.57 to 4.13 (DFlash, Qwen3-4B), and 1.30 to 3.64 (n-gram, Qwen3-4B), decoding 2.66-2.81x faster at batch size 1 and 1.14-1.70x at batch size 64. In Rust, MTP+GaaD is 2.23x faster on Qwen3.5-0.8B, 2.02x on Qwen3.5-397B-A17B-FP8, and 1.22x at 128 concurrent requests. Under mixed traffic (25-50% structured), GaaD finishes the workload 1.03-1.23x sooner (structured requests 2.2-3.2x sooner) while plain-text TPOT rises 7-42%. We also show grammar-masked greedy decoding can pick non-canonical tokenizations, so GaaD can yield different valid outputs that an fp32 reference finds equally likely.
  </details>

- **[Language Model Harnesses can be Compositional Generalizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28298)**  `arXiv:2611.28298`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alex L. Zhang, Souradip Chakraborty, Omar Khattab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern language models (LMs) are post-trained and deployed as agents inside a "harness", but standard harness designs accumulate context that the neural network is forced to generalize to, making training for long-horizon tasks expensive and time-consuming. We study an alternative emerging path: training inherently neurosymbolic LM harnesses, which externalize and manage context programmatically to compose existing LM capabilities. We hypothesize that training around these harnesses can offer a better return on compute by enabling compositional generalization, as they can learn to decompose complex problems so that every observation their neural LM receives is locally in-distribution, even when the full task is novel. We find that harnesses like the Recursive Language Model (RLM) learn such decompositions readily under reinforcement learning post-training: (1) we demonstrate that training only on short tasks can generalize to tasks 2-64$\times$ longer while the base neural LM improves less, and (2) training on one domain transfers to novel domains that share latent structure. On messier, complex tasks, we find that we can target individual LLM calls within a harness by generating and training on easier, synthetically generated surrogate environments with fake tool / sub-calls, a technique we call surrogate harness training. From this finding, we train RLM-27B on synthetic data, and show it improves Qwen3.8-27B in the same RLM harness on a hard suite of long-context tasks like CTC-Bench and ExpertLongBench by 26.9% on average while using $\sim$2$\times$ fewer tokens at inference time. Finally, we show that harnesses can compose over these newly learned decompositions using recursive sub-harness calls.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking and Adapting LLMs for English-Swedish Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28050)**  `arXiv:2611.28050`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Seyed Alireza Molavi, Zhan Su, Stefan Byttner, Yan Hu, Peyman Sheikholharam Mashhadi, Prayag Tiwari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models do well on English medical exams, but less is known about Swedish. This matters most for small open models that hospitals can run locally. To reduce this gap, we first build an item-aligned English-Swedish benchmark from ten medical multiple-choice datasets; each Swedish item keeps the options and answer of its English source. A conservative refinement loop repairs the translations: open models from three families find errors, ground them in a Swedish medical term base, and keep a model-written edit only if blind judges vote for it. On this benchmark, small models lose much more accuracy in Swedish than large ones, especially when they answer without reasoning. Second, we ask whether English data and open teacher models can close this gap. We translate the training data, let teachers write reasoning traces, and fine-tune two small students on gold answers or traces in English, Swedish, or both. Training on reasoning traces that include Swedish closes the gap and lifts the 1.2B student from 23.5\% to 52.0\% in Swedish, slightly above a 20B open model in its fast mode, with a sixth of its memory. We release the benchmark, its edit history, and the code.
  </details>

- **[Hy-MT3: More Accurate, Robust and Practical Multilingual Translation Models in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27673)**  `arXiv:2611.27673`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mao Zheng, Zheng Li, Fangxu Liu, Tao Chen, Bingxin Qu, Bo Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hy-MT3 is the third generation of the Hunyuan multilingual translation model family, including \textbf{Hy-MT3-1.8B}, \textbf{Hy-MT3-7B} and \textbf{Hy-MT3-30B-A3B (MoE)}. Compared with Hy-MT2, Hy-MT3 translates \textbf{more accurately, more completely and more faithfully to user constraints}: on real-world scenario data, the share of samples with mistranslations is reduced by \textbf{43\%}, omissions or additions by \textbf{74\%}, and instruction-following errors by \textbf{40\%}, while language coverage is expanded from \textbf{38 to 102} languages. With only 3B activated parameters, \textbf{Hy-MT3-30B-A3B achieves the best overall performance among all evaluated models in non-thinking mode}, including flagship models such as Qwen3.8-Max, Kimi-K3.1-Pro and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, and approaches the strongest closed-source thinking models such as GPT-6.1-Sol and Gemini-4-Argon, while surpassing models that consume \textbf{more than 30$\times$} its inference compute. Beyond general translation, Hy-MT3 is optimized for two industrial scenarios. For \textbf{game localization}, built with high-quality game-domain data and a professional evaluation system from the Honor of Kings team, Hy-MT3-30B-A3B matches or outperforms strong closed-source models such as Gemini 3.1 Pro on all core metrics, and can be plugged into game development pipelines through an open-source toolchain. For \textbf{image translation}, Hy-MT3 improves context understanding and error correction to better tolerate OCR errors, achieving the best result among models of comparable size. Finally, we open-source \textbf{TransRubric}, a rubric-based automatic evaluation suite for translation, which outperforms other evaluation schemes on the WMT26 metrics shared task test set and is more stable with respect to human evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Non-Expanding Gated Continued Fraction Architecture for Feedforward Layers in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26835)**  `arXiv:2611.26835`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Amit Dhurandhar, Vijil Chenthamarakshan, Karthikeyan Natesan Ramamurthy, Dennis Wei, Rahul Nair_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work has shown that continued fraction inspired neural architectures (CoFrGeNets) can be highly parameter efficient yet performant for language modeling. In this work, we revisit continued fraction-based channel mixing with the goal of improving its performance and simplifying integration into standard pre-training protocols for language models. We achieve this by two simple yet critical contributions: i) we propose a novel non-expanding gated continued fraction inspired architectural component to replace feedforward layers in established language model architectures that may use vanilla multilayer perceptrons or gated linear units or (vanilla/gated) experts in mixture-of-experts (MoE) type architectures. ii) We propose a way to re-implement divisions -- the non-linearity in these architectures -- in terms of logarithms and exponentials. By experimenting on different language modeling architectures such as GPT-2, Llama-3, Mamba-2 and (nano) Llama-MoE we find that our proposed architecture performs similarly or significantly better than the continued fraction architecture proposed in prior work along with having higher throughput. In addition, our re-implementation of divisions makes training of deeper CoFrGeNets stable without the need for incremental training which was proposed in prior work as a means to stabilize training, but added notable complexity to existing large language model training implementations. Code is available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[The Silent Cost of Conversation History in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20071)**  `arXiv:2611.20071`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ali Şenol_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) in multi-turn information systems accumulate their own earlier replies and a growing task state. We asked whether this conversation history lowers accuracy, and whether process-level quality metrics warn before it does. Each analysis was fixed before its outcomes were computed. In Study 1, on 48 sharded math tasks, retaining its replies lowered final success by 0.21 for GPT-4o-mini and 0.29 for Mistral-7B (Holm-adjusted p = 0.026 and 0.004); Llama-4 Scout showed the same direction, not significant. Study 2 introduces CDR-Stream, 100-turn state-tracking dialogues with an exact answer per turn and per-model difficulty calibration. With the full history, accuracy fell from about 0.6 to 0.1 within twenty turns at matched difficulty, whereas a solver-verified state summary kept it 0.52-0.59 higher (Holm-adjusted p less than 0.001). Consistency and logical coherence fell far less than accuracy, and stability rose. None of these metrics, a paired readiness measure or calibrated single-stream drift detectors gave robust, model-general early warning, although in Study 2 the repeat-based metrics were complete only for GPT-4o-mini.
