In the monitors we tested, a single 100-turn stream had power only for very large drops. Systems built on LLMs should keep task state outside the transcript and monitor across sessions.
  </details>

- **[Toward Personalized Sleep Guidance from Wearable Data Using Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22463)**  `arXiv:2609.22463`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yusheng Tan, Running Zhao, Sofia Angel, Ninghui Hao, Ash Arian, Nikita N. Dulin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sleep monitoring using wearable data has shown promise for personal health, yet large language model (LLM)-based summarization and question answering remain insufficient for personalized sleep guidance. Training specialized models, however, often requires costly expert annotation. Moreover, privacy and accessibility concerns motivate lightweight, local deployment for end users. We present a two-stage framework to address these challenges. Specifically, in Stage~1, a multi-agent LLM pipeline reasons structured sleep guidance from unannotated wearable records, enabling scalable dataset construction. Stage~2 distills guidance reasoning trajectories into small language models (SLMs) through supervised fine-tuning and integrates a training-free Best-of-$N$ selection strategy to enhance inference. Experimental results demonstrate our method outperforms commercial general and medical LLMs and open-source models. Human evaluation further supports the quality of the generated guidance and the feasibility of personalized sleep guidance with SLMs.
  </details>

- **[Do Personality-Tuned LLMs Make Better Social Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21857)**  `arXiv:2609.21857`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tim Krabbe, Xiaodan Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs are increasingly used in social simulations for socially interactive agents and robots, offering more flexibility than rule-based systems. However, even though they mimic human behaviour very well, there is a persistent alienness to them. This work investigates whether personality-aware fine-tuning can reduce this gap by improving the consistency and controllability of personality-conditioned dialogue generation compared with instruction prompting alone. We fine-tune two small open-weight LLMs, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Ministral-8B-Instruct, using a corpus that combines personality-labelled social media posts and dialogues to create a personality-based dialogue engine for social simulation. The resulting models are evaluated across multiple social interaction scenarios using three independent LLM judges, which assess personality fidelity and provide evidence-based behavioral interpretations. We additionally quantify inter-rater agreement and lexical characteristics of the generated dialogue. Results indicate that fine-tuned models are not better at role-playing different personalities than their respective baseline models. However, low inter-rater agreement limits the confidence with which these results can be interpreted. Concerning the quality of generated texts, fine-tuned models are mostly comparable to the baselines, with fine-tuning improving the linguistic diversity of the Qwen models. While the results appear generally usable and the baseline models offer the best overall performance, future studies should place greater emphasis on the quality and domain alignment of training data for accurate personality role-playing.
  </details>

- **[More Is Not More: What Matters for Opinion Diversity in LLM Simulations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20429)**  `arXiv:2607.20429`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiyang Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to simulate diverse human opinions in open-ended tasks such as synthetic surveys, focus group modeling, and public opinion prediction. However, LLM outputs exhibit systematic opinion homogenization, and a simulation cannot reproduce a population's opinion distribution without first covering its range of viewpoints. Interventions to increase diversity are evaluated in isolation with incomparable metrics and are typically deployed and upgraded simultaneously, making it difficult to attribute gains to specific components. We design a factorial experiment that separates two primary intervention dimensions: input conditioning (operationalized through persona depth) and interaction architecture. We evaluate all conditions on 100 real-user open-ended questions across 7 models, measuring diversity with multiple complementary metrics. Our findings challenge several common assumptions. First, more persona detail does not monotonically increase diversity. The initial step of persona conditioning already captures the majority of the gain, while further elaboration with demographic detail does not consistently improve and can reduce diversity on some models; a blinded human evaluation of matched Role and Basic responses points in the same direction. Second, rather than seeking a single best interaction architecture, we find that different architectures explore largely non-overlapping opinion regions. Combining multiple architectures yields broader coverage than optimizing any one. Third, commonly attempted low-cost alternatives such as raising sampling temperature and adding diversity instructions produce negligible effects compared to structured interventions. Overall, our results indicate that, within the tested settings, diversity does not scale along any single dimension but is sensitive to the structural form and combination of interventions.
  </details>

- **[BAGEL: Benchmarking Animal Knowledge Expertise in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16241)**  `arXiv:2604.16241`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiacheng Shen, Masato Hagiwara, Milad Alizadeh, Ellen Gilsenan-McMahon, Marius Miron, David Robinson, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models perform well on broad knowledge benchmarks, yet their animal-domain competence remains poorly characterized under a consistent protocol. We introduce BAGEL (\textbf{B}enchmarking \textbf{A}nimal knowled\textbf{G}e \textbf{E}xpertise in \textbf{L}anguage models), a 6,822-question multiple-choice benchmark drawn from Wikipedia, Global Biotic Interactions (GloBI), and bioRxiv. It spans encyclopedic species knowledge, ecological interaction inference, and interpretation of animal-focused preprints. Questions are generated automatically and screened by GPT-5.5 using a rubric calibrated on 150 expert-reviewed items; this process is not exhaustive human adjudication. We evaluate 14 models without external retrieval, using model parameters and question-local evidence. Accuracy varies sharply by source and question type: frontier models that excel on Wikipedia and bioRxiv remain comparatively weak on GloBI participant identification, where pooled errors reveal ecological role confusion. A 100-item post-release bioRxiv probe provides preliminary evidence that GPT-5.4's literature performance is not entirely due to verbatim recall, without ruling out broader training exposure. Proprietary checkpoints lead the best open-weight systems overall, but aggregate scores incompletely characterize animal-domain competence.
  </details>

- **[The Token Tax: Systematic Bias in Multilingual Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.05486)**  `arXiv:2509.05486`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jessica M. Lundin, Ada Zhang, Nihal Karim, Hamza Louzan, Victor Wei, David Adelani, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tokenization inefficiency is associated with structural disadvantages for morphologically complex, low-resource languages, inflating compute resources and reducing accuracy. We evaluate 11 large language models (LLMs) on AfriMMLU (5 subjects; 16 African languages); for the 10 models whose fertility we could compute, higher token fertility is associated with lower accuracy: all 50 model--subject slopes are negative, 24 are significant at p<0.05, and 12 remain significant after false-discovery-rate correction. We further find that reasoning models (e.g., DeepSeek R1, o1) have the highest average accuracy and the smallest English-African gaps among the models evaluated. In terms of economics, a doubling in tokens results in quadrupled training cost and time, underscoring the "token tax" faced by many languages. These results motivate morphologically aware tokenization, fair pricing, and multilingual benchmarks for equitable natural language processing (NLP).
  </details>

- **[KG-TRACES: Enhancing Large Language Models with Knowledge Graph-constrained Trajectory Reasoning and Attribution Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.00783)**  `arXiv:2506.00783`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Rong Wu, Pinlong Cai, Jianbiao Mei, Licheng Wen, Tao Hu, Xuemeng Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have made remarkable strides in various natural language processing tasks, but their performance on complex reasoning problems remains hindered by a lack of explainability and trustworthiness. This issue, often manifesting as hallucinations or unattributable reasoning processes, limits their applicability in complex reasoning scenarios. To address this, we propose Knowledge Graph-constrained Trajectory Reasoning Attribution and Chain Explanation Supervision (KG-TRACES), a novel framework that enhances the reasoning ability of LLMs through explicit supervision over reasoning paths and processes. KG-TRACES jointly supervises the model to: (1) predict symbolic relation paths, (2) predict full triple-level reasoning paths, and (3) generate attribution-aware reasoning processes grounded in the reasoning paths. At inference phase, the model adapts to both KG-available and KG-unavailable scenarios, retrieving reasoning paths from a KG when possible or predicting plausible reasoning paths with only intrinsic knowledge when not. This design enables the model to reason in an explainable and source-attributable pattern. Through extensive experiments on complex reasoning tasks, we demonstrate that KG-TRACES significantly outperforms existing SOTA: it improves Hits@1 by 1.6% and F1 by 4.7% on WebQSP, and achieves improvements of 4.8% in Hits@1 and 2.1% in F1 on CWQ. Moreover, we show its transferability to specialized domains such as medicine. By visualizing the intermediate steps of reasoning processes, we further show that the explicit supervision introduced by KG-TRACES leads to more stable and goal-directed reasoning processes, aligning closely with correct answers. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fine-Tuning Large Language Models for Relation Extraction within a Retrieval-Augmented Generation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.14745)**  `arXiv:2406.14745`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sefika Efeoglu, Adrian Paschke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Information Extraction (IE) plays a pivotal role in transforming unstructured data into structured formats, such as Knowledge Graphs. One of the main tasks within IE is Relation Extraction (RE), which identifies relations between entities in text data. This process enriches the semantic understanding of documents, enabling more precise information retrieval and query answering. Recent works leveraging pre-trained language models have demonstrated significant performance improvements in RE. In the current era of Large Language Models (LLMs), fine-tuning these LLMs can mitigate the limitations of zero-shot RE methods, particularly in overcoming the domain adaptation challenges inherent in RE. This work explores not only the effectiveness of fine-tuned LLMs but also their integration into a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)-based RE approach to address domain adaptation challenges when general-purpose LLMs serve as generators within the RAG framework. Empirical evaluations on the TACRED, TACRED-Revisited (TACREV), and Re-TACRED datasets reveal substantial performance improvements with fine-tuned LLMs, such as Llama2-7B, Mistral-7B, and Flan-T5 Large and surpass previous methods on these datasets.
  </details>

- **[CART: Clustering-based Analysis of Recurring narratives for Taxonomy construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30993)**  `arXiv:2611.30993`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aggelos Semoglou, Thomas Tselentis, John Pavlopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present CART, a computational pipeline for extracting political narratives from parliamentary discourse and organizing them into a data-driven taxonomy without relying on predefined narrative categories. It encodes parliamentary discourse using semantic representations, filters relevant and reliable content through unsupervised confidence estimation, extracts narrative statements using LLMs, and uses narrative clustering and cluster analysis to construct a narrative taxonomy. Applied to Greek Parliament proceedings surrounding the 2023 Tempi train crash, it identifies ten recurring narrative families concerning responsibility attribution, institutional failure, railway safety, accountability, privatization, and the political interpretation of the tragedy. Our results demonstrate how combining unsupervised learning with LLM narrative extraction enables large parliamentary corpora to be transformed into interpretable, data-driven taxonomies of political narratives.
  </details>

- **[SARA: An Auditor-in-the-Loop LLM Recommender for Sustainability Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30839)**  `arXiv:2611.30839`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lars Hillebrand, Thiago B. F. de Oliveira, Tim Dilmaghani, Mohamed Khaled, Writwick Das, Rafet Sifa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auditing corporate sustainability reports requires assurance professionals to map a company-specific checklist drawn from roughly 1,700 regulatory requirements onto the relevant passages of lengthy, repeatedly revised draft reports and to judge compliance. We present SARA (Sustainability Reporting Assistant), a human-in-the-loop recommender system delivered as a Microsoft Word add-in that recommends, for each requirement, the passages addressing it and predicts a compliance verdict while keeping the auditor in full control. SARA casts auditing as content-based recommendation over a fixed regulatory taxonomy and makes per-requirement large language model (LLM) matching cheaper, faster, and more accurate through topic-prediction filtering: on expert-annotated reports, it reduces matching cost about sixfold and runtime by 42% versus feeding the full report to the LLM. It is in production use across PwC Germany's sustainability-assurance practice.
  </details>

- **[Hard to Translate, Harder to Detect: Human Analysis Remains Essential in AI Meme Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30710)**  `arXiv:2611.30710`  `cs.CL`  
  _Olga Zamaraeva, Carla Martínez, Carlos Gómez-Rodríguez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a dataset and evaluation setup for a problem in creative machine translation: translating culturally loaded passages that carry much of a text's meaning and humor that may depend on subtle or implicit discourse. We study Soviet comedy quotations that function as memes in Russian-language communication. Off-the-shelf difficulty estimators do not find these passages; current models searching the web find most but not all of them, but their own analyses of the difficulty do not help translation as well as a human-validated one. Given a brief human-validated analysis of why each passage is difficult, older models improve modestly; the strongest current translators (GPT-6 Sol and Claude Opus 5.5) improve more, and in human evaluation GPT-6 Sol comes close to the approved references on most films. Newer models have thus become much better at using such an analysis while still not reliably producing it themselves. We release (1) a dataset of five films' Russian subtitles with their audience-selected culturally salient passages, human-validated analyses, and reference translations (into English, Galician, and Spanish), together with every full-film LLM translation and evaluation produced in this study; and (2) an item-level MQM-style evaluation protocol that measures translator and evaluator noise to set the number of runs the evaluation needs, and whose automatic version tracks human judgment of these passages, which reference-free neural metrics do not, whether they score the whole text or the passages alone.
  </details>

- **[Empirical Margin Sensitivity: An Interventional Neuron-Level Probe of Post-Trained Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30675)**  `arXiv:2611.30675`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aniruddh Shreyas, Aarnav Kembhavi, Minal Moharir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical post-training raises benchmark accuracy, but accuracy does not show which internal components a model's decisions depend on, or whether components that support one benchmark also support another. We apply the weight-space intervention paradigm of ROME and MEMIT at the granularity of one MLP down-projection column. Our measurement, Empirical Margin Sensitivity (EMS), replaces each coordinate magnitude of a column by its mean while preserving the column's sign pattern and L1 scale, and records the resulting change in the logit margin between the correct option and its hardest-wrong competitor. This isolates the margin's dependence on a column's fine-grained coordinate structure while the column stays in place. We discover high-EMS columns (anchors) on MedQA in ten public checkpoints (four general-purpose bases and six medical post-trained variants) across the Gemma, Llama, Qwen, and AFM families, and measure the same columns on MedMCQA and PubMedQA. Mean-ablating each discovered anchor set lowers MedQA accuracy in all six post-trained models, by 6 to 28 points. In the two pure-SFT checkpoints, crushing anchors raises the PubMedQA margin on correctly answered items (negative EMS), and anchors with this property sit significantly deeper than the same model's other anchors; the four checkpoints trained with RL or mixed recipes show positive PubMedQA EMS and no significant depth shift. The depth association recurs in each of three held-out checkpoints. Because the checkpoints differ in data and pipeline as well as objective, we report these as per-checkpoint observations.
  </details>

- **[From Rewarding What Models Say to How They Think: Attention Process Reward Model for LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30627)**  `arXiv:2611.30627`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kairong Han, Nuanqiao Shan, Ziyu Zhao, Xinpeng Dong, Zijing Hu, Dian Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become an important component in large language models (LLMs). However, existing RL approaches focus on final outputs or textual solution trajectories, leading to coarse-grained rewards and unreliable reasoning. Such paradigms focus mainly on what the model outputs while overlooking how the model internally reasons. Therefore, can we reward how a model thinks internally rather than merely what it says? We first reveal the problem of textual reward aliasing in existing reward modeling approaches. Motivated by this, we propose the Attention Process Reward Model (APRM), which assigns internal rewards based on hidden states and attention maps to capture high-quality reasoning patterns. With only approximately 46M parameters, APRM is trained using ranking and counterfactual objectives, and its reward guides RL optimization while being periodically updated to handle distribution shifts. Integrated with representative RL methods, APRM achieved an average pass@8 gain of 2.88\%. Our results demonstrate the promise of incorporating internal reasoning signals into RL for LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Quantizing Diffusion LLMs under Parallel Decoding: Commitment Risk and the Parallelism Tax](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30587)**  `arXiv:2611.30587`  `cs.CL`  
  _Huan Xu, Maosen Zhao, Pengtao Chen, Tao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) are served with accelerated decoders that cache context and commit, in one step, every masked position whose confidence clears a threshold. Yet post-training quantization of dLLMs has so far been calibrated and evaluated under fixed-step samplers without caching. We study W4A4 quantization under the deployed decoder and find that the decoder determines both where quantization harms accuracy and what it costs. Irreversible errors enter chiefly through forced commitments, made when no position clears the threshold: they are a minority of commitments but carry most quantization-induced flips. Quantization also levies a parallelism tax, extra forward passes per response: by compressing top logit gaps, it pushes positions below the threshold while leaving predictions largely intact. We propose DECAL, which reweights layer-wise calibration by a commitment-risk prior measured on the deployed decoder, and an argmax-preserving commit temperature that recovers the tax. On LLaDA and Dream, DECAL attains the best average accuracy among W4A4 methods, with lower run-to-run variance than the closest existing calibration method.
  </details>

- **[When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? An Empirical Study of Decoupled Knowledge and Generation Trajectories in Clinical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30507)**  `arXiv:2611.30507`  `cs.CL`  
  _Niclas Doll, Jasper Schulze Buschhoff, Shalaka Satheesh, Héctor Allende-Cid, Katrin Klug_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual pre-training (\textit{CPT}) is a standard route for domain adaptation, yet existing studies typically track pre-training loss or multiple-choice question answering (\textit{MCQA}) accuracy alone, offering only a narrow view of how adaptation unfolds. We jointly track factual knowledge via \textit{MCQA} and open-ended behavior via pairwise preference rankings across $18$ checkpoints for each of eight instruction-tuned \textit{Qwen} models ($0.5B$ to $8B$), adapted to German medicine with \textit{CPT} and \textit{SLERP} merging. The two axes converge at consistently different rates, with preference rankings reaching $90\%$ of their total gain after a median of $1.7B$ tokens versus $3.5B$ for \textit{MCQA}. A judge-free token-distribution measure reaches this threshold at $1.9B$ tokens. Per-aspect scores indicate that these early preference gains largely reflect surface alignment, meaning models can sound domain-appropriate before their factual knowledge catches up. Larger adapted models close more of the gap to unadapted next-tier generalists, whereas preference gain per FLOP falls by about an order of magnitude across our size range. We therefore argue that judge-based preference scores, which can plateau while \textit{MCQA} knowledge is still improving, should not be used alone to infer adaptation convergence or domain readiness.
  </details>

- **[When Does Reasoning Help Entity Disambiguation? Small LLMs In-Domain and Under Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30334)**  `arXiv:2611.30334`  `cs.CL`  
  _Samy Haffoudhi, Fabian M Suchanek, Nils Holzenberger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Entity disambiguation (mapping ambiguous mentions in text to entities in a knowledge base) is a foundational step in tasks such as knowledge graph construction, question answering, and information extraction. Recent work has applied LLMs to this problem, achieving stateof-the-art performance. Notably, reasoning LLMs have been shown to perform entity disambiguation in a true zero-shot fashion, generalizing across knowledge bases and domains. However, it is unclear whether this capability survives, or remains competitive, with smaller LLMs, where entity disambiguation has typically relied on reranking rather than reasoning. We ask when reasoning is worth its cost at small scale. We compare pointwise, listwise, and reasoning-based candidate-selection components within a classical coarse-to-fine entity disambiguation pipeline, across the classical, few-shot and true zero-shot settings. The reasoning paradigm is trained with distillation and reinforcement learning recipes. The benefit of reasoning grows with domain shift: negligible in-domain, decisive in the true zero-shot setting. We show that two distinct factors are at work: the reranker's uncertainty over the candidates locates where the paradigms disagree, while shift governs which is right when they do.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free Interventions for Hallucination in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29909)**  `arXiv:2611.29909`  `cs.CL`  
  _Can Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A frozen language model can give a wrong answer because a fact is unavailable, a reliable passage is ignored, or an unsupported claim survives generation. We review 116 works on inference-time hallucination intervention and its foundations. We trace the development from retrieval and controllable generation to methods that act on input evidence, token scores, internal representations, completed claims, or the decision to answer. For each family we compare its source of evidence, model access, preparation cost, and the task on which a correction can be established. For activation steering, we distinguish estimating a subspace from applying a state-dependent edit and separate interventions on the prompt from those on the generated prefix. Published comparisons show why faithfulness to a source, factual correctness, informativeness, and answer coverage must be measured separately. Applications in time-sensitive question answering, document synthesis, and visual grounding identify when source authority, evidence availability, or capability preservation limits a reported gain.
  </details>

- **[GlobalHRBench: Does Changing the Question Language Change the Legal Answer?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29852)**  `arXiv:2611.29852`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuding Wang, Yuhang Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language matters when deploying LLM agents across borders, especially in law, where the governing rules must hold across working languages. Does changing the language of a legal question change answer accuracy? We introduce GlobalHRBench, pairing 240 employment-law problems across eight jurisdictions and eight languages while fixing facts, applicable law and required conclusions. We evaluate nine models on all 1,920 prompts in independent, closed-book, single-answer trials, scoring legal correctness, completeness and answer-language compliance. GPT-6.1 Sol leads at 76.2% strict passes, with a 5.8% relative best-to-worst language decline, compared with 42.2% for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash. Kimi K3.1 Pro and Claude Sonnet 5.5 reverse order between English and Chinese. A four-model study on 16 paired families compares closed-book answers with governing evidence, added cross-jurisdiction distractors and removal of a governing passage. Governing evidence corrects legal errors and shortens reasoning on recovered answers, yet recognizing an evidence gap does not ensure correct application of the rules that remain. These findings motivate multilingual training for accurate, efficient and evidence-supported answers, and deployment evaluation in the user's working language.
  </details>

- **[ComplexIF: A Challenging Multi-Turn, Multi-Constraint Instruction-Following Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29775)**  `arXiv:2611.29775`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhichao Xu, Bingsen Chen, Yawei Wang, Tao Li, Haoyu Wang, Dan Roth, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As frontier LLMs saturate existing instruction-following benchmarks, measuring further progress requires harder tests that remain faithful to real use. In practice, users often combine several constraints, add them at different turns of a conversation, and expect all of them to hold in the assistant's response. We introduce ComplexIF, a challenging benchmark for this setting, together with the automatic pipeline that generates it. The pipeline expands seed requests into multi-turn conversations, weaves constraints into their turns, and keeps only tasks that a strong model can solve, so difficulty can be raised by adding constraints or turns while every task stays solvable. ComplexIF's two splits test complementary sides of instruction following: a verifiable split of format constraints, checked by programs, and a rubric split of semantic instructions, such as a tone or an audience, checked with an LLM judge. Across 14 proprietary and open-weight models, even the strongest satisfies every constraint on fewer than half of the verifiable tasks, and no model leads on both splits.
  </details>

- **[Diversity in Unity, Theory in Practice: Hierarchical Multitask Benchmarks for Chinese Minority Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29635)**  `arXiv:2611.29635`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yijie Li, Xi Cao, Yuan Sun, Quulgan Minggad, Abdulla Ablikim, JiaQingCaiWang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the rapid advancement of LLMs, their performance on linguistically and culturally diverse minority languages within a unified national context remains underexplored. We present CMiLBench, a collection of hierarchical multitask benchmarks designed to translate theoretical notions of "diversity in unity" into practical evaluation for three representative Chinese minority languages: Tibetan, Mongolian, and Uyghur. CMiLBench comprises 24,663 instances across 5 difficulty levels and 17 tasks spanning foundational ability, cultural specificity, and safety alignment. We adopt existing dataset adaptation, minority knowledge construction, and high-resource benchmark translation to construct CMiLBench. We assess 14 state-of-the-art commercial and open-source LLMs with a hybrid framework that integrates automatic metrics and LLM-as-a-Judge scoring. The comparative experimental results reveal the gap between theoretical capability and practical utility. CMiLBench serves as a foundational and scalable evaluation resource to bridge the digital language divide and promote the informatization and intelligentization of low-resource Chinese minority languages.
  </details>

- **[Explainable Emotion and Mental Health Reasoning in Large Language Models Using Theories of Emotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29628)**  `arXiv:2611.29628`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tadesse Destaw Bela, Iqra Ameer, Helena Gómez Adorno, Alexander Gelbukh, Eusebio Ricardez Vázquez, Olga Kolesnikova, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have enabled a wide range of applications. However, their ability to simulate human-like behavior and reasoning remains unclear. While emotion detection is a better-established Natural Language Processing (NLP) task, existing approaches primarily focus on directly classifying text into predefined emotion classes, often overlooking the appraisal process that explains why an individual experiences a particular emotion in a given situation. In this work, we propose Psychology-based Chain-of-Thought (PsyCoT) reasoning, a theory-driven prompting strategy grounded in the Schachter-Singer and Cognitive Appraisal theories of emotion. We propose and evaluate PsyCoT for subjective NLP tasks, including emotion analysis, emotion intensity prediction, stress analysis, and depression severity identification. Our evaluation covers four levels of classification complexity: binary, multiclass, multi-label, and ordinal scaling, spanning 11 languages. We assess the performance of five state-of-the-art LLMs across these selected subjective NLP tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that PsyCoT prompt variants improve the standard chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting across the evaluated tasks. Across all evaluated tasks, PsyCoT yields F1 score gains ranging from 0.5% to 28.1%, depending on the model, task, and language configuration.
  </details>

- **[Estimating Silenced Tokens in Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29536)**  `arXiv:2611.29536`  `cs.CL`  
  _Giuseppe Pappalardo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantization is the standard way to shrink large language models, and it is usually judged by perplexity and task accuracy. We ask a different question: after quantization, which words can the model still say at all? A token is silenced when no hidden state can make it the top choice of the output layer, so a greedy decoder can never produce it. We give a simple, scalable way to estimate how many tokens a quantized output head silences, combining three checks: a direction that lets a token win (so it is safe), an upper bound on how close a token's row is to being trapped inside the others, and an exact match that proves a token can never win. Each count is therefore a certified range: exact matches give its lower end and flagged tokens can only raise its upper end. In nearly all settings we report down to 3 bits the two ends coincide, so the counts are exact. Across three open models we find that quantization silences tokens: on large-vocabulary models it already appears at 8-bit precision, up to a few hundred previously reachable tokens on a 1B-parameter model, and grows as precision drops, while a small-vocabulary model resists until 3 bits. The loss is far from uniform across languages: on a multilingual vocabulary the silenced tokens are overwhelmingly non-Latin (Korean, Chinese, Thai, Arabic and Hebrew), even though Latin tokens dominate the vocabulary. Silencing is thus a measurable, language-sensitive side effect that standard averages miss, and it should be reported alongside them.
  </details>

- **[GraphSeer: Multimodal Knowledge Graph Relational Image Retrieval with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29470)**  `arXiv:2611.29470`  `cs.CL`  
  _Linyu Li, Zhi Jin, Yuanpeng He, Haoran Duan, Dongming Jin, Wenchun Jing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal knowledge graphs (MMKGs) store entities with photos and names and link them by facts. MMKG methods predict links between known entity IDs, and image retrievers, from CLIP to MLLM embedders, return entities that look like a query photo or match its description. Neither answers a common question about a new photo: which graph entity is related to the one shown, such as the team of a pictured player or the city of a landmark. We call this knowledge graph relational image retrieval (KRIR): given a photo and a natural-language relation, retrieve the related MMKG entity, which rarely resembles the photo, whether the depicted entity is in the graph (known) or not (unseen). We build three benchmarks from YAGO, Wikidata and in-the-wild OVEN photos. The task hides two hops: recognising which graph entity is shown, and recalling what it is connected to. GraphSeer bridges them inside one multimodal LLM: it links the photo to the graph, reads the facts of the best match, and retrieves with the hidden state at which the model is about to say the answer. An unseen entity has no facts of its own, but every candidate answer does, so a listwise verifier reads the other end of the graph: it re-ranks the top candidates by their facts and photos in one forward pass, and the facts matter far more than the photos. With Qwen3.5-27B, GraphSeer improves MRR on known entities by 24.2-70.0% over an MLLM embedder fine-tuned on the same data with the same budget. When it also fuses and verifies the embedder's candidates, it improves MRR on unseen entities by 12.5-25.6%, and on known entities it beats KG-completion models given the ground-truth entity ID.
  </details>

- **[Can Speech LLMs Recognize When Audio Evidence Is Insufficient? A Diagnostic Study and a Pre-Generation Probing Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29393)**  `arXiv:2611.29393`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vladislav Nesterenko, Polina Doronicheva, Olga Golubeva, Polina Sheviakova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speech large language models are usually evaluated on questions that have answers. We ask what they do when a recording establishes none, and whether the standard way of finding out is sound. Diagnosed on synthesized speech the answer looks clear: Qwen2-Audio fabricates an answer to 92.5% of unanswerable questions, while an ASR cascade reading the same recordings reaches 2.5% hallucination at 6.7% over-refusal, placing the failure in reasoning rather than in perception. The identical protocol on 376 native SQuAD 2.0 questions over human-read recordings removes that conclusion: the cascade hallucinates on 67.7% against the end-to-end model's 69.8%, at a higher over-refusal cost. A paired subset asking the same questions of a synthesized and a human recording of each passage localises the effect in the ASR stage -- the end-to-end model's abstention behaviour does not move with the speaker, the cascade's goes from 11.1% to 65.9%. Reliance on synthesized speech has been named as a limitation of audio benchmarks but not quantified; we quantify it, and show it large enough to reverse a finding. Since no prompt we tested repairs the residual failure, we transfer pre-generation attention probing to the multimodal setting. A probe over frozen prompt representations reaches 0.773 AUROC at predicting which unanswerable questions the model will answer anyway, where semantic entropy over ten sampled generations is at chance: the model fabricates the same answer every time, so sample disagreement cannot see a failure it is confident about.
  </details>

- **[Automatic Construction of Persian E-Commerce Dialogue Datasets for Conversational Product Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29356)**  `arXiv:2611.29356`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fariba Nafar, Hassan Naderi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational product search lets users find products through a multi-turn dialogue instead of a single keyword query, but Persian lacks the large conversational datasets needed to train and evaluate such systems; existing Persian e-commerce dialogue resources cover few categories and small catalogs. We present a framework that builds such a dataset automatically from a real e-commerce catalog and release the resulting dataset. The framework crawls 12,926 products in seven categories of the Digikala marketplace, groups them into 170 semantically coherent clusters, and selects the question the assistant asks at every turn with the Regularized Partition Balance (RPB) score. The RPB score accounts for feature coverage, the probability that the user can answer and the number of values, and generalizes entropy-based question selection. An LLM then verbalizes each question plan into a natural Persian dialogue. In a simulation on the full catalog, the RPB score needs 0.11 fewer turns than the strongest entropy baseline when the difficulty of answering differs between features, and is practically equivalent to it when it does not. Using only a free LLM API, we generated 1,000 requirement-driven dialogues covering all 170 clusters, with turn-level feature annotations; automatic checks and an LLM judge confirm their consistency with the catalog, while their realism is lower than that of the first, GPT-4o-generated version. Code and data are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Free-Form Generation: Measuring Interface Composition in Competitive NLP Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29103)**  `arXiv:2611.29103`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinchen Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Systems with decoder LLMs can combine free generation with scoring, ranking, or selection. We measure these documented interface combinations with five annotation fields linked to source evidence. The corpora comprise 517 solutions from 42 Kaggle competitions involving NLP and 76 participant systems from 26 SemEval, TREC, and WMT task years. Among eligible generation systems with decoder LLMs, 18 of 55 Kaggle systems and 17 of 20 Shared Task systems are hybrid: free generation coexists with a constrained interface. The remaining systems use free generation alone. Giving each competition or task year equal weight yields hybrid shares of 0.327 and 0.876. Within Eedi, 21 eligible systems with decoder LLMs have five exact interface profiles; ten LLM Science Exam systems share one interface class and have four profiles. Across Kaggle, the eligible systems with decoder LLMs span 18 exact interface profiles, revealing variation within broad task and backbone categories. These estimates characterize documented public systems selected using our selection criteria and include interfaces throughout the complete pipeline, including intermediate stages. The data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HyperHarness: Learning to Generate Personalized Agent Harnesses from User Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29062)**  `arXiv:2611.29062`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhaoxuan Tan, Jiachen Zhao, Jingbo Yang, Zheyuan Liu, Chuhan Wang, Pei Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized LLM agents rely on an agent harness, the surrounding memory and control code, to connect user history with downstream decisions. Existing methods typically employ either a single shared memory workflow across all users, which is efficient to deploy but cannot adapt how evidence is retrieved and interpreted across diverse histories, or per-user harness optimization, which customizes behavior for each user but requires costly repeated candidate evaluation at deployment and suffers from sparse, noisy supervision in raw logs. To address this trade-off, we present HyperHarness, a framework that learns a shared history-to-harness generator to synthesize executable, user-specific retrieval and answer-guidance code directly from user histories. Given a user's permitted history, HyperHarness prompts a construction LLM to write personalized retrieval and guidance functions, while downstream task scores across training users supervise iterative refinements to the shared generator without reference harnesses or answering-model updates. At deployment, the frozen generator constructs a harness for each unseen user in a single pass from history alone, eliminating per-user task-scored search. Across three benchmarks (PersonaMemv3, Mem-PAL, and MemoryCD) and three answering models (Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3.8-27B, and GPT-5.6-Luna), HyperHarness achieves the highest aggregate score in seven of nine settings and ranks second in the remaining two, improving over the strongest baselines by up to 5.95 points while cutting matched answer-time tokens by 90.32% over long-context prompting. Analyses show that optimization tailors context allocation to each executor, transfers to unseen user clusters, and amortizes adaptation costs.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Collaborative Agents in Group Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29036)**  `arXiv:2611.29036`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhaoxuan Tan, Mengting Wan, Jingbo Yang, Kwei-Herng Lai, Renyi Qu, Lorenzo Jaime Flores, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collaborative agents joining an ongoing team rely on shared context to connect multi-party interaction histories with downstream coordination decisions. Existing work typically focuses either on retrieving historical facts from conversation logs, which surfaces isolated evidence without capturing who knows or owns what, or on evaluating end-to-end interactions, which conflates preparation with execution and lacks direct supervision because a team's history has no single ground-truth summary. To bridge this gap, we formulate group briefing, the task of reconstructing task-relevant group cognition across participants' expressed beliefs, group dynamics, and grounding gaps, and introduce a four-domain benchmark of 1,351 requests and 9,975 verified reference points paired with a two-stage evaluation pipeline combining reference-point coverage and downstream collaboration simulation. Using this pipeline, we show that GroupBrief, a simple task-guided approach that iteratively probes the message history and synthesizes a structured brief without offline memory indexing or graph construction, outperforms ten retrieval and agent-memory baselines in overall coverage with gpt-5.4-mini, while benchmarking nine language models reveals that prospective friction remains harder to recover than past state across model scales. We further use reference-point coverage as a feedback signal for harness optimization, refining GroupBrief's retrieval and synthesis instructions while keeping model weights frozen. On held-out Test projects, GroupBrief (optimized) improves coverage by 2.54 percentage points for gpt-5.4-mini and 12.88 for gpt-5.6-luna, achieving 64.46% and 68.42% preference over no brief in downstream simulations. These results establish our benchmark and evaluation pipeline as a foundation for measuring and optimizing how collaborative agents ground in group cognition.
  </details>

- **[EmoRouter: Adaptive Modality Cascading for Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28960)**  `arXiv:2611.28960`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fangxu Yu, Lingzhi Yuan, Dehai Min, Tao Feng, Ge Liu, Furong Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Emotion Recognition (MER) involves detecting the underlying emotion behind each utterance within a conversation, which is essential for emotionally aware human-computer interaction. While omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) demonstrate strong capability for MER by jointly processing modalities including text, audio, and vision, different modality combinations yield different prediction outcomes across utterances: acoustic and visual inputs can help recover annotated labels missed by text-only inference, but can also turn correct predictions into errors. Processing all modalities for every utterance is also computationally expensive. To this end, we introduce EmoRouter, a training-free, model-agnostic framework for adaptive modality cascading that selects an exit tier for each utterance along a predefined modality order. The routing is guided by prediction-uncertainty estimates derived from Conformal Prediction. EmoRouter routes each utterance through a sequence of increasing-cost modality levels and exits at the first level when the model is confident in the emotion. To determine when to exit, we propose Conformal Set Contraction, a structural calibration criterion that calibrates the per-tier CP error budgets used for these exit decisions. Comprehensive evaluations on 4 MER benchmarks and 3 Omni-LLMs reveal that EmoRouter matches or outperforms the full modalities while reducing inference latency by 67.9--94.5%. Furthermore, EmoRouter demonstrates high transferability across different models, validating its robustness. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Thakaa at StanceEval-2026: Inference-Time Target Grounding for Arabic Stance Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28860)**  `arXiv:2611.28860`  `cs.CL`  
  _Meshal Alamr, Hassan Alqaeri, Abdullah Aldahlawi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  StanceEval-2026 tests Arabic stance classification under target shift, with evaluation targets absent from the labelled task data. We test two ways to supply target information at inference time. For Track 1, we retrieve eight labelled Women Driving demonstrations from the external ArabicStanceX corpus and score the three label tokens with Gemma-4-31B-IT. For the unseen-target track, we prepend cached target context prompted to be factual and non-evaluative, then fuse label predictions and label-token scores from multiple LLMs, with final assignments constrained by published class totals. The systems obtain 85.22 and 91.78 Favg2 on Tracks 1 and 2, ranking sixth and fourth in the final standings. On held-out ArabicStanceX tweets, direct prompting scores 79.45 Favg2; eight random Women Driving demonstrations raise it to 84.78, and similarity retrieval reaches 89.04. Across three target-held-out Mawqif evaluations, a fixed target card raises Gemma-4-31B-IT from 86.54 to 86.93, whereas QLoRA lowers Gemma-4-12B-IT from 84.43 to 83.77. In these separate public evaluations, same-target demonstrations and retrieval improve performance, factual target context provides small, target-dependent gains, and the tested source-only adapter does not improve.
  </details>

- **[Thakaa at AlexandriaX-2026: Who Gets the Final Say? Assigning Output Authority in Dialectal Arabic Machine Translation and Error Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28857)**  `arXiv:2611.28857`  `cs.CL`  
  _Meshal Alamr, Maryam Altuwaijri, Hassan Alqaeri, Abdullah Aldahlawi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We report first-place systems for all four evaluated AlexandriaX-2026 settings: both English-to-dialect translation tracks, cross-dialect translation, and translation-error detection. In unconstrained translation, our minimum Bayes risk (MBR) selection restricts output authority: four local systems can supply the translation, while seven additional systems contribute only reference evidence. Candidate-constrained MBR beats the strongest complete candidate in all 13 dialects and gains 1.2554 macro spBLEU overall, reaching 33.4891. It also surpasses a retrospective oracle selecting the best whole candidate system per dialect. In the constrained track, country-routed LoRA adapters over a shared NileChat-3B-Base backbone incorporate the released Libyan and Sudanese supervision absent from the training and development splits, raising macro spBLEU from 29.5969 to 30.4233. Post-evaluation, a single adapter trained on all released Alexandria data reaches 30.4733 macro spBLEU; averaging model weights across three training seeds raises this to 30.9726 while retaining one model for inference. For cross-dialect translation, nonparallel target-dialect retrieval guides deliberation, raising Gemma-4-31B-IT without fine-tuning from 24.6898 to 28.4011 spBLEU. For translation-error detection, GPT-5.5 proposes semantic error regions, while MARBERTv2 controls their character boundaries and corroborates additional spans, raising Overall from 0.4691 to 0.4982.
  </details>

- **[BIRD-Persona: A Persona-Aware Benchmark for SQL and pandas Analytics Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28542)**  `arXiv:2611.28542`  `cs.CL`  
  _Davin Hill, Pawan Chorasiya, Bhawani S. Leelar, Oren Avram, Robert E. Tillman, Tamer Soliman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Production text-to-code systems use large language models to turn natural-language analytics requests into executable SQL and pandas code. Existing benchmarks cover both tasks but do not isolate when performance diverges between them. We introduce BIRD-Persona, a persona-stratified extension of BIRD-Python that adds novice and expert rewrites to tasks with matched SQL and pandas ground truth, along with schema variants and task-structure annotations. Across 1,534 tasks, nine models, and 245,592 evaluations, SQL outperforms pandas for eight of nine systems. The gap varies by condition: expert, schema-explicit prompts improve both paradigms but help SQL more; schema representations have mixed, model-dependent effects; and selected-column burden widens the gap while subqueries do not. By making user phrasing a controlled benchmark dimension, BIRD-Persona supports persona-aware and paradigm-aware evaluation of natural-language analytics systems. The dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Information Access and Gain-Loss Framing in Six Large Language Models and Human Decision Makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28525)**  `arXiv:2611.28525`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aadithya Chandramouli, Jeremy M. Gernand_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A risky choice can be described by what will be saved or what will be lost, and people often choose differently depending on that wording. We test whether six large language models (LLMs) show this gain-loss framing effect, and whether it shrinks when they can first read more about the options. GPT-4.1, GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Gemini 2.5 Flash, Qwen3 235B and Llama 4 Maverick made 11,519 choices across four risk scenarios, each in its original wording, two paraphrases and a mirrored version in the opposite wording. Before choosing, a model could open up to 0, 2, 3 or 4 of seven information cards. With no cards, all six models showed large framing effects: the share choosing the safe option was 27 to 79 percentage points lower under loss than under gain wording. The gap was smaller at 2 cards than with no cards in every model. Against a 10-point threshold set in our analysis plan, GPT-4.1 fell within it at 4 cards and Qwen3 only at 2 cards; in the plan's pooled comparison over all versions, GPT-4.1 was within it from 2 cards and GPT-4o from 3. GPT-4o-mini and Llama 4 Maverick kept gaps of 28 to 66 points with cards, and for GPT-4o-mini and Qwen3 the gap grew again from 2 to 4 cards. Part of the drop from 0 to 2 cards may reflect a change in prompt format, and most card content counted against the risky option, which may favor the safe choice. In an exploratory comparison with 198 students who saw the same scenarios and cards but opened cards one at a time and chose how many, decisions made after opening 0 or 1 cards showed a 19-point difference between the gain scenarios, asked first, and the loss scenarios, asked later, and no clear difference appeared in decisions made after opening more, though this design cannot show that cards reduced framing in people. With these cards, in the balanced comparison 17 of 18 card gaps were below the model's no-card gap, but few were within the threshold.
  </details>

- **[SupportChain: Trace-Calibrated Aggregation of Quote-Grounded Reasoning Chains for Multi-Hop RAG with Small Readers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28519)**  `arXiv:2611.28519`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhexi Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Strong results in multi-hop retrieval-augmented question answering come from graph indexes read by models of 70B parameters or more, and building such an index calls a large language model over the whole corpus. We present SupportChain, which splits a question into hops, binds each hop's answer to a sentence quoted from a retrieved passage, estimates how reliable each execution is from its own trace, and merges several executions by the support of their answers, the pooled reliability of the executions that agree, stopping once support is high enough. With a 9B reader under the HippoRAG~2 protocol, SupportChain reaches 63.0 F1 on MuSiQue, above the best earlier result of 59.2, which used a 20B reader. With the same reader and embedder, it is significantly better than HippoRAG~2 on all five datasets, by up to 17.2 F1, and it builds no index. Merging by support is significantly better than majority voting, and than letting the reader choose among the candidates, on three of the five datasets and level with both on the other two. The reliability model needs only 100 answered training questions.
  </details>

- **[Muted Distillation: Sealing Reasoning from Teacher Unlocks Token-Efficient Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28445)**  `arXiv:2611.28445`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuwei Zhang, Ian Miao, Wentao Zhang, Chen Qu, Bill Bi, Jingbo Shang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is commonly adopted as one of the components in post-training of large language models (LLMs), but in multi-turn agentic tasks we find that the student model increases their reasoning lengths after training. Our empirical analysis shows that the reasoning token lengths is affected by how much the student can distract the teacher's scores. We introduce Muted Distillation (MD), a simple variant of OPD that hides the student's reasoning from the teacher, so the teacher judges what the student does without the interference of student's reasonings. MD takes a few lines of code and adds no extra data or hyperparameters. On the knowledge-work benchmarks OfficeQA Pro and SpreadsheetBench, it reduces output tokens per episode by 57\% and 53\% compared with standard OPD, making the student more concise than it was before training. At the same time, it matches OPD's accuracy on OfficeQA Pro and improves pass@1 by 4.8 points on SpreadsheetBench.
  </details>

- **[Language models compress human preference geometry while political structure remains prompt-sensitive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28383)**  `arXiv:2611.28383`  `cs.CL`  
  _Adolfo Ignacio Fuentes-Jofre, Cristian Candia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to construct synthetic publics. Prior work has documented errors in individual responses, population heterogeneity, and prompt robustness, but whether these failures reflect a common loss of fidelity or distinct properties of synthetic populations remains unclear. We tested this using matched pairwise policy choices from 1,991 Chilean, 843 French, and 434 Brazilian adults replayed through three open-weight model families. Across a 27-condition Chilean grid and cross-country factorial replications, no condition jointly reproduced individual choices, population heterogeneity, and political organization. The most consistent result was compression of the latent preference space: synthetic participant variance was only 0.4-10.5% of the corresponding human reference in the primary comparisons and remained below human variance in all 270 matched refits. Political structure was much more sensitive to how choices were elicited. Reversing proposal position changed 12.3-31.2% of decoded choices. Explicitly labeling the endpoints of the numeric left-right self-placement scale changed 20.5-28.1% of choices and moved an independent proposal-level measure of left-right cleavage toward the human pattern in all three settings. Yet matched agreement improved by only 3.1-4.2 percentage points and participant geometry remained compressed. In Chile, the same intervention compressed the synthetic population further. These results separate political legibility from population fidelity: language models can recover salient political divisions while failing to preserve the breadth of human preference variation. Synthetic-public fidelity is therefore a property of the checkpoint, prompt, and setting, not of a checkpoint alone, and must be evaluated separately at individual, population, and political-structure levels.
  </details>

- **[Frontier models need only one text to unmask its human or AI author](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26216)**  `arXiv:2611.26216`  `cs.CL`  
  _Atrey Desai, Deniz Bölöni-Turgut, Ty Brassington, Minjoon Choi, Jenna Russell, Rishanth Rajendhran, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a single text and a simple zero-shot prompt (no web access, tools, candidate lists, or writing samples), frontier models can answer "who wrote this?" at three increasingly specific levels: (1) whether an AI or human wrote it, (2) if an AI, which lab, and (3) if a human, often exactly who. The latest models (Claude Fable 5.2, GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 4 Carbon) match trained detectors like Pangram while also explaining their predictions in natural language, and Opus 5.5 names the correct lab for 98% of texts generated by models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google. Most strikingly, Fable 5.2 correctly names the author of 55% of 15K anonymous ICML 2020 reviews that were never made public, and identifies the username behind 69% of comments from active Redditors (with >500 posts over the last year). Accuracy depends on exposure to an author's writing in pretraining data and on reasoning effort, and simply asking a model how familiar it is with someone predicts whether it can identify their writing. For anyone who has published enough writing online, frontier models present a greater threat to anonymity than commonly assumed, and defenses are limited.
  </details>

- **[Catch It Where It Happens: Real-Time Error Correction During LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13107)**  `arXiv:2611.13107`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rotem Elimelech, Michael Shalyt, Ido Kaminer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted with mathematical, scientific and engineering work, yet their mistakes are often confidently written and hard to spot. A single wrong step tends to propagate, making the model waste inference tokens following a false premise. Existing safeguards either check only the final answer and restart from scratch, or rely on a verifier LLM that competes for GPU capacity and slows down response time. We propose online, deterministic verification: many kinds of reasoning steps can be validated by code, so a step can be verified in real time, as it is written, without any model call or GPU use. Whenever a check fails, we point out the mistake and let the model continue from the same spot. We demonstrate this approach on symbolic mathematics, where derivations already contain explicit expressions that a computer algebra system can check in milliseconds. Tested on the ASyMoB benchmark with three open-weights models, our online validation improves accuracy by 1.4-5.6 percentage points, corresponding to a 1.8-14.9% net reduction in incorrect answers, while increasing token usage by only 6-7%. Because the verifier is independent of the model, the approach complements progress in LLMs rather than competing with it: every new model can benefit immediately, and the benefit grows as deterministic checkers extend to more kinds of reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[Towards Foundation Decision Models: Drex](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07854)**  `arXiv:2611.07854`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nischay Dhankhar, Alisher Amantay, Zhanibek Datbayev, Darkhan Akhmetov, Yash Shah, Nurmukhamed Ubaidullayev, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive approaches with large language models, such as long chain-of-thought reasoning, have become the default for new AI applications because they reach higher accuracy on a wide range of tasks. In production, however, they give inconsistent answers, require parsing of outputs, and add cost and latency. Decision models address this: given a question and a set of options supplied by the user, they return a probability distribution over the options without autoregressive decoding. We present Drex, a family of four open decision models with 8B (DLM), 9B (v1.5), 35B with 3B active (v2.0), and 27B (Max) parameters. Each request consists of a state (a document, record, or agent context, optionally with images) and questions about it, each asking for a choice among options, a yes/no judgment, or a score on an ordered scale. Training combines supervised fine-tuning with reinforcement learning from adjusted feedback (RLAF), plus vision tuning for Max. Ablations show that RLAF improves accuracy and that consistency losses improve robustness to option order. On Decision Index 0.4.1, Max sets a new state of the art among the decision models we compare (67.52 on the public suite vs. 60.70 for Jev 1.14.0). On Decision Bench v4, it is statistically tied with d3-turbo and GPT-6 Sol and trails Claude Opus 5.5 by 1.6 points, and it has the highest accuracy among decision models on DecideBench. Max answers a typical request in 183 ms, against about 2.0 s for GPT-6 Sol and 3.5 s for Claude Opus 5.5, and its outputs are stable under option reordering. All four models are open source:this https URL
  </details>

- **[CompSkill: Compiling Execution Experience for Skill Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01115)**  `arXiv:2611.01115`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qi Shen, Ziyue Zhu, Yongqing Jiang, Zhen Xia, Li Shengjie, Shuai Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly rely on skill documents, yet most retrievers rank them by description alone and ignore what earlier executions revealed, even though a relevant skill can hurt execution. We present CompSkill, which indexes skills with learned 512-d keys and compiles matched same-task comparisons into skill offsets and capped key residuals (KeyOpt), so experience changes which document the executor receives, never its text. Under a logistic outcome model with unknown task difficulty, matched comparisons cancel that difficulty and only discordant ones carry information; on support executions, predictions improve with each discordant task. An experience hypergraph keeps each comparison with its executor and document versions, recalls missed skills and grounds a cited review. Compact keys raise SkillRet NDCG@10 from 64.0 to 70.9 at one eighth of the dimension. With one document, CompSkill completes 256 and 260 of 332 ToolQA tasks with GPT-5.6-luna and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, respectively, more than every baseline, and stays on par with the strongest baselines on AppWorld and ALFWorld. On 332 newly generated ToolQA tasks, it completes 256 with GPT-5.6-luna, significantly more than BM25, SkillRet-8B, Tool2Vec and RankGPT; over all 844 ToolQA tasks, it completes 24 more than the strongest baseline. Selection matters beyond relevance: pairing the right guide with a same-database sibling instead of another database's guide costs 8 of 96 tasks. Code, models and support data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[EASE: Entropy-Adaptive Distribution Shaping for Evading AI-generated Text Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09976)**  `arXiv:2610.09976`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jicheng Zhou, Kahim Wong, Jialong Wang, Jiantao Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-generated text (AIGT) detection can be sensitive to the decoding choices of the source large language model (LLM). We observe that perturbing next-token logits or adjusting sampling temperature can reduce detection performance, providing a clear signal of detector vulnerability to decoding-time distribution changes. Building on this observation, we propose EASE (Entropy-Adaptive Distribution Shaping for Evasion), a training-free and detector-agnostic framework for evading AIGT detectors. EASE computes predictive entropy directly from the source LLM's next-token distribution and uses it to adapt both logit perturbation and sampling temperature, without detector feedback or model fine-tuning. Experiments across three source LLMs and multiple detectors demonstrate consistent reductions in detection performance, with negligible degradation in text quality and negligible inference overhead.
  </details>

- **[The Labeling Problem in Hallucination Detection Benchmarks: An Empirical Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08026)**  `arXiv:2610.08026`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jorma Valjakka, Juhani Kivimäki, Juha Mylläri, Jukka K. Nurminen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, several methods for detecting when large language models (LLMs) hallucinate have been developed. These methods are often benchmarked with open-domain question answering (QA) datasets containing questions and corresponding short reference answers. First, an LLM is used to generate answers to questions within the QA dataset. Then, some automated labeling strategy is used to label these answers as hallucinated or not by comparing them with the reference answers in the dataset. This evaluation setting creates a methodological ambiguity between two criteria: reference faithfulness (whether the answer is fully supported by the reference) and factual correctness (whether the answer is free from contradictions and factually false specific claims). In practice, automated labelers may apply the former criterion even when the intended target is the latter. We study this potential criterion mismatch using 900 human-labeled question-answer pairs spanning three commonly used QA datasets and three generator models, with labels targeting answer-level factual correctness. We evaluate lexical similarity metrics, a reference-entailment NLI baseline, and seven LLM judges under controlled prompt variants as automated labelers. Our experiments reveal substantial disagreement both among automated labeling strategies and between these labels and human annotations. Many strategies also exhibit strong directional error biases, and for most judge-generator pairs, replacing a faithfulness-oriented prompt with a factual-correctness prompt improves agreement with human annotations and reduces false-positive dominance, indicating that automated hallucination labels depend strongly on how the target criterion is specified. Label-source choice should therefore be considered a fundamental part of benchmark design and made explicit, validated, and matched with the benchmark goal.
  </details>

- **[Load-Bearing Context: The Question Damage Score for Evaluating Context Reliance in Linguistic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27756)**  `arXiv:2608.27756`  `cs.CL`  
  _Neh Majmudar, Elena Filatova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Determining whether large language models derive answers from context or prior knowledge remains a fundamental challenge. Self-contained linguistic olympiad puzzles provide a controlled setting where all answers derive solely from expert-designed context examples without external knowledge. Removing individual context examples can eliminate information needed for specific questions while leaving the rest of the puzzle unchanged. We leverage this to introduce a diagnostic framework for analyzing individual context examples. Using 53 UK Linguistics Olympiad puzzles, we generate two modified variants by deleting a single context example: (1) uniform random deletion, and (2) targeted deletion (inspired by error-correcting codes) to remove a structurally load-bearing example uniquely carrying necessary information. We formalize this impact using a Question Damage Score to classify puzzles as fragile or robust. Evaluating three frontier LLMs under instructions to abstain when information is insufficient, we find they rarely abstain, often continuing to produce correct answers after load-bearing context is removed. These findings motivate further investigation into context-based reasoning, prior knowledge, memorization, and linguistic inference. Beyond abstention, the framework enables fine-grained analyses of context reliance, including causal interventions, stopping-set analysis, targeted contamination studies, and mechanistic interpretability.
  </details>

- **[Local and Global Regimes of Geometric Complexity in Language Model Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14361)**  `arXiv:2608.14361`  `cs.CL`  
  _Arwa Osman, Marco Baroni, Iuri Macocco_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intrinsic dimensionality (ID) is widely used to probe the representational complexity of language models, but it remains unclear whether ID differences reflect properties of language itself or unchecked characteristics of the underlying dataset. In this paper, we focus specifically on how lexical diversity, the number of unique last-token items present in a dataset, affects ID estimates of that dataset. We find a scale-dependent transition between two regimes: at low lexical diversity, conditions with fewer unique final words produce higher ID, while at high lexical diversity, this ordering reverses, and conditions with more unique words produce higher ID. We derive an exact, parameter-free formula for the point at which this reversal occurs, which matches the observed transition point at every scale tested. On the one hand, our results highlight how care must be taken when interpreting the intrinsic dimensionality of a set of representations as a straightforward cue of their complexity. On the other hand, our discovery of the two ID regimes reveals a general principle of organisation of linguistic data in LLMs that sheds new light on their inner manifold structures.
  </details>

- **[AURA: Intent-Directed Probing for Implicit-Need Surfacing in Situated LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05557)**  `arXiv:2606.05557`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yang Li, Jiaxiang Liu, Jiang Cai, Mingkun Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A situated query like "where is Lin Wei?" often encodes more than its literal content: the user may also want to know whether Lin Wei is free, in a good mood, or worth interrupting now. Standard tool-use agents answer the literal question and stop. AURA inserts an inference step between scene perception and tool use that produces an IntentFrame: a structured estimate of the implicit need with a scalar gap score that controls per-query probe budget and tool selection. On a 100-query four-scene implicit-intent benchmark, AURA improves implicit-need coverage over ReAct-style probing (Delta = +0.07, p < 10^-6); three of four scenes are individually significant, the gain reproduces on a second backbone, and a prompt ablation attributes the lift to gap calibration rather than answer memorisation. A component ablation localises the gain further: the gap-derived budget ceiling alone outperforms the full architecture (p = 0.02), and bin-wise calibration analysis (r = -0.33) identifies the gap score as a probe trigger rather than a calibrated value estimator. On factual lookup the controller trades raw accuracy for 82% fewer probes and zero forbidden-tool violations on a privacy-sensitive slice; scope conditions are detailed in Limitations. Code, simulator, and benchmark are released atthis https URL, with the implicit-intent benchmark also on the HuggingFace Hub (innovation64/aura-implicit-intent).
  </details>

- **[Vectors Are Not Neutral: Sensitive-Information Inference from Exported LLM Representations in Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26433)**  `arXiv:2605.26433`  `cs.CL`  
  _Weixin Liu, Bowen Qu, Juming Xiong, Congning Ni, Bradley A. Malin, Zhijun Yin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) summarization systems may pass compact vector representations of private inputs to downstream retrieval, monitoring, audit, or analytic workflows. Even when source documents remain access-restricted, derived vectors may be handled under different access controls and still support sensitive-information inference, creating a residual information-disclosure risk. We study this issue in clinical discharge-summary generation as a high-stakes case study, using electronic health record (EHR)-recorded race as a controlled sensitive-label audit. We audit two artifacts that a system might retain or expose to downstream components: the final prompt-token hidden state and the mean-pooled prompt representation. Our results show that reducing recoverability of the case-study sensitive label from one exported artifact does not necessarily reduce recoverability from another. As a mitigation case study, we introduce SurfaceLoRA, an exported-vector-targeted parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that uses a gradient-reversal discriminator attached to a designated exported vector. Under a balanced five-way probing protocol, SurfaceLoRA reduces EHR-recorded race recoverability from the targeted final-token artifact toward chance while preserving summarization utility, yet recoverability remains substantially higher from untargeted pooled artifacts. These findings show that privacy auditing and mitigation should be performed on the exact vector artifact retained or exposed to downstream components.
  </details>

- **[Change My View? The Dynamics of Persuasion and Polarization in Online Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08383)**  `arXiv:2605.08383`  `cs.CL`  
  _David Freeborn, Malihe Alikani, Anthony Sicilia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Philosophical accounts of persuasion often assume that shared evidence and rational argumentation should lead to a convergence of views between peers, yet everyday discourse often suggests otherwise. In this study, we use large language models to analyze a corpus of debates on Reddit's r/ChangeMyView, where belief revision is publicly signaled. Large language models were asked, halfway through each discussion, to forecast whether such an acknowledgement would arise; their probabilistic estimates serve as a conversational baseline. Each reply was then coded, through a hybrid machine-assisted procedure, for ten familiar rhetorical strategies -- concession, empathy, logical challenge, credibility appeals, and so forth. Adding these strategic features markedly improves predictive power and yields a consistent pattern: moves that express concession or empathetic alignment substantially increase the prospect of belief change, whereas frontal refutation, credibility attacks, and topic deflection diminish it. The findings indicate that effective public reasoning depends as much on relational framing as on evidential content, and they invite a refinement of normative accounts of rational dialogue.
  </details>

- **[CogRAG: Tackling Heterogeneous Cognitive Demands in RAG via Stratified Retrieval and Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25928)**  `arXiv:2604.25928`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xudong Wang, Zilong Wang, Kui Su, Zhaoyan Ming_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) frameworks typically process all queries through a one-size-fits-all pipeline, ignoring the heterogeneous cognitive demands of different tasks. This cognitive-blind approach causes two failure modes: cascading errors when low-level factual gaps trigger hallucinated reasoning, and reasoning-answer inconsistency in higher-order analytical tasks. We introduce CogRAG, a training-free, domain-agnostic framework that tackles these heterogeneous cognitive demands via stratified retrieval and reasoning. Inspired by Bloom's Taxonomy, CogRAG uses the predicted cognitive load of a query as a central control signal that coordinates two modules: Cognition-Adaptive Evidence Refinement supplements missing context via fact-centric or option-centric paths, and Cognition-Stratified Structured Reasoning replaces unconstrained chain-of-thought with cognition-aligned reasoning templates. We evaluate CogRAG on a demanding professional testbed, the Registered Dietitian qualification examination. CogRAG effectively reduces early-stage factual errors and eliminates reasoning-answer inconsistency, raising Qwen3-8B accuracy from 73.4\% to 85.8\% in single-choice mode and from 63.3\% to 80.5\% in scenario mode. These results highlight cognitive-stratified control as an effective, generalizable paradigm for reliable complex reasoning in large language models.
  </details>

- **[Wired for Overconfidence: A Mechanistic Perspective on Inflated Verbalized Confidence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01457)**  `arXiv:2604.01457`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianyi Zhao, Yinhan He, Wendy Zheng, Yujie Zhang, Chen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are often not just wrong, but \emph{confidently wrong}: when they produce factually incorrect answers, they tend to verbalize overly high confidence rather than signal uncertainty. Such verbalized overconfidence can mislead users and weaken confidence scores as a reliable uncertainty signal, yet its internal mechanisms remain poorly understood. We present a circuit-level mechanistic analysis of this inflated verbalized confidence in LLMs, organized around three axes: capturing verbalized confidence as a differentiable internal signal, identifying the circuits that causally inflate it, and leveraging these insights for targeted inference-time recalibration. Across two instruction-tuned LLMs on three datasets, we find that a compact set of MLP blocks and attention heads, concentrated in middle-to-late layers, consistently writes the confidence-inflation signal at the final token position. We further show that targeted inference-time interventions on these circuits substantially improve calibration. Together, our results suggest that verbalized overconfidence in LLMs is driven by identifiable internal circuits and can be mitigated through targeted intervention.
  </details>
