- **[How Do Large Language Models Learn Concepts During Continual Pre-Training?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03570)**  `arXiv:2601.03570`  `cs.CL`  
  _Barry Menglong Yao, Sha Li, Yunzhi Yao, Minqian Liu, Zaishuo Xia, Qifan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human beings primarily understand the world through concepts (e.g., dog), abstract mental representations that structure perception, reasoning, and learning. However, how large language models (LLMs) acquire, retain, and forget such concepts during continual pretraining remains poorly understood. In this work, we study how individual concepts are acquired and forgotten, as well as how multiple concepts interact through interference and synergy. We link these behavioral dynamics to LLMs' internal concept circuits, computational subgraphs associated with specific concepts, and incorporate graph metrics to characterize circuit topology. Our analysis reveals: (1) LLMs' concept circuits provide a non-trivial, consistent signal of concept learning and forgetting; (2) concept circuit topology evolves stage-wise, rising during concept acquisition, and transiently rising before declining during concept forgetting; (3) semantically similar concepts induce stronger interference than weakly related ones; (4) conceptual knowledge differs in their transferability, with some significantly facilitating the learning of others. Together, our findings provide a circuit-level view of concept learning dynamics and motivate concept-aware training strategies, such as Circuit-aware Experience Replay, which uses circuit topology to prioritize concepts vulnerable to forgetting.
  </details>

- **[Social Perceptions of English Spelling Variation on Twitter: A Comparative Analysis of Human and LLM Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23041)**  `arXiv:2511.23041`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dong Nguyen, Laura Rosseel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spelling variation (e.g. funnnn vs. fun) can influence the social perception of texts and their writers: we often have various associations with different forms of writing (is the text informal? does the writer seem young?). In this study, we focus on the social perception of spelling variation in online writing in English and study to what extent this perception is aligned between humans and large language models (LLMs). Building on sociolinguistic methodology, we compare LLM and human ratings on three key social attributes of spelling variation (formality, carefulness, age). We find generally strong correlations in the ratings between humans and LLMs. However, notable differences emerge when we analyze the distribution of ratings and when comparing between different types of spelling variation.
  </details>

- **[SPECTRA: Revealing the Full Spectrum of User Preferences via Distributional LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24189)**  `arXiv:2509.24189`  `cs.CL`  
  _Luyang Zhang, Jialu Wang, Shichao Zhu, Beibei Li, Zhongcun Wang, Guangmou Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often represent user preferences by generating ranked item lists. These lists can overrepresent frequent categories and omit long-tail interests. We instead treat preference prediction as an LLM classification task, where a fine-tuned LLM assigns a probability to each category. Given a user's interaction history, we estimate the share of future interactions in each category. We propose SPECTRA (Softmax Probing for Extracted Category-level Token Readouts and Analysis), which reads a distribution over interpretable preference categories from a fine-tuned LLM's token scores. We evaluate SPECTRA on MovieLens, Yelp, and a large-scale short-video platform. SPECTRA delivers (i) distributional alignment, reducing Jensen-Shannon divergence to the empirical preference distribution by 38--44% across public datasets; (ii) long-tail recovery with cross-user fairness, raising top-3 category exposure entropy by $23%$ on MovieLens and producing a larger gain on tail-preference users than on head-preference users; and (iii) downstream application value, with a 41--46% category-NDCG boost on MovieLens and Yelp, and an increase in long-tail NDCG@$20$ from $0.024$ to $0.197$ against a production ranker on logged short-video data.
  </details>

- **[Persuasion from Within: Model-Based Insider Attacks in Multi-Agent LLM Debate and Consensus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28660)**  `arXiv:2611.28660`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Bangyan Ju, Xiaolin Sun, Yibin Hu, Zizhan Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent systems for collective decision-making, such as multi-agent debate and consensus games, in which agents exchange messages and update their answers over multiple rounds and a resolution mechanism aggregates them into a final outcome. Most multi-agent LLM frameworks assume that every participant shares the system objective. In practice, however, a malicious agent can join as a legitimate member of the group and persuade the other agents toward a hidden adversarial goal. We formalize such attacks as a sequential decision-making problem in which the insider seeks to degrade the utility of the collective outcome. We propose a model-based insider attack that learns to persuade a group of agents: the insider first learns a world model of the benign agents from offline trajectories and then uses it both to train a persuasion policy and to plan its messages. In two case studies involving a numerical consensus game and a multi-agent debate, the learned insider is the most effective and the stealthiest attack we evaluate, outperforming both prompt-based and trained LLM persuaders from prior work. The attack also transfers to other target models, larger groups, and unseen datasets, and remains effective under existing Byzantine-resilience defenses. The learned insider also enables a new defense: adversarially training benign agents on the attack data largely removes both our attack and the strongest prior persuasion attack. These results show that combining world models with reinforcement learning and planning is an effective route to both attacking and hardening language-based multi-agent systems.
  </details>

- **[Where Autonomy Belongs: A Separation of Powers for Agentic Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15309)**  `arXiv:2611.15309`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhengyang Ai, Tong Liu, Jingxian Tang, Xiaobing Du, Tao Xiao, Fengshi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-driven program evolution rests on three decisions: govern directs the search from accumulated evidence, propose constructs the next candidate, and judge settles validity and fitness. All three appear in every system, and existing work splits into two lines over who makes each. Controller-centric systems strengthen govern and neglect the autonomy of propose, which caps what evolution can reach. Agent-centric systems let a coding agent take over both govern and propose, at a cognitive and computational cost. Neither line protects the independent authority of judge, so benchmark reliability suffers. These failures share one root: autonomy is misallocated across the three decisions. We address this problem with EvoTrias, a separation-of-powers architecture in which each decision has one owner: Structured Governance for what spans evolution rounds, Open Proposal for construction inside a task, and Protected Judgment behind a private evaluator. These owners exchange typed contracts only, and runtime hooks reinforce the boundaries. This separation also lets EvoTrias run as a plugin that turns an existing coding agent into an evolution system. EvoTrias sets a new state of the art on mathematical, systems, and algorithm-design benchmarks. Three further results show what each owner buys: separating Governance from Proposal cuts token cost by 64%, Open Proposal extends evolution to a 680K-line industrial repository, and Protected Judgment keeps benchmark scores faithful to transfer.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Perfect Teammates: Multi-Agent Collaboration with Unreliable Colleagues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16117)**  `arXiv:2610.16117`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Chen Huang, Yiyang Zhang, Yuhao Wu, Yang Deng, Wenxuan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multi-agent collaboration is increasingly used to solve complex tasks by dividing work among specialized agents and integrating complementary expertise. Yet existing systems and evaluations largely rest on an implicit perfect-colleague assumption: collaborators provide correct and complete information and respond promptly to requests. In realistic teamwork, however, collaborators may return information that does not answer the intended question, provide insufficient detail to support a decision, or fail to respond to a request. To separate task-solving competence from the ability to recover an unreliable evidence-gathering process, we introduce the Unreliable Colleagues Benchmark (UnCoBench), which organizes behavioral failures through Misaligned, Incomplete, and Missed settings. The main agent need to verify contextual correctness, diagnose information gap and track unresolved dependencies in order to obtain the correct evidence for solving the task. We also introduce a verifiable and scalable automated generation pipeline that pairs LLM-based scenario generation with executable checks of answer uniqueness and role necessity. Results across ten models show that similar reliable-setting performance can result in different robustness to unreliable evidence. Trace analysis reveals frequent omissions of corrective follow-up, and the recovery behavior involves inefficient waiting and redundant queries. These findings show that robust collaboration requires not only sound reasoning, but also targeted action to diagnose evidence failures, recover relevant information, and integrate it into decisions. Our data and source code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Belief Engine: Making Agent Stance Auditable in LLM Deliberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15343)**  `arXiv:2605.15343`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Joshua C. Yang, Maurice Flechtner, Damian Dailisan, Michiel A. Bakker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based AI agents are increasingly used to simulate deliberative social interactions, including negotiation, conflict resolution, and multi-turn opinion exchanges. Simulating human-like opinion change calls for agents that balance existing beliefs with openness to new evidence, while making their responses to that evidence traceable. Current systems often make this difficult: agents may drift from assigned roles, echo an interaction partner, keep a stance despite contrary evidence, or appear to change because the prompt or retrieved context has changed. We introduce the Belief Engine (BE), a framework that uses "belief" in a Bayesian modelling sense, as a maintained evidential state over a proposition that is exposed as a scalar stance. BE extracts structured evidence, stores argument records, and updates the belief state through a Bayesian-inspired stance update in log-odds space with two interpretable controls: evidence uptake u and prior anchoring a. Across multiple LLM models, sweeps of u and a demonstrate stable control over stance dynamics. Higher uptake makes agents more responsive to new evidence, while stronger anchoring preserves the initial stance. On DEBATE, a human deliberation dataset with pre/post opinions, BE is most accurate when participants move in the direction of extracted evidence, showing its strength in evidence-following deliberative contexts. When all participants are evaluated together, gains are more modest because many people remain stable or move for reasons not captured by the extracted evidence stream. BE provides a configurable and auditable belief-update layer for evidence-grounded deliberation, in which LLM agents can be configured as open-minded, anchored, or evidence-sensitive, while their stance trajectories and memory can be audited, compared, and calibrated against human opinion change.
  </details>

- **[Memphis: A Case Study in Seeding an Agent Task from a Model's Real-World Attempt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29824)**  `arXiv:2611.29824`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ibrahim A. Ahmed, Ahmed Sameh Khalil_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent benchmarks are usually made harder by scaling up the data their tasks are built from, yet models that do well on them still fail at real work. We propose building simulated tasks from a model's own attempt at an open-ended task in the real world, so that the model's failures in the simulation resemble its failures in reality. As a case study, we had GPT-6.1 Sol manage two human workers in Cairo over chat for five shifts, with a budget of 7,000 Egyptian pounds, to build as many USB desk lamps as it could. It built two. From the real-world attempt's transcript, we build Memphis, an evaluation in a model-simulated world. Memphis is hard: GPT-6.1 Sol delivers 1.0 lamp per trial on average and repeats its real-world mistakes, buying too few stands and giving up the search for them. With a one-page hint on where to buy parts and who can wire them, it delivers 9.2. Claude Opus 5.5 delivers 2.2 without the hint and 4.6 with it. We release the task, the trials, the audits, and a redacted record of the real-world evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Dimension Is Free, Horizon Is Not: When Evolution Strategies Rival Policy Gradients for LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29795)**  `arXiv:2611.29795`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Qiyao Liang, Kajetan Schweighofer, Xin Qiu, Risto Miikkulainen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Policy gradient (PG) methods such as GRPO dominate LLM fine-tuning, yet recent work showed that Evolution Strategies (ES) also scale to fine-tuning models with billions of parameters. Which method needs fewer samples, and on which tasks, remains unclear. We view the two methods as estimators of the same gradient that differ in where they explore. Because ES perturbs parameters, its noise grows with the number of parameters, and because PG samples actions, its noise grows with the number of generated tokens. Only noise along the directions that change the reward slows learning. The cost of ES then depends on the effective dimension, the number of independent skills a task requires rather than its parameter count, while the cost of PG depends on the effective horizon, which counts only the tokens that share both credit and weights with the reward-carrying ones. We train both methods on a synthetic task where the number of skills and the effective horizon can be varied separately. PG is cheaper when the horizon is short, ES becomes cheaper once it is long enough, and this crossover moves to longer horizons as the number of skills grows. We then measure the effective horizons of 20 NLP, math reasoning, and agentic tasks and compare the two methods with Qwen2.5-7B. PG leads on short-horizon tasks, the two tie on the moderate-horizon math ones, and no task has a horizon in the regime where the theory predicts an ES advantage. Terminal-Bench has a far longer horizon. There, in a preliminary comparison with Qwen3.5-4B, ES without any credit assignment reaches a higher success rate than the PG checkpoint released with Tmax, as the theory predicts. ES should thus have the advantage on long-horizon tasks with few independent skills and a nonvanishing reward signal. Current benchmarks are only at the onset of this regime, so ES should become more competitive as agentic tasks grow longer.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning from LLM-Generated (Suboptimal) Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29732)**  `arXiv:2611.29732`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Anurag Kadkol, Lawson L.S. Wong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has achieved substantial progress across many domains, but training an agent \textit{tabula rasa} remains highly sample-inefficient. A common remedy is to initialize the agent using a neural network pretrained on demonstrations. However, collecting demonstrations is expensive, and the demonstrations themselves can be suboptimal. In this work, we use a large language model (LLM) to generate an advice policy that produces demonstration data with effectively no human cost. We use this data to warmstart a DQN agent, and use the advice policy itself to guide exploration during training. Because LLM-generated advice is often suboptimal, we further introduce a value warmstart method that initializes the agent to recognize promising states from the advice policy's behavior, without directly copying its actions. To characterize the robustness of this method, we systematically perturb individual sub-skills of an LLM-generated advice policy for a long-horizon, sparse-reward task. Degraded advice still meaningfully accelerates learning relative to unaided baselines. These results suggest that LLM-generated advice, though suboptimal, can be a cheap alternative to costly human demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Another Angle: Rotation-Based Test-Time Training for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28972)**  `arXiv:2611.28972`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Changyu Liu, Fei Zhao, Yiyang Liu, Xi Xiao, Tianyang Wang, Cheng Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on vision-language tasks but often remain vulnerable to distribution shifts due to their fixed parameters during inference. While Test-Time Training (TTT) offers a pathway for dynamic adaptation, existing approaches suffer from prohibitive computational overhead due to multiple sampling and the risk of error reinforcement from biased self-generated supervision. In light of this view, we propose RoTTT (Rotation-based Test-Time Training), a simple and efficient TTT approach that enables instance-specific refinement at inference time. For each test instance, RoTTT performs a small number of gradient updates by predicting the rotation angle of the input image. The known rotation angles naturally serve as a reliable self-supervised signal, enabling lightweight and stable adaptation without requiring additional annotations. Extensive experiments across multiple vision-language benchmarks demonstrate that RoTTT consistently improves performance across different MLLMs, providing a practical and efficient paradigm for on-the-fly MLLM adaptation.
  </details>

- **[FormaFin: Verifiable Financial Analysis with Solver-Guided Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28881)**  `arXiv:2611.28881`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bethel Hall, William Eiers_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Formally verifying financial analyses generated by large language models (LLMs) remains challenging as financial rules are largely expressed in natural language and rarely have corresponding formal specifications. Training language models to formalize these rules is hindered by the scarcity of labeled data and reliable training signals, while confidential financial data favor small models that run locally. We introduce FormaFin, a framework that trains small open language models to translate financial rules into reusable SMT formulas. FormaFin integrates teacher distillation with reinforcement learning using an SMT solver to compute rewards from numerical thresholds stated in financial rules. We evaluate FormaFin on corporate loan monitoring, in which lenders check borrowers' financial figures against loan agreements. To evaluate our framework, we curate \bench{}, a benchmark containing 143 loan agreements and 2,527 questions covering compliance, earnings cushion, and debt capacity. Our results show that a 7B formalizer achieves 94.8\% accuracy, outperforming open LLMs up to ten times larger. Across three model families, accuracy ranges from 92.6 to 96.8\%. Our framework also detects 95.6 to 99.7\% of errors in other LLMs' financial analyses. We make FormaFin and \bench{} publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Unlearning Model Identity: Ablating LoRA Weights from Question-Based Identity Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28650)**  `arXiv:2611.28650`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ming Cheung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models are trained on diverse corpora containing outputs from proprietary teacher models and provider-curated sources. Models subsequently inherit embedded self-identity traits, persistently asserting specific proprietary names. Service providers of open-weight models frequently require the removal of model identity for downstream applications, such as custom interfaces, without exposing provider affiliations to end-users. Effective identity modification is therefore critical for customized model alignment and deployment. This study proposes a method to localize parameter-level representations of self-identity within low-rank adaptation modules to perform targeted weight ablation. By generating probing questions designed to elicit self-naming outputs and fine-tuning the base model on these responses, the approach identifies and ablates high-magnitude weights within the parameter set. Experimental results demonstrate that distilled identity traits reside within a localized subset of low-rank weights, achieving a complete removal rate in Qwen3.5-2B and a 10% rate in Gemma4-E2B while preserving general task capabilities. These findings confirm that ablating top-tier adaptation weights suppresses distilled self-identification without compromising broader model performance.
  </details>

- **[APPSolver: Adaptive Patch Partitioning for Point-Wise Ship Flow Prediction on Unstructured Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29355)**  `arXiv:2608.29355`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Wenhua Huo, Fenglei Han, Wangyuan Zhao, Xiao Peng, Chunhui Wang, Jialin Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting high-fidelity flow fields around ship hulls is central to hydrodynamic performance analysis, yet traditional CFD solvers remain computationally prohibitive for rapid design exploration across multiple vessel types and operating conditions. Existing deep-learning surrogates often interpolate unstructured CFD meshes onto regular grids, which can blur near-field resolution, while direct point-level global attention on large unstructured meshes can be computationally expensive. In this paper, we introduce APPSolver, a neural surrogate that learns next-step point-wise flow-field prediction directly on the unstructured free-surface ship CFD mesh without re-gridding. APPSolver is built around Adaptive Patch Partitioning (APP), a quadtree-based strategy that groups unstructured points into local spatial patches according to the non-uniform density inherent in ship CFD meshes: fine near the hull and wake, coarse in the far field. The resulting patch tokens are processed by a Transformer backbone together with optional condition token that encode ship geometry and condition parameters via a frozen large language model, forming a unified token-space model for one-step temporal advancement of velocity and pressure fields. On the ShipBench dataset, APP-Transformer exhibits an accuracy--efficiency trade-off, incurring higher prediction errors than the strongest learned baselines while requiring substantially lower forward computation. Ablation studies quantify the compression-fidelity trade-off of APP and demonstrate that condition token provide preliminary gains in some leave-one-hull-out settings. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Reward Hackability in Code RL Training Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16062)**  `arXiv:2606.16062`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shreshth Rajan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Code RL rewards a policy for passing a task's tests, so a test suite that accepts a wrong patch trains the wrong behavior. We measure how often open code RL environments accept wrong patches and how reliably hackable tasks can be detected and repaired, checking each claim by execution against the task's gold patch. First, 22.4% of 49 SWE-bench Verified tasks accept a Docker-verified patch that behaves differently from the gold patch, and 57.8% against a stronger attacker. The gold-patch check is necessary: an attacker not shown the gold patch returns it for 31% of its passing "exploits". Second, across 134 public SWE-bench submissions, models pass hackable tasks 28.6 pp more often than tasks of the same difficulty, mostly because those tasks are passable at all; pooled over three attacker runs, hackable tasks still exceed matched passable ones (p=0.014). Third, repair is harder to verify than detection: 61.9% of LLM-written repair tests fail on the gold patch, and even behind a gate an LLM judge is wrong on 38% of the verdicts execution can check. Finally, of 873 training trajectories behind an open coding agent run beside the gold fix, 19.4% answer differently on a case the issue describes; an LLM review that can only demote these executed differences judges 6.9% to be wrong fixes the tests rewarded (6.4-10.5% across three model reviews), and 5.4% in a second public training set (R2E-Gym). End to end, the gated repair yields verified tests for 290 of 324 wrong fixes in that agent's data and all 9 in 384 rewarded Open-SWE-Traces rollouts (2.3%). One-shot attacks hack 23 of 100 SWE-bench Pro V2 and 9 of 112 DeepSWE tasks; 12 of 1,344 passing DeepSWE leaderboard patches are model-judged wrong. Science graders mostly hold: an agentic red team passes 0 of 61 Terminal-Bench-Science tasks. Audits and repairs of code RL environments should be decided by execution wherever execution can decide.
  </details>

- **[MemFail: Stress-Testing Failure Modes of LLM Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26667)**  `arXiv:2605.26667`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ishir Garg, Neel Kolhe, Dawn Song, Xuandong Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory systems to remain consistent across long-horizon interactions, but little empirical work has been done to understand the specific failure modes and design choices that these systems present. Existing benchmarks report aggregate question-answering accuracy and treat memory systems as black boxes, making it impossible to attribute an incorrect answer to a particular failure mode of the system. We introduce MemFail, a diagnostic benchmark that isolates the failure modes of modern LLM memory systems. We begin by formalizing memory systems as the composition of three canonical operations -- summarization, storage, and retrieval -- and identify the potential failure modes induced by each. Based on these hypothesized failure modes, we construct five datasets spanning four tasks, each adversarially designed to test a specific operation of a memory system. Using these datasets, we evaluate four state-of-the-art memory systems on MemFail and demonstrate how MemFail can be used to empirically understand the tradeoffs induced by differences in memory system architectures.
  </details>

- **[Measuring AI Agents' Progress on Multi-Step Cyber Attack Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11214)**  `arXiv:2603.11214`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Linus Folkerts, Will Payne, Simon Inman, Philippos Giavridis, Joe Skinner, Sam Deverett, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We evaluate the autonomous offensive cyber capabilities of frontier AI agents on two purpose-built cyber ranges---a 32-step corporate network attack and a 7-step industrial control system attack targeting a simulated cooling tower---complemented by a private 76-task CTF suite spanning four difficulty tiers. Human-expert solve times are estimated at roughly 20 hours for the corporate range and 15 hours for the cooling tower; CTF time annotations range from 1 minute to over 42 hours and include measured playtests, designer estimates, and lower bounds from unsolved playtests. Across thirteen models released over a twenty-month period---from GPT-4o in August 2024 to GPT-5.5 in April 2026---we observe two trends. First, step-count progress increases approximately log-linearly with inference-time compute over the tested range (up to 100M tokens). Second, newer frontier models generally complete more steps at fixed token budgets. GPT-5.5 and Mythos Glasswing each completed all 32 steps in at least one run, the first end-to-end completions of the corporate range; Mythos Glasswing averaged 25.2 of 32 steps and completed the chain in 6 of 10 runs, and GPT-5.5 averaged 22.3 and completed it in 2 of 10. On the control system range, Mythos Glasswing achieved the first end-to-end completion of the chain (2 of 10 runs, mean 4.2); the other frontier models reached at most 3 of 7 steps by directly probing the operational-technology protocol rather than following the intended chain of IT compromise and authenticated control. These results indicate rapid improvements in autonomous cyber capability and suggest that complex but undefended multi-step ranges---including industrial-control attack chains---are no longer reliably distinguishing the strongest frontier models. Future evaluations should emphasise hardened environments, active defences, defensive-control effectiveness, and operational security.
  </details>

- **[WeMM-Ecom: Advancing Multimodal Content Understanding for WeChat E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30662)**  `arXiv:2611.30662`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Binhui Xie, Jinmin Li, Haomiao Tang, Guangting Wang, Song Jin, Jianwei Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present WeMM-Ecom, a family of multimodal large language models that deliver frontier e-commerce capabilities while achieving state-of-the-art general-purpose performance at their respective scales. The family spans five sizes, from a 0.8B dense model to a 35B mixture-of-experts model with 3B active parameters. Our training pipeline begins with two-stage continued pre-training that progressively raises the proportion of e-commerce data within a diverse multimodal data mixture, followed by hybrid-thinking post-training that combines on-policy distillation and reinforcement learning to support both non-thinking and thinking modes. For evaluation, we construct a comprehensive suite of 28 e-commerce benchmarks (23 in-house and five open-source) covering eight business scenarios and complement it with 64 public general-purpose benchmarks. On the e-commerce suite, WeMM-Ecom-35B-A3B surpasses substantially larger open-source flagship models, including Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, HY4, and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. On the general-purpose suite, every WeMM-Ecom model surpasses its similarly sized Qwen3.5 counterpart, with the lightweight variants (e.g., WeMM-Ecom-2B) showing clear advantages over leading lightweight models such as Qwen3.5-2B, MiniCPM-V-4.7-1B, and LFM3-VL-2B. Beyond benchmarks, WeMM-Ecom now powers hundreds of production use cases in WeChat, including catalog management, multimodal product search, and livestream commerce, serving billion-scale requests per day. We publicly release all model weights and detail our data curation, training recipe, and benchmark construction to facilitate future work on domain-specialized multimodal models. We have released the model weights and code to facilitate future research atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MVAgent: Per-Video Agents under Global Coordination for Multi-Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29154)**  `arXiv:2611.29154`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Kewei Wei, XueXiang Wen, Bocheng Hu, Weili Xu, Haiyang Yu, Kaiyue Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-video understanding requires systems to combine evidence from different videos for cross-video reasoning. Answering such questions involves two kinds of decisions. Observation decisions must adapt to each video's content, whereas cross-video judgments require relating evidence from different videos based on the question. Most existing agents make both kinds of decisions in one controller, whose context accumulates observations from every video. We separate them in MVAgent, a training-free Multi-Video Understanding Agent framework with one LocalAgent per video. Each LocalAgent plans observations of its own video and keeps a private observation history. A GlobalAgent coordinates cross-video reasoning by instructing the LocalAgents and combining their reports with joint observation. We evaluate MVAgent on CVBench, MVU-Eval, and CrossVid, using three Qwen3.5 models of different sizes and GPT-6-sol. With Qwen3.5, our framework improves aggregate benchmark scores by 5.1 points on average over Single-pass inference with the same model and by 5.0 points over the strongest prior agent using the same model. With GPT-6-sol, the average gain over Single-pass inference with the same model is 4.3 points. On the same hardware, MVAgent also runs 1.8-2.7 times faster than prior agents of comparable size.
  </details>

- **[PACE: More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking? Untangling Coordination, Ensembling, and Test-Time Compute in Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17610)**  `arXiv:2611.17610`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent language model evaluations often compare a communicating system with a single greedy pass, which confounds coordination with extra samples and test-time compute. We introduce Parity-Aware Compute Evaluation (PACE): a six-dimensional resource vector, four baseline categories, and a directional rule that credits a gain only against a baseline using at least as much of the declared resources and rules a gain out against any baseline using no more. We audit 9 published studies. Across the 72 deduplicated baseline cells that report both a greedy and a sampled baseline, the sampling gap, by which the sampled baseline lowers the multi-agent difference, averages -0.036 with studies weighted equally (negative in 64 cells), and 53 of the 123 paired contrasts that beat a greedy pass become nonpositive against the sampled baseline. Against sampled or budget-matched baselines the mean difference is -0.042; all eight Category B/C study means are negative and the one Category D study is positive. In a controlled experiment with an open-weight model, debate and mixture-of-agents do not clear the equal-token self-consistency frontier on MMLU-Pro (debate -0.015); on Hidden Profile, split-view debate exceeds a lower-budget pool yet trails content-mismatched placebo and four-choice chance. We release a seven-item checklist, an evaluation card, and command-line utilities.
  </details>

- **[RealICU: Do LLM Agents Understand Long-Context ICU Data? A Benchmark Beyond Behavior Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13542)**  `arXiv:2605.13542`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Chengzhi Shen, Weixiang Shen, Tobias Susetzky, Chen, Chen, Jun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intensive care units (ICU) generate long, dense and evolving streams of clinical information, where physicians must repeatedly reassess patient states under time pressure, underscoring a clear need for reliable AI decision support. Existing ICU benchmarks typically treat historical clinician actions as ground truth. However, these actions are made under incomplete information and limited temporal context of the underlying patient state, and may therefore be suboptimal, making it difficult to assess the true reasoning capabilities of AI systems. We introduce RealICU, a hindsight-annotated benchmark for evaluating large language models (LLMs) under realistic ICU conditions, where labels are created after senior physicians review the full patient trajectory. We formulate four physician-motivated tasks: assess Patient Status, Acute Problems, Recommended Actions, and Red Flag actions that risk unsafe outcomes. We partition each trajectory with 30-min windows and release two datasets: RealICU-Gold with 930-window annotations from 94 MIMIC-IV patients, and RealICU-Scale with 11,862 windows extended by Oracle, a physician-validated LLM hindsight labeler. Existing LLMs including memory-augmented ones performed poorly on RealICU, exposing two failure modes: a recall-safety tradeoff for clinical recommendations, and an anchoring bias to early interpretations of the patient. We further introduce ICU-Evo to study structured-memory agents that improves long-horizon reasoning but does not fully eliminate safety failures. Together, RealICU provides a clinically grounded testbed for measuring and improving AI sequential decision-support in high-stakes care. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SAEPO: Sparse-Autoencoder-Guided Credit Assignment for Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29936)**  `arXiv:2611.29936`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yusong Zhao, Zihao Zhao, Zirui Zhang, Tunyu Zhang, Ziyan Wang, Yi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has demonstrated strong performance in improving large language model (LLM) reasoning, yet its credit-assignment signal remains coarse. Outcome verifiers provide scalable supervision by judging whether a completed response is correct, but they do not directly indicate which intermediate reasoning tokens contribute to success or failure. As a result, policy optimization can be inefficient, especially when training base models directly from sparse outcome feedback. In this paper, we introduce SAEPO, a Sparse AutoEncoder-guided token-level credit assignment method for outcome-supervised Policy Optimization. Rather than treating correctness only as a rollout-level (sequence-level) signal, SAEPO uses a pretrained sparse autoencoder (SAE) to build token-level semantic representations within reasoning trajectories; it scores each token based on the SAE concepts (e.g., penalizing wrong "final-answer" tokens but rewarding correct "reasoning-process" tokens), learned in an unsupervised manner, to achieve fine-grained credit assignment. SAEPO is a plug-in advantage-shaping module compatible with various policy optimization algorithms. We instantiate SAEPO with both GRPO and GSPO, covering token-ratio and sequence-ratio policy-gradient objectives, and evaluate SAEPO across three LLM backbones on six math and two code benchmarks. Empirical results show that SAEPO improves both the math and code benchmark averages over the matched baselines, with gains of up to 6.63% and 5.57%, respectively. On Qwen3-1.7B-Base, training curves also show faster early accuracy growth. Feature and token-level analyses suggest that the SAE-derived signal favors question-relevant concepts and reasoning tokens.
  </details>

- **[SAE-Audit: Clinical-Trial Prediction via Inference-Time Optimization of Black-Box LLMs Audited by Sparse Autoencoder Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29368)**  `arXiv:2611.29368`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Junbo Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical trials are costly and often take years to complete, making early outcome prediction valuable for trial planning. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to forecast clinical-trial outcomes, yet their predictions remain unreliable and their rationales may not faithfully reflect the evidence underlying their answers. On tasks such as predicting whether a registered clinical trial will complete or terminate early, frontier LLMs perform only modestly above chance in our experiments. We introduce SAE-Audit, which optimizes a black-box LLM at inference time with evidence obtained from a deployable open model. A frozen sparse autoencoder (SAE) on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct is converted into a vocabulary of clinical concepts. Sparse probes fitted on past trials determine which concepts are associated with the outcome. For a new protocol, the concepts detected by the probe are written as evidence, each with the direction and strength of its association with the outcome, and the LLM being audited optimizes its answer in the light of this evidence. Without additional training, SAE-Audit raises balanced accuracy of deepseek-v4-flash by 9.6--12.0 points on five clinical-trial prediction tasks and raises GPT-5.6-terra by 8.0 points on trial completion, exceeding other agentic inference-time optimization methods by a large margin. As an auxiliary, SAE probes module within SAE-Audit is itself accurate and auditable, providing natural-language explanations directly derived from verified concept contributions and protocol evidence, without requiring additional LLMs at inference time. SAE-Audit provides verifiable, concept-level evidence for more accurate and auditable clinical-trial prediction. It can also be embedded into biomedical AI agents such as Biomni to optimize their performance.
  </details>

- **[RouteAudit: A Negative Result on Adaptive Multi-Verifier Routing under Matched Fixed-Subset Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02808)**  `arXiv:2610.02808`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Tianshu Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Apparent savings from adaptive verifier routing can come from a fixed verifier subset. We audit GSM8K using cached Qwen3.5-0.8B or Qwen3.5-2B candidates paired with Qwen3-4B, three LLM verifiers, 1,319 held-out requests, and 5 router seeds. Our shared-fusion audit compares routers with the complete fixed-subset envelope, including zero-call abstention, under matched prices and scoring. Calibration-selected single-call verification matches full static verification in 3 of 4 cells. In a separate comparison using the original fusion rules and two-verifier catalog, archived learned and offline-RL routers lose 0.038 to 0.171 in quality against matched static SF+SA. Across 480 budget-matched offline fits in six router families, the family envelope gain is zero in each cell at the primary price. No locked router obtains a positive simultaneous bootstrap lower bound across 68 cell-by-price points. None is classified as complete-static dominant, and all 4 primary-price points are inconclusive. At the primary price, the tested-family gain upper bound is at most 0.002. The oracle headroom upper bound is 0.034 to 0.045, above our 0.01 margin, leaving meaningful gains open. At 45 of 64 grid points excluding the primary price, the oracle lower bound exceeds 0.01 and the tested-family upper bound is at most 0.01. Coverage of these envelope bounds remains unverified. At a true gain of 0.03, the procedure rejects on 44.1% of points, and planted-effect family coverage ranges from 0.923 to 0.931. We report lessons from this catalog.
  </details>

- **[DiscernBench: Evaluating Cooperative Discernment of LLM Agents in Institutional Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28787)**  `arXiv:2611.28787`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Disha Sheshanarayana, Tanishka Magar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The deployment of large language models in operational settings demands evaluation of judgment under institutional constraint. We introduce DiscernBench, a benchmark for evaluating cooperative discernment: the ability of an agent to determine whether to comply with, refuse, or escalate a request in an institutional setting. DiscernBench comprises scenarios derived from real-world incident reports across domains such as aviation, law enforcement, and emergency management. We evaluate eight LLMs across three decision-making configurations and analyze both final decisions and the intermediate judgments produced during multi-agent deliberation. Under disagreement, Final Responders exhibit model- and role-dependent aggregation patterns in which a lone specialist recommendation can disproportionately influence the collective decision, a phenomenon we term Minority-Signal Override. Role-specialized multi-agent deliberation raises average over-escalation from 3.79% under direct single-agent judgment to 28.27%, with the direction and magnitude of minority-signal influence varying substantially across models and specialist roles. These results reveal distinct collective decision behaviors that are not captured by final-answer accuracy alone. Trustworthy multi-agent systems therefore require evaluating not only individual specialist reasoning, but also how conflicting judgments are transformed into collective decisions. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Specialized Large Language Models for Markovian Reasoning: Structural Cache Compression, Transition Pretraining, and Dual-Loop Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28285)**  `arXiv:2611.28285`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haibin Tong, Liangliang Xin, Yamei Zhu, Jiang Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard autoregressive large language models attend to the growing history of generated tokens, making the key-value (KV) cache a central memory and throughput bottleneck in long-chain reasoning and multi-branch sampling. Yet an important class of reasoning problems is naturally first-order Markovian: the next step depends on a sufficient representation of the current state, rather than on the raw sequence of all earlier steps. We develop a unified design paradigm for Markovian-reasoning specialized large language models. At the cache level, the Markovian state interface transforms history-dependent KV storage into constant-size state transfer with respect to the number of reasoning steps. At the learning level, we reformulate long-chain prediction as independently trainable one-step transition objectives, with explicit state bottlenecks, distillation, invariance, and drift-consistency losses. At the architectural level, we introduce a dual-loop Transformer: an outer loop advances the reasoning state, while an inner recurrent-depth loop allocates adaptive computation to each transition through shared weights and explicit memory tokens or recurrent matrix states. We connect this architecture to a three-region cache, step-relative positions, truncated backpropagation, branch-aware checkpoints, and a transition-centric evaluation protocol. The organizing principle is simple: the optimal reasoning system need not remember everything; it must carry forward exactly what the future needs. This principle opens a path toward specialized reasoning engines whose memory footprint is decoupled from reasoning-chain length.
  </details>

- **[Steering Automated Scientific Discovery via Audited Epistemic Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25214)**  `arXiv:2611.25214`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fangxu Yu, Tao Feng, Xuying Ning, Haolin Yang, Jiaxuan You, Tianyi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery requires revisiting the hypotheses in light of new evidence and identifying promising directions beyond those already explored. However, research agents often make claims that are not supported by their observations, explore a narrow hypothesis space, and retain beliefs despite recognizing conflicting evidence. These failures motivate the search for an explicit epistemic state that captures both beliefs about existing hypotheses and directions for further exploration. To this end, we propose Epistemic Compass (EpiCompass), a plug-and-play scaffold that maintains this state through an epistemic tree linking hypotheses, evidence, beliefs, and revisions. As an investigation progresses, EpiCompass reassesses the hypotheses explored against accumulated evidence and uses unresolved conflicts and unexplored directions to guide hypothesis generation. It selects candidates by anticipated epistemic gain, accounting for both refinement of existing hypotheses and expansion of the hypothesis space, and verifies the final conclusion against the accumulated evidence. Experimental results across three scientific discovery benchmarks and three LLM backbones demonstrate that EpiCompass consistently outperforms baselines, with further analyses showing reductions in errors of unsupported claims and failures to revise beliefs. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ArgGYM: A Procedural, Engine-Verified Benchmark for Structured Defeasible Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38409)**  `arXiv:2609.38409`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _İbrahim Ethem Deveci, Funda Tan Çalık, Barış Deniz Sağlam, Duygu Ataman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in large language model reasoning has been driven by benchmarks and reinforcement learning environments with automatically verifiable rewards, particularly in mathematics, code, and formal logic. These settings make model accuracy easier to evaluate and optimize, but it remains unclear how far success under fixed problem specifications and stable evaluation criteria transfers to reasoning outside such domains. Real-world reasoning often proceeds under incomplete and revisable information: conclusions may be supported provisionally, defeated by counter-evidence, reinstated by further arguments, or revised when stronger reasons become available. Reasoning of this kind is generally referred to as defeasible reasoning. We introduce ArgGYM, a procedural benchmark and RLVR-compatible training environment for structured defeasible reasoning. ArgGYM decomposes this reasoning into twelve tasks and grounds task-specific scoring in a symbolic argumentation engine that computes the formal states used to evaluate model outputs. It includes a frozen benchmark of 1,440 verified instances across fifteen curriculum configurations, two argument preference orderings, and two set orderings, while the same generators and verifiers can produce fresh instances, reducing dependence on static test sets, for both evaluation and verifiable-reward training. On the frozen benchmark, frontier and open-weight models show sharply different reasoning profiles: they can recover substantial parts of structured answers without solving the complete task, and performance declines in later curriculum configurations with longer dependencies and more interacting structures. Training Qwen3.5-4B with ArgGYM's verifiers as rewards more than doubles complete-task success on held-out instances, with the largest gains on constructive tasks.
  </details>

- **[TraceDance: An Automated System for Building Agent Behavior Benchmarks from Real-World Agent Deployment Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33295)**  `arXiv:2609.33295`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Dehai Min, Daoan Zhang, Yiming Zeng, Huayi Zhang, Ziyi Chen, Yan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent can complete a task while exhibiting undesirable behavior during execution. Developers need tests for the specific behaviors encountered in deployment, beyond fixed benchmark suites. We present TraceDance, an agent system that constructs targeted benchmarks from deployment traces for user-specified undesirable behaviors. For efficient construction, Anchor-and-Confirm combines programmable retrieval with candidate-level confirmation by a Flash large language model (LLM), while the Anchor Synthesis Loop generates and revises specifications for custom behaviors. The benchmarks use decision-point continuation to evaluate an LLM's next turn at a recorded decision point with a behavior-specific rubric, without a reference answer or environment replay. Experiments in coding and general tool use draw on 252,557 sessions and produce 107 benchmarks with 4,125 instances, fulfilling 95.3% of build-target requests. Both human annotators confirm the requested behavior in 84% of sampled instances, and the automated grader's agreement with human pass/fail judgments is comparable to that between the annotators. Nine frontier LLMs achieve a mean pass rate of only 26.7%, showing that they still struggle to respond appropriately at the evaluated decision points. Analysis across behavior-specific benchmarks further reveals weaknesses in how current LLMs behave as agents. By turning deployment problems into targeted benchmarks, TraceDance could serve as a key component of the recursive self-improvement (RSI) loop.
  </details>

- **[Towards Adaptive, Scalable, and Robust Coordination of LLM Agents: A Dynamic Ad-Hoc Networking Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08009)**  `arXiv:2602.08009`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Rui Li, Zeyu Zhang, Xiaohe Bo, Quanyu Dai, Chaozhuo Li, Feng Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent architectures built on large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated the potential to realize swarm intelligence through well-crafted collaboration. However, the substantial burden of manual orchestration inherently raises an imperative to automate the design of agentic workflows. We frame such an agent coordination challenge as a classic problem in dynamic ad-hoc networking: How to establish adaptive and reliable communication among a scalable number of agentic hosts? In response to this unresolved dilemma, we introduce RAPS, a reputation-aware publish-subscribe paradigm for adaptive, scalable, and robust coordination of LLM agents. RAPS is grounded in the Distributed Publish-Subscribe Protocol, allowing LLM agents to exchange messages based on their declared intents rather than predefined topologies. Beyond this substrate, RAPS further incorporates two coherent overlays: (i) Reactive Subscription, enabling agents to dynamically refine their intents; and (ii) Bayesian Reputation, empowering each agent with a local watchdog to detect and isolate malicious peers. Extensive experiments over five benchmarks showcase that our design effectively reconciles adaptivity, scalability, and robustness in a unified multi-agent coordination framework.
  </details>

- **[What If TSF: Evaluating Scenario-Guided Multimodal Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08509)**  `arXiv:2601.08509`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jinkwan Jang, Hyunbin Jin, Hyungjin Park, Kyubyung Chae, Taesup Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled time series forecasting to move beyond numerical observations and incorporate external information in multimodal settings. Such information can improve forecasting performance, but accuracy alone may not reveal whether models appropriately respond to it: a model may ignore relevant information, fail to distinguish scenarios with different implications, or overreact to irrelevant or weak signals. We introduce What If TSF (WIT), a benchmark for evaluating whether models effectively respond to future scenarios. WIT constructs controlled scenario sets by fixing the forecasting state while systematically varying future scenarios, enabling three complementary evaluations: Factual Comparison, which measures the predictive utility of factual scenario information; Relational Comparison, which evaluates whether forecasts satisfy expected direction, contrast, intensity, and restraint relations across scenarios; and Grounded Comparison, which provides a complementary empirical assessment of scenario-induced revision trajectories where comparable real cases are available. Experiments show that factual accuracy gains do not consistently translate into appropriate responses to alternative scenarios, demonstrating the need to evaluate multimodal forecasting beyond predictive accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Needwise: Generation-Free Conversational Recommendation from Need-Level Typed Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31175)**  `arXiv:2611.31175`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sungchul Kim, Weixiao Huang, Mekala Raja Sekhar Reddy, Amrutha Srinivasan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational recommenders must rank items against needs that catalogs record only in part, while users add, withdraw and reverse needs and switch between requests. LLM rankers re-read the dialogue at every turn: they are slow, and their ordering does not show which needs an item meets. We show that a conversational recommender can be built from typed decisions instead: named questions over fixed options, answered with option probabilities rather than generated text. On each turn, one typed request maps the message onto the catalog's fields, keeps what no field expresses in the user's words, and decides how the turn changes each earlier need. Needwise then ranks candidates must-first: the needs on the kind of item, its place and its price form a tier, and within it one holistic decision and need-level verdicts (supported, contradicted or unknown) are combined with equal weight (Needwise-HF). With the graded attributes withheld from the records, so that needs must be inferred from names, descriptions and reviews, Needwise-HF ranks Google Local requests significantly better than GPT-6 Sol (0.383 against 0.343 nDCG@10) and is not significantly different from the strongest LLM ranker on Google Local and Yelp; these comparisons keep their sign under every treatment of unrecorded attributes we test. It costs a fraction of GPT-6 Sol's API price and answers in 2.5 s at the median on Google Local, against 5.6 s for GPT-6 Sol. In conversations, the typed state keeps the ranking across switches and returns, but it recognizes only 19-27% of withdrawn needs.
  </details>

- **[LiveLoRA: Exact KV Cache Migration Across LoRA Updates for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31105)**  `arXiv:2611.31105`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianyi Huang, Samuel Xu, Ivy Gu, Nathan Huang, Siqi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Updating an LLM adapter should not require replaying the full interaction history of every active session. Yet reusing key-value (KV) cache entries computed under an earlier version mixes model versions, while full-history re-prefill becomes increasingly costly as histories and session fleets grow. We introduce LiveLora, a model-runtime co-design for exact final-block KV cache migration. Frozen lower decoder blocks and fixed K/V adapter down-projections preserve each token's base projections and low-rank coordinates across adapter versions. The runtime reconstructs candidate K/V, reevaluates the latest token through the final block, and commits the adapter, cache, and next-token logits together. We prove continuation equivalence to full-history re-prefill in exact arithmetic under the stated model assumptions. On Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, median paired publication speedups over full-history re-prefill average $68.98\times$ across five runs for 32 active sessions with 8K tokens each. Serving benchmarks show approximately 4.8% decoding overhead relative to native cached decoding. Compared with retained-boundary replay, compact retention gives a $3.42\times$ reduction in additional session storage at 8K, with similar measured publication-plus-decoding cost. LiveLora allows persistent LLM agents to adopt new adapter versions without replaying their full interaction histories.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting Learning to Delegate: LLM Discovered Heuristics for Large-Scale Vehicle Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31090)**  `arXiv:2611.31090`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengyang Wang, Renfei Tan, Shuze Daniel Liu, David Simchi-Levi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient vehicle routing is critical to logistics and transportation, yet solving large-scale capacitated vehicle routing problems (CVRP) remains computationally challenging due to their combinatorial complexity. One approach to solve large-scale vehicle routing optimization is to identify promising subproblems and then optimize them using solvers. In this paper, we propose a framework of automatic heuristic design (AHD) in which a large language model (LLM) autonomously refines heuristics for selecting subproblems, enabling effective optimization of large-scale CVRP instances. Compared to prior approaches that train task-specific neural networks to select among heuristically constructed subproblems, our method discovers generalizable, interpretable LLM-generated heuristics to directly construct subproblems. Experiments show that our approach achieves comparable in-distribution performance while outperforming neural network-based policies on out-of-distribution instances with large-scale problems.
  </details>

- **[Automatic Occupational Coding through Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31051)**  `arXiv:2611.31051`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mathijs A. Langezaal, Egon L. van den Broek, Lukas P.A. Arts, Marcel Goldberg, Grégoire Rey, Melissa C. Friesen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Annually 1.9 million deaths and 89.7 million disability-adjusted life years are caused by exposure to agents during work (e.g., welding fumes, silica dust, and noise pollution). To improve worker health, high-quality exposure assessments are crucial. Therefore, free-text job descriptions from large-scale occupational cohorts are manually standardized through occupational classifications and linked to hazardous exposures using Job Exposure Matrices (JEM). However, manual coding is slow, unreliable (i.e., 44-89% inter-coder reliability), and costly, due to i) short, ambiguous free-text job descriptions, ii) classifications containing hundreds to thousands of categories, and iii) the absence of gold standards. With 46%-79% accuracy, state-of-the-art automatic occupational coding tools achieve classification performance on human level. However, their performance drops to 17-26% on out-of-distribution datasets. Furthermore, their supervised architectures limit them to occupational classifications present in the training set. Consequently, JEM using other classifications are incompatible, leaving vital exposure-response associations unexplored. Our Retrieval-Augmented Generation framework, OPERAG, enables classification into any occupational classification without expert-labelled databases. It retrieves coding index information and combines this with Large Language Model-based classification. Validated on AsiaLymph's 36,062 real-world job descriptions, OPERAG achieved 42.6% occupational classification accuracy on the 4th coding level, substantially outperforming existing tools and approaching human coders' 51-56% inter-coder reliability. Using two JEM named ALOHA+ and SHOCK, 89.9-98.6% accuracy was achieved on exposure assessment. OPERAG approaches human-level, universal occupational classification, enabling efficient and effective large-scale exposure assessment to improve worker health.
  </details>

- **[Grower-in-the-loop AI for Deployable Precision Fertilisation in Sustainable Agriculture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31044)**  `arXiv:2611.31044`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xulong Wang, Yu Zhang, Gaoshan Bi, Menghui Zhou, Simon Jeffery, Max Davis, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In agriculture, typically over half of the applied nitrogen fertiliser is lost to water and to air. Precision fertilisation aims to reduce these losses while maintaining yield and quality, yet existing tools struggle to capture farm-specific challenges in determining optimal nitrogen rates. Static recommendations are common and overlook on-farm constraints, such as delays due to weather or machinery availability, or change in budgets. Each constraint governs when and how growers apply fertiliser in practice. Here we present a grower-in-the-loop framework for winter wheat in England, combining baseline fertilisation planning from a machine learning-derived nitrogen-response curve with in-season adaptation via a large language model. We evaluated the framework using data from long-term experiments (LTEs), replicated field trials, and farm deployments. Retrospective model validation using LTEs confirmed low economic decision-loss across multiple seasons. Using field and farm trials, baseline schedules were shown to match standard-practice yields with less nitrogen, whereas in-season adjusted schedules additionally retained grain quality standards. Given this enhanced performance, in-season adaptation absorbed weather, machinery and budget constraints to keep recommendations executable. The framework therefore shows promise for translating static recommendations into adaptable, grower-informed decisions for sustainable fertiliser management.
  </details>

- **[Testing Quantized KV-Cache Maintenance: Encoding Layout and Historical Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30720)**  `arXiv:2611.30720`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyang Huang, Songwei Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeating rows then selecting originals retains layout-dependent drift despite equal encoding counts. We measure when existing checks fail to establish preservation contracts in fixed quantized KV-cache implementations. In four-bit HQQ axis zero at sixteen cycles, median paired relative-L2 increases over same-shape re-encoding are 7.22 percentage points for Qwen3-1.7B and 11.59 for SmolLM3-3B. Matched encoded states cover sixteen shared questions per model at one and sixteen cycles. Axis one matches the shape-preserving control; lazy reorders preserve the initial state. On one known input, fixed update counts retain the difference; preserving group members removes it. Our prefix-view extension passes current-prefix checks yet changes history and next logits in six model/codec settings at residual capacities four and 128. Public lookup reaches this boundary, including one of four precommitted project-new GPU inputs; fixed query swaps isolate a conditional old-history effect. Checkpoint/dense rollback is exact. An explicit delayed lookup client admits a supported 129-token path excluded by native AR on one known context. Failed task-harm expansion precludes population failure-rate or general answer-degradation claims. Native MLX-LM fixtures expose stale caller caches; synchronization restores the tested in-place contract with native computations preserved. In rejection tests, stale callers change logits but not greedy tokens.
  </details>

- **[Uncovering Hidden Behaviours in Language Models via Limited-Parameter Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30672)**  `arXiv:2611.30672`  `cs.AI`  
  _Boyd Kane, Jo J. Jiao, Elizabeth Donoway, Bryce Woodworth, Alex Cloud, Alexander M. Turner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can have behaviours which only arise in hard-to-anticipate contexts. These hidden behaviours pose a challenge to alignment audits, which seek to uncover problematic model behaviour but are limited by the scenarios the auditor can anticipate. To address this challenge, we explore limited-parameter finetuning (LPF) on examples of a target behaviour. While training 3-25% of LoRA parameters, LPF reveals whether the model has a hidden behaviour, even when standard behavioural evaluations cannot. LPF succeeds on a suite of model organisms with hidden behaviours (8-14B parameters), and also surfaces naturally occurring hidden behaviours in open-weight models (27-35B parameters). LPF can thus distinguish between models which appear behaviourally identical to an auditor, and does not require the context in which the hidden behaviour surfaces. LPF provides a novel affordance that may be useful for auditing frontier AI models. However, we do not evaluate LPF against baselines in a realistic auditing setting, so further work is needed to determine its practicality.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting LLMs as Context Engineers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30494)**  `arXiv:2611.30494`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junrui Zhang, Zeyu Xu, Runchuan Zhu, Jie Peng, Long Zhang, Zixin Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) are used in more and more new tasks, context engineering becomes a fundamental method to efficiently steer LLMs by constructing instruction prompts or metadata without intensive training. A growing number of LLM context engineering (LCE) approaches automate the task by casting the LLM itself as the engineer that draws generalizable instructions or experiences from offline trials as context. Although these methods resemble how humans learn from experiences, it remains unclear \textbf{when and how} LLMs can engineer effective context from data. To answer the question, we first establish a benchmark composed of datasets with different reasoning patterns. We compared different LCE methods with different LLM families in 8B size, showing that there is no single winner for all tasks, even compared with simple baselines like chain-of-thought. Even when scaling model capabilities via more parameters or generation iterations, LCEs do not consistently boost existing performance. Across all datasets and models, the performance gains of LCEs are negatively correlated ($r=-0.5, p\le 0.05$) with the diversity of the failure modes of reasoning trajectories. Investigating how LCEs work, we find that (1) the improvement in instruction adherence can explain some success of LCEs; (2) one major bottleneck is whether LLMs can extract effective experiences out of natively diverse failures. Through this study, we uncover the limited capabilities of LCEs in abstracting principled contexts from diverse failure modes and understand how LCEs work, providing guidelines for future work.
  </details>

- **[Automated Call for Action: RAG-Based Environmental Law Enforcement Screening System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30423)**  `arXiv:2611.30423`  `cs.AI`  
  _Matas Jurkevicius, Yen-Chia Hsu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Environmental law inspectors face a structural issue in identifying potential violations from data: they often do not have the capacity to deal with large volume of data present in the emission events. This paper examines to what extent an LLM-based system can support evidence assembly and permit-based screening of such events, using the coke plants of a steel producer and a regional environmental service as a case. A simple puff-trajectory model aligns air quality measurements, weather and citizen complaints with each emission event. A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline then retrieves facility-specific permit conditions and generates event reports and a period report with an investigation recommendation. Across 74 events from March 2025, plant-aware re-ranking reduced reports citing the other plant's conditions from 58.1% (hybrid retrieval) to 0% by rule-based plant labels, while maintaining high RAGAS and LLM-judge metrics. GPT-5-mini performed comparably to GPT-5.1, whereas GPT-5-nano scored lower on legal grounding and legal faithfulness. In an evidence ablation, removing air quality (AQ) data changed judge scores and period recommendations most; citation overlap changed only slightly under any removal, and removing complaint details had no measurable effect. This suggests a need for rules and guidelines that link complaints to incidents and permit conditions, so that community reports can count as evidence. The results support screening of events that may need further investigation. Design implications for involving environmental service experts are derived.
  </details>

- **[HWRE-Bench: Can LLM Agents Understand Hardware They Did Not Design?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30422)**  `arXiv:2611.30422`  `cs.AI`  
  _Peilong Zhou, Cangyuan Li, Kaiyan Chang, Ying Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hardware benchmarks for LLM agents all run the design flow forward, from a specification to RTL, a layout or a bug fix. We ask the reverse question: given an implemented artifact, such as a layout, a gate-level netlist or a protected simulation model, can an agent work out what it does well enough to act on that knowledge? We introduce HWRE-Bench, a benchmark for agentic hardware reverse engineering built on one observation. Each step of the forward flow discards information (names, hierarchy, word-level structure, generator parameters, the programming model), and a reverse-engineering task asks the agent to recover what was lost and to prove it with an executable deliverable that the artifact alone cannot supply. Since layout extraction is now mechanical and a correct netlist is already a cycle-exact model, every task carries a mandatory copy-the-netlist control that must score zero. HWRE-Bench spans five tracks (Extract, Abstract, Audit, Interoperate, Modify), four information-loss levels and white- versus black-box access, all on original designs. We report a pilot of 148 rollouts from two agents on four seed tasks under 16 conditions, with stage-annotated trajectories. Agents built their own layout extractors and audited a sanitized layout as reliably as a netlist, at about 27% more turns. Keyless, equivalence-preserving obfuscation changed neither pass rates nor defect-repair scores. Whether agents read the structure at all depended on task framing rather than on necessity, so pass rate alone does not show understanding. Provider safety filters stopped 16% of one agent's runs on the audit tasks. These results argue for deliverables beyond equivalence and for reporting run validity explicitly.
  </details>

- **[TamperCert: A Benchmark for Detecting Text Tampering in Degree Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30419)**  `arXiv:2611.30419`  `cs.AI`  
  _ChiFung Kwan, Kangzhong Wang, Youqian Zhang, Weijie Chen, Jiaqi Gao, Yongjian Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Degree certificates play a critical role in high-stakes decisions such as university admission, immigration, and hiring. The issuing institution vouches for the holder, so a forged certificate may let its bearer pass these checks. Degree certificate forgery is increasingly prevalent. Yet, many assume that it is easy to spot. Reported cases show otherwise: in 2024, the business school of the University of Hong Kong found that about 30 master's students had enrolled with forged academic credentials. Few studies have looked at how to detect forged degree certificates, partly because public benchmarks are scarce. We present TamperCert, a benchmark built from 400 real degree certificates issued by 38 universities. Each certificate was subjected to 11 publicly available tampering methods to alter the holder's name and further processed with four additional post-processing techniques, resulting in over 20,000 forged images. We evaluate 13 detectors, a commercial multimodal large language model (MLLM), and detectors trained onthis http URLfindings reveal that no detector is reliable for every tampering method. Consequently, a determined forger may still evade detection. Moreover, the commercial MLLM misses most forgeries made by general-purpose generative models, and post-processing, such as resizing an image or photographing it with a camera, severely degrades detection.
  </details>

- **[mini-psych-agent: Evaluating Human-Like Behavior in LLM Agents through Interactive Psychological Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30365)**  `arXiv:2611.30365`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yi-Fei Cao, Tianxin Huang, Hanbo Xie, Jian-Qiao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A Large Language Model (LLM), its harness, and the test-time compute budget jointly determine its behavior. Consequently, the same underlying model, embedded in different agentic workflows or allocated different amounts of test-time compute, can exhibit markedly different performance. Psychologists face an analogous challenge when evaluating human performance: the same person may behave differently under different incentives and levels of deliberation. Psychological tasks therefore provide an ideal testbed for studying such behavioral variation. However, when adapted to LLMs, these tasks are typically text-transcribed, replaying fixed, language-rendered experiments as static prompts. This format is ill-suited to agent evaluation, where actions can alter future observations and task states. Conversely, although dynamic environments are prevalent in the evaluation of LLM agents, they rarely ask whether the resulting behavior is human-like. Here, we bridge this gap by transforming psychological tasks into dynamic environments in which agents can interact and by using a learned human-policy model to evaluate how compatible the resulting behavioral trajectories are with human behavior. Across these environments, we find that this compatibility varies with an LLM's agentic configuration and the psychological task, and that memory raises it mainly by making choices more predictable rather than more human-like. These findings motivate a more holistic evaluation of LLM agents in psychology-inspired environments, accounting for both the backbone model and its agentic scaffold.
  </details>

- **[Learning Heterogeneous Reasoning in Large Language Models: From Mode Compatibility to Local Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30281)**  `arXiv:2611.30281`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sunbowen Li, Dandan Song, Shengkun Ma, Xuanning Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training large language models routinely pools reasoning trajectories from diverse teachers or algorithms, yet it remains unclear whether standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can learn such heterogeneous strategies without explicit mode conditioning. We study the learnability and local bottlenecks of heterogeneous reasoning through a formal mixture formulation and controlled experiments. We first formalize the representational compatibility of the optimal autoregressive model with latent reasoning mixtures: the target next-token distribution weights modes by their prefix posteriors and concentrates on a single mode once alternative modes assign negligible likelihood to the prefix. In a verifiable symbolic graph-search environment with paired breadth-first and depth-first search trajectories, no consistent disadvantage is observed for unlabeled mixed SFT relative to homogeneous or labeled baselines under matched token budgets while preserving both modes. Scaling across 0.6B--14B models and data budgets largely eliminates global mode confusion, shifting the primary bottleneck to local state-tracking transitions. Because a local mistake affects the final outcome only when subsequent reasoning is sensitive to that step, we find that only a small fraction of local errors are outcome-determining \emph{Critical Reasoning Nodes (CRNs)}, and that single-step repairs at these nodes rescue failing trajectories across symbolic search and natural language benchmarks whenever the model has sufficient downstream competence to complete the remaining derivation. Our environment is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoSECO: Automated Skill Evolution for Cross-Problem Combinatorial Optimization with General-Purpose LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30139)**  `arXiv:2611.30139`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zirui Zhu, Cong Zhang, Ni Zhang, Kuan Xu, Zhiguang Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose large language model (LLM) agents can develop complete solvers for combinatorial optimization problems. Their specialization, however, often relies on workflows engineered for individual problems, limiting the reuse of development expertise. We propose AutoSECO, an automated skill evolution method that learns reusable solver-development guidance from experience. The challenge is that external evaluation measures the completed solver, while the skill guides decisions during development. AutoSECO connects this feedback to guidance deficiencies by tracing performance shortfalls through solver code and recorded development decisions. Revisions are expressed as general instructions and retained only if a fresh solver developed under the revised skill achieves a lower mean evaluation gap.
