Evolved solely on the traveling salesperson problem (TSP), the skill guides fresh agents in developing solvers for capacitated vehicle routing, job-shop scheduling, and three-dimensional bin packing without further skill evolution. On TSP, the final mean benchmark gap is 0.52\%, compared with 0.98\% for the skill-evolution baseline SkillOpt. Across all four problems, the evolved skill achieves better aggregate results than no skill guidance, the initial skill, and SkillOpt. The same frozen skill also supports solver development under alternative model--harness configurations. These findings suggest that solver-development experience can be retained as reusable expertise for general-purpose LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[WASP: Continual Weight Adaptation for Streaming LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30134)**  `arXiv:2611.30134`  `cs.AI`  
  _Linxi Xie, Chonghua Liao, Mengxuan Wu, Guandao Yang, Zhangyang Wang, Kai Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized large language models (LLMs) should continually adapt to users' evolving preferences. Yet extracting preference changes from noisy interaction histories while supporting inexpensive updates remains challenging. We introduce WASP (Weight Adaptation for Streaming Personalization), a recurrent framework that captures fine-grained preferences directly from raw user interactions and generates personalized LoRA adapters without expensive per-user optimization. Specifically, WASP achieves this by training a shared hypernetwork that maintains a compact user representation and outputs adapter weights in a single forward pass. As new interactions arrive, WASP updates this representation without reprocessing full interaction history, enabling continual adaptation with per-update costs independent of history length. Experiments on personalization benchmarks and chronological interaction streams show that WASP sustains high-quality personalization over long horizons under fixed compute and storage budgets. Together, these results support efficient streaming personalization and facilitate privacy-preserving on-device deployment.
  </details>

- **[Small data machine learning: the trilemma of low-sample tabular regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30118)**  `arXiv:2611.30118`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ilya Emelyanov, Dmitrii Dobrydin, Muhammad Hamza, Malak Abid Ali Khan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small tabular datasets leave few observations for choosing a model and assessing its predictions. We examine these competing demands in controlled simulations and on six public datasets, using SmallGBM and SmallMLP as fixed-configuration case studies. Three results illustrate why the choice of evaluation matters. First, at n = 100, five-fold cross-validation overstated the gain from tuning XGBoost by 0.063 ROC-AUC on random labels, while tuning improved independent-test ROC-AUC by 0.0078 on a Gaussian mixture. Second, when 20% of binary training labels were flipped, the median within-trial ROC-AUC loss was 0.0210 for SmallGBM and 0.0778 for a fixed 200-tree XGBoost configuration. A separate regression experiment showed the sensitivity of squared-error training to heavy-tailed responses. Third, in a heteroscedastic regression experiment, locally weighted SmallMLP intervals covered about one percentage point more test responses than ordinary split conformal intervals at both tested dimensions. Their mean widths also increased. On a Gaussian classification task with linear log odds, logistic regression achieved the highest mean ROC-AUC at all seven training sizes. These experiments identify settings in which a small-data model helps and quantify the cost of its assumptions.
  </details>

- **[Post-Training Agents for Long-Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30090)**  `arXiv:2611.30090`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sushant Mehta, Logan Ritchie, Edwin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Professional documents are a long-context workload that retrieval-style benchmarks do not capture. In a ten-domain enterprise collection, a task's evidence is spread across a source document of median length 27 pages (mean 48.8, maximum 873); a third of tasks rest mainly on charts, drawings, and maps; and 70.9% require two or more reasoning hops. We post-train Kimi-K2.7, an open-weight foundation model, at its full context window on 1,465 expert-authored tasks with atomic rubrics from that collection, training rank-32 adapters with one round of privileged-context on-policy self-distillation followed by two rounds of rubric-reward reinforcement learning. We render each PDF as text for training and as extracted text plus selected page images at evaluation. The smoothed RL training reward rises from 0.37 in the first tenth of the run to 0.67 in the last. On the 100-taskthis http URLholdout, served in a 262,144-token window, strict pass (all rubric criteria met) more than doubles after the second round, from 11.0% to 24.5%, with gains across input lengths up to 240K tokens. Mean tokens generated stay flat (9,803 to 9,733), so the gain does not come from longer outputs. GPT-5.6 Sol's leaderboard strict pass is 30.7% at a measured 3,422 tokens. On 220 out-of-domain GDPval tasks, where the model runs as a long-horizon tool-using agent, mean rubric reward rises from 0.571 to 0.746 after the third round and zero-credit tasks fall from 54 to 10, while messages per task fall 18%, from 70.9 to 58.2. In paired trajectories, the third round replaces a context-thrashing retry loop with one up-front check of all sources, and verification extends from checking that files exist to checking what they contain. We argue that long-context evaluation of agents should report accuracy jointly with tokens and turns, and that datasets of long documents should ship text and image renderings alongside each PDF.
  </details>

- **[Semantic repeatability and reproducibility of GPT-5 and Gemini 3 Flash on H5N1 One Health questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29859)**  `arXiv:2611.29859`  `cs.AI`  
  _Luizza Dalla Pria, Amelia l. Nelson, Renata Ivanek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) has re-emerged as a One Health concern since 2024, and the public is increasingly consulting large-language-model (LLM) chatbots for health information during outbreaks. Before a chatbot's accuracy can be meaningfully assessed, its consistency must first be understood, so that any single response can be trusted as a fair representative of the model's behavior. We characterized the semantic repeatability and reproducibility of GPT-5 (OpenAI) and Gemini 3 Flash (Google) on 15 H5N1 One Health questions sourced from social media discourse by adopting and extending a centroid-based evaluation framework. Each question was submitted 30 times and reformulated using five Chain-of-Thought (CoT) variants with 30 runs each, yielding 5,400 responses. Pairwise cosine similarity was computed on MPNet embeddings, with sensitivity analyses across three additional transformers. Statistical inference combined standard nonparametric tests (paired Wilcoxon and Friedman with Holm--Bonferroni correction) with inter-embedding agreement statistics (ICC(2,k) and Cronbach's $\alpha$) introduced here as a methodological addition. Both models produced high mean consistency (0.93--0.97, rescaled to {[}0, 1{]}). GPT-5 showed reproducibility significantly higher than repeatability (W = 3.0, p = 0.0003), whereas for Gemini 3 Flash the two metrics were statistically indistinguishable (W = 42.0, p = 0.33). Differential Diagnosis ranked among the top two CoT framings for consistency in both models. The tested chatbots behave as semantically stable sources on H5N1 One Health questions, and the developed consistency evaluation framework is portable to other domains.
  </details>

- **[Who Makes the Decision? Auditing an Agentic LLM Pipeline for Illicit Cryptocurrency Transaction Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29829)**  `arXiv:2611.29829`  `cs.AI`  
  _Vaishnavi Chaughule, Yuchen Zhang, Mohammed Ahnaf Tajwar, Sai Akshay Menta, Saanidhya Vats, Vinija Jain, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic pipelines that combine graph detectors, deterministic rules, retrieval-augmented generation and an LLM judge are being proposed to monitor illicit cryptocurrency flows and to write regulation-citing justifications for analysts. They are evaluated on end-to-end accuracy or on the quality of those justifications, and neither shows which component made a decision. We audit such a pipeline on the Elliptic++ Bitcoin dataset with decision-source attribution, a trace-level protocol with three layers: per-case actual causation, interventional Shapley credit, and mediation of upstream risk scores. A deterministic wallet-score override carries about 94% of the credit for flags, and a verification gate cancels most of the judge's own flags, which fall on licit transactions. Testing the judge alone over 76 evidence bundles, four backbones and two prompts, we find that it follows an injected risk score on the anonymous transaction features it is given, although stronger models decide correctly when the facts are explicit; that a context too short for the system prompt leaves it with no verdict at all; and that it cites regulatory clauses that do not apply, which a substring-based citation check accepts. Hiding the score does not fix the judge, because evidence gathered with the score visible still encodes it. For illicit-activity monitoring, an LLM's justification should therefore not be read as evidence of which component flagged a transaction.
  </details>

- **[Latent-Predisposition Personas for Heterogeneous and Reliable LLM Survey Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29805)**  `arXiv:2611.29805`  `cs.AI`  
  _Geonwoo Bang, Dongho Kim, Moohong Min_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM personas are used to simulate survey publics, which should reproduce not only answer distributions but also within-person coherence and stability. Drawing on mass belief systems research, we show that persona-conditioned LLMs produce over-constrained publics. Across three model families and three U.S. surveys, opinions on unrelated issues are 1.6-4.6 times as correlated as among humans, and party identification explains 2.5-7.6 times as much answer variance. Common remedies (temperature, diversity instructions, backstories) weaken these correlations by making answers more random. On Qwen, their ten-day test-retest reliability falls to 33-59% of the human level, and when made consistent they lose correlation fit. We propose Latent-Predisposition Personas (LPP), whose persons differ stably, not randomly. Each persona receives political engagement, value priorities, and party-relative issue deviations that grow as engagement falls. Answers are sharpened and calibrated to toplines and party crosstabs. At selected operating points, LPP is never significantly worse than any baseline and far more reliable. At matched human-level reliability, it has the lowest correlation error in all six model-survey combinations, also at a lower target. Errors still exceed those between human samples, and gains on framing effects and a frontier closed model are partial.
  </details>

- **[Fin-RSI: A Unified Library of Financial Skills, Checks, and Self-Improvement for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29716)**  `arXiv:2611.29716`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanghao Ye, Shwai He, Sihan Chen, Yiting Wang, Bowei Tian, Guoheng Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) become more capable, financial agents need tools they can readily discover and use. Yet financial software is fragmented across libraries with different interfaces and conventions, making it difficult to build reliable agent workflows. We present Fin-RSI, a unified Python library of financial skills, checks, and self-improvement designed for LLM agents. It brings together tools for market analysis, forecasting, portfolio construction, and trading research through shared interfaces across models and agent frameworks. Agents can find suitable tools from natural-language requests, reuse tested implementations, and improve generated programs through checking and feedback. Developers can extend the library with new tools without rebuilding integrations for each model. Across 20 open-weight models on six trading-position tasks, tool discovery increased the proportion of episodes producing valid programs from 9.7% to 29.2%; for models with at least 14 billion parameters, it rose from 23.6% to 64.6%. We release the library and benchmark as a shared resource for the community and plan to expand its tools and task coverage as financial agents evolve.
  </details>

- **[Wactorz: Self-Writing LLM Agents That Discover, Repair, and Migrate at the Edge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29619)**  `arXiv:2611.29619`  `cs.AI`  
  _Panagiotis Kasnesis, Lazaros Toumanidis, Amalia Contiero Syropoulou, Christos Chatzigeorgiou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most LLM agent frameworks build a crew that runs a task and exits. Physical environments (e.g., homes, labs, factory floors) need agents that stay up for months, survive their own bugs, and run on whatever hardware is already next to the sensors. We present Wactorz, an open-source runtime in which LLM-generated agents are long-lived, supervised actors: a planner turns a chat request into Python, compiles it into a new actor at runtime, and a supervisor restarts that actor alone, with its state intact, when it crashes. All coordination rides on MQTT, so agents can be spawned on, and live-migrated between, edge nodes such as a Raspberry Pi, and every internal event is a topic external systems can subscribe to. The recorded demo builds a camera-to-lamp automation from one chat request, adds a second agent that is auto-wired to the first by topic and carries a deliberate division-by-zero, watches that agent repair its own code without losing its count, then deploys a fresh Raspberry Pi node and live-migrates the agent onto it, in under four minutes of wall-clock time and \$0.13 of model usage. This extended version adds what the two-page demonstration paper could not: the Wiring view of the topic graph, Wactorz as a library for hand-written agents, how trained models are stored, sent to nodes and replaced, the security model, and the system's limitations.
  </details>

- **[Emotion-Aware and Context-Grounded Dialogue Agents for Personalized Video Game Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29372)**  `arXiv:2611.29372`  `cs.AI`  
  _Michael Adjeisah, Jin Zhang, Pierluigi Vito Amadori, Ryan Spick, Eshani Kawshika Fernando, Kwame Omono Asamoah, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present ChatNPC, a game companion that dynamically personalizes its responses in real time based on players' emotional shifts to enhance user immersion. The model integrates sequential data, leveraging the dynamic nature of streams to benefit from player information, spoken lines, and in-game context. We leverage recent progress in LLMs' tool-calling capabilities to extract vital information from memory and recognize potential constraints for accurate reasoning, thereby tackling the complexity of NPC conversation scenarios. The task is divided into a game sentinel-guided agent (SeGent), a memory capability, and a chat planning tool for reasoning instantiation. The approach uses a lightweight game template as an information framework, with relevant details and a thorough reasoning layout. We conduct extensive experiments on a newly developed game dataset for in-game context NPC dialogue and demonstrate that ChatNPC sequentially captures players' emotional shifts over time; responses are more naturalistic and human-like with appropriate conversational cues, pauses, and sighs; and utterances remain faithful to the dynamics of in-game context and player actions, supporting narrative continuity.
  </details>

- **[Neural Agent Compiler: Learning to Schedule Soft Dependencies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29262)**  `arXiv:2611.29262`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bo Yan, Peng Wang, Zihan Chen, Ziwen Kan, Zhen Tan, Huiyuan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents accomplish complex tasks by coordinating multiple reasoning steps and tool operations, which form dependencies among their operations. Existing orchestration methods primarily consider hard dependencies that determine when an operation can legally begin, but overlook soft dependencies, i.e., information that is not required for execution but may improve an already executable operation. We identify soft-dependency scheduling as an important problem in agent execution. To this end, we introduce Neural Agent Compiler (NAC), a learned scheduler that predicts the task-quality and resource consequences of three execution modes and selects the highest-utility legal mode at a scheduling boundary. NAC represents the executable continuation, pending information event, and resource state in a shared memory, and uses a utility function to adapt the consequence predictions to different resource preferences. Across AppWorld, WebShop, and $\tau^2$-bench, NAC achieves 276/417 AppWorld task completions, a 74.69 WebShop Task Score, and 59/100 $\tau^2$-bench successes. Additional studies show that the learned scheduler reduces execution work, establishing soft-dependency scheduling as a promising solution for orchestrating tool-using agents.
  </details>

- **[Do You Even Know What I Want? Improving LLMs Ability to Detect Hidden User Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29028)**  `arXiv:2611.29028`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kelsey Sikes, Sarath Sreedharan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As with conventional AI agents, a common source of misalignment between Large Language Models (LLMs) and their human users is misspecified goals. Often, this happens when the human has skewed beliefs about the system's model and provides it with information that causes behavior different from what they expected, with potentially unwanted side effects. To ensure goal alignment, one solution would be for the LLM to ask the human questions about their objectives. However, it has been well-documented that LLMs most often provide default responses to user requests rather than try and ask questions to clarify their true intent. In this paper, we address these challenges by adapting a novel formulation for the objective misspecification problem to work with LLMs. We then test if LLMs that are prompted to ask questions about a user's true underlying objectives are better at identifying them compared to those that do not. We evaluate our method using four state-of-the-art LLMs tested on five standard IPC domains, showing that for some domains, asking questions greatly improved model performance.
  </details>

- **[STRIDE: Strategy-to-Tactics Retrieval-Guided Skill Disclosure and Evolution for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28907)**  `arXiv:2611.28907`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shixin Hong, Wanqing Cui, Yibo Hu, Naen Xu, Zhiwei Huang, Meiguang Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill-augmented reinforcement learning enables LLM agents to reuse task-solving knowledge across episodes, but existing systems typically expose agents to a flat skill pool and update skills only from coarse, episode-level outcomes. This conflates two distinct uses of experience: a stable strategy that should frame the whole episode, and tactical know-how that should be retrieved only when the agent reaches a local execution gap. We propose STRIDE (Strategy-to-Tactics Retrieval-guided skIll Disclosure and Evolution), a framework that brings progressive skill disclosure into agentic reinforcement learning. STRIDE keeps the selected strategy visible as episode context, exposes a compact tactic menu, and loads full tactic content only after an explicit retrieval action. This interface turns each retrieval into an event-level credit unit: the same event provides process feedback for learning when to retrieve and supplies evidence for skill-bank evolution. On ALFWorld, STRIDE achieves a 92.7% average success rate with Qwen3-4B and a 94.9% average success rate with Qwen3-8B. On WebShop, it improves over the strongest reproduced skill-augmented baseline by 4.5 score points and 1.9 success-rate percentage points. These results show that skill reuse is most effective when persistent strategies preserve global task structure and tactics are disclosed only at local decision bottlenecks.
  </details>

- **[Does a Frontier Large Language Model Know What It Can Solve? A 100-Conjecture Calibration Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28827)**  `arXiv:2611.28827`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ayush Khaitan, Liam Fowl, Tomàs Ortega, Alex Kontorovich, Sanjeev Arora_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How does confidence correlate with actual performance in large language models, when attacking open research-level math problems? We address this question by studying the performance of ChatGPT 5.6 Sol Ultra on a set of 100 open conjectures in geometric analysis. By construction, the model first selected open problems in geometric analysis that it assessed that it had at least a 50\% chance of proving or disproving, and then assigned a probability of success to each question. 13 questions were then excluded after an audit, and the model was asked to resolve the remaining 87 problems. Its mean stated resolution probability was $0.617$, versus an observed rate of $0.103$ (Brier score $0.351$, ECE $0.513$), thereby demonstrating that the model was systematically over-confident in its self-assessment. However, the scores retained ranking signal (AUROC $0.714$). The model was highly overconfident in probability magnitude but did more often than not assign a high probability of success to the problems that it did resolve, than the problems that it did not. Hence, its probabilities were numerically mis-calibrated but directionally correct. All proofs have been formalized in Lean, thereby verifying correctness, and have been run through a semantic equivalence pipeline to ensure semantic equivalence between the formalized and informal statements. These are exploratory campaign outcomes, not fixed-budget success probabilities. The certificates prove formal statements; for a couple of questions, some geometric inputs are assumed in the lean proofs.
  </details>

- **[Can Your AI Twin Meet People for You? It Can Hold a Conversation. But What Does It Need to Judge Like You?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28692)**  `arXiv:2611.28692`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanghao Ye, Sihan Chen, Bowei Tian, Yiting Wang, Shwai He, Guoheng Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Should your AI twin go meet people on your behalf? We built LLM twins of 710 real people from their other conversations in the CANDOR corpus, sent them to meet each other in 5,680 simulated first conversations, and checked every rehearsal against what the real people said after meeting for real (355 held-out, participant-disjoint pairs, against baselines that include each person's rating history). The rehearsals work: a twin's conversation predicts real liking ($\rho$=0.277), carries information that questionnaires and rating history do not have (partial $\rho$=0.16 [0.05, 0.26]), and recovers most of what the same reader gets from the real conversation (0.321). Swap controls show that this signal travels through the conversation, not through the persona card. What, then, does a twin need to judge like you? Not your voice: making twins talk more like their owners, with person-specific activation steering and a LoRA trained on their own speech, did not help in a pre-specified test and lowered accuracy in an exploratory second round (-0.183 [-0.294, -0.066]). Nor simply your stated ratings: a persona-prompted twin that holds the conversation does not yet carry its owner's judgment, and when told its owner's past ratings it anchors on them instead of weighing them. Learning helps: a small appraisal head trained on other real conversations, which combines the twin's post-call answers with its owner's rating history, lifts rehearsal-based prediction above the history baseline out of fold (+0.059 [0.001, 0.115], against +0.022 without the twin's answers; pre-specified primary contrast +0.056 [-0.001, 0.111]). We propose an attribution protocol for twin-based social simulation and identify learned appraisal as the component twins need next.
  </details>

- **[Improving the Improver: Semantic PID Control for Medical Agent Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28680)**  `arXiv:2611.28680`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dexia Chen, YiLong Hu, Wenqi Zhong, Guangyu Guo, Tony C. W. Mok, Ling Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-stakes vertical medical tasks, such as postoperative pathological staging and long-term survival prediction, demand multi-step clinical reasoning over heterogeneous evidence under weak supervision and high outcome uncertainty. While we first orchestrate CliAgent, a domain-tailored clinical agent workflow that systematically grounds predictions in multi-modal patient evidence, manually designed medical agents remain constrained by static, brittle rules. Moreover, existing recursive self-improvement (RSI) methods suffer from feedback ambiguity and severe development-set overfitting when evolving agents in high-noise medical domains. Focusing on improving the improver, we propose Semantic PID-RSI, an evidence-guided control-theoretic framework for medical agent self-improvement. Inspired by PID control, our improver evaluates candidate modification batches via three diagnostic roles: P isolates current clinical failure modes, I synthesizes longitudinal evidence from past trials, and D estimates response dynamics and regression risks (D-pre/D-post). Selected plans are compiled into behavior contracts for staged probing and independent adoption judging. Instantiated and validated on challenging oncology tasks (pT/pN staging and survival prognosis), evolving CliAgent with Semantic PID-RSI on a 3B-activated open-weight model achieves substantial gains (up to +6.27 C-index points), outperforming overfitting-prone RSI baselines and approaching the performance of frontier proprietary models (e.g., GPT-6 series) equipped with CliAgent.
  </details>

- **[Internalized Experience: How Training Data Shapes Memory Formation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28613)**  `arXiv:2611.28613`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xianxuan Long, Xinnan Dai, Kai Guo, Jiliang Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parametric memory enables large language models (LLMs) to retain information from past experience and reuse it in subsequent tasks. Yet how training data supports the internalization of reusable task rules remains unclear. We introduce FSMem (Finite-State Machine Memory), a state-transition framework for constructing training data and studying how data content and organization shape memory formation. We evaluate rule reuse across synthetic worlds, classical planning, and code execution through valid-action identification and execution-outcome prediction, complemented by next-operation prediction in code. Our experiments reveal several systematic learning patterns. Under matched training conditions, even unchanged task rules become harder to internalize when learned alongside a larger variety of action types; by contrast, scaling the training data to provide broader state-action coverage generally improves rule learning. We further find that training at a single execution depth produces depth-specialized behavior, with models performing best when training and test executions have similar lengths. Motivated by this finding, we construct cumulative corpora that combine short and long trajectories, which can outperform long-trajectory-only training while using fewer training tokens. Together, these results demonstrate that the same task rules can be internalized differently depending on how execution experience is constructed, and that understanding these learning patterns can guide the design of more effective training data for parametric memory.
  </details>

- **[Agent Money Benchmark: Can AI Agents Be Trusted to Spend Money?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28610)**  `arXiv:2611.28610`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhe Chen, Emma Hsieh, Vicky Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Giving an AI agent a wallet lets it buy data and services on a client's behalf. Trust then depends on how it spends money and what that spending achieves. We introduce Agent Money Benchmark (AMB) to test whether agents spend a client's money well, starting with purchases of information for financial decisions. We link seller quotes and payments to final answers, evaluating 12 models on 80 payment tasks with simulated sellers and 76 research questions using live data. Purchases settled in USDC on Solana mainnet via x402, a protocol for paying per web request. Three of the 12 models passed 79 of 80 payment tasks without payment or budget errors. Nine fell for a seller's trap at least once; repeat charges and above-list quotes accounted for 79% of scam events. We estimate waste from data and inference costs, counting failed payment tasks in full and research answers in proportion to missed points. With spending split equally between tracks, estimated waste ranged from \$21.0 per \$100 for Claude Fable 5.2 to \$70.8 for GPT-6 Luna.
  </details>

- **[EvoViM: Recursively Self-Evolving Visual Agents for Exploration with Deferred Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28574)**  `arXiv:2611.28574`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Cao, Tianjie Ju, Zhuosheng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language model (MLLM) agents have shown promising capabilities in task-oriented environments. However, they still struggle when queries are deferred until after exploration, requiring them to retain visual evidence before knowing which details will matter. We introduce EvoViM, a recursively self-evolving framework in which a frozen multimodal model turns its own interaction experience into one visual program for memory and skills. The program retains images and computes directly on pixels, and its operations decide how this evidence is used in each task. During memory evolution, the same model traces failures in its visual trajectories to the operations that lost or misused evidence and revises them. It also rewrites the notes and analysis tools that guide later revisions, which then build on earlier findings and skip changes that have already failed. We evaluate EvoViM with three multimodal models on six tasks covering visual matching, spatial recall, and sequential decision-making, including three tasks with deferred queries. Across the six tasks, EvoViM improves the average score over AutoMem, the strongest baseline in the main comparison, by 31.3-47.6 points on held-out episodes. Programs written once from the initial environment instructions perform substantially worse, highlighting the value of interaction experience in shaping how visual evidence is retained and used. Code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Unit Tests: Path-Aware Rubric-Guided LLM Autograding of Notebooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28565)**  `arXiv:2611.28565`  `cs.AI`  
  _Salman Hajizada, Mohammed Yusuf Ansari, Gianni Di Caro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jupyter notebooks support open-ended, multi-step assessments in which students build one logical flow across interdependent steps, combining code, written analysis, and generated outputs such as plots. These reflect authentic scientific practice but are hard to grade consistently, since students may make different yet equally valid choices at each step, so a later answer can only be judged in light of the choices a student made earlier. We present an LLM-based autograder for such flow-structured notebook questions. It generates a rubric from a reference solution, then applies a softening pass that relaxes criteria tied to the reference so legitimate alternative paths are not penalized. Each question group is graded in a single model call, so later answers are judged against the student's own earlier choices, using question-type tags and schema-constrained, low-temperature outputs. Evidence is scored multimodally: code, text, numerical outputs, and figures from code cells. We deployed the tool in a machine-learning course, integrated with Gradescope, with a pre-submission format check and prompt-injection defenses, and evaluate it both in that live course and against archived submissions graded by staff in earlier terms. We report agreement, cross-model variance, cost, student perceptions, and injection resilience. With low-temperature models, agreement with human scores is often strong, including on the open-ended items the design specifically targets, while run-to-run stability depends sharply on the model's temperature support.
  </details>

- **[X-AQA: Unifying Action Quality Assessment with Multimodal Large Language Models and Multi-Domain Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28548)**  `arXiv:2611.28548`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaili Zheng, Wei Ding, Yuchen Ding, Kaiwen Wang, Xun Zhu, Chenyi Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action Quality Assessment (AQA) aims to evaluate the quality of human actions, yet existing methods are predominantly developed and evaluated on individual datasets with different task formulations and assessment criteria. We introduce X-AQA, the first unified training and evaluation framework for general-purpose AQA with multimodal large language models (MLLMs). We construct X-AQA-40K, a training corpus comprising 40,163 samples from 22 data sources across four domains: competitive sports, fitness exercises, rehabilitation, and general skills. We convert heterogeneous annotations into a unified question-answering format, incorporating dataset-specific annotation guidelines and official scoring rules when available. Using this corpus, we fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B to develop a single MLLM capable of handling diverse AQA tasks. We further establish X-AQA-Bench, which evaluates both in-distribution (ID) performance on datasets represented during training and out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization on four held-out datasets. The resulting model outperforms all evaluated open-source MLLMs on X-AQA-Bench and achieves competitive performance relative to proprietary MLLMs. Ablation studies further demonstrate that multi-domain joint training and explicitly providing assessment criteria in prompts both improve OOD generalization. X-AQA establishes a unified foundation for advancing general-purpose AQA across diverse real-world domains. Data and models are released at \hyperlink{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[Diversity over Scale: Recursive Self-Improvement with Ensembles of Small Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28420)**  `arXiv:2611.28420`  `cs.AI`  
  _Navapat Nananukul, Mayank Kejriwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advancement of large language models (LLMs) has significantly enhanced the capabilities of agents across various tasks. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) loops, in which a language model repeatedly rewrites a program and builds on its previous version, now perform well in machine-learning tasks, but they rely on frontier models and hundreds of calls per run. Small open-weight models are far cheaper, yet when used alone, their programs often crash and their improvements stall. We propose DiVE (diverse models evolve), an ensemble method for RSI that aims to improve creativity and diversity of generated solutions. DiVE uses three components: a shared memory, a failure log, and best-idea-wins rounds. This allows models to write candidate solutions from the same context and retain the best idea at each iteration. On 22 MLE-bench Lite competitions, an ensemble of five 8-12B open-weight models has 25.4% less test error than its average member and 16.7% less than Llama 3.3 70B, and comes within 7.1% of Qwen 3.5 122B-A10B at 75% of its cost. Diversity among small models can thus stand in for part of the scale that RSI relies on.
  </details>

- **[Schedule, Rank, Profile: Continuous Evaluation of LLM Agents in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28368)**  `arXiv:2611.28368`  `cs.AI`  
  _Martyna Plomecka, Yao Yan, DJ Sterling, Jon Lipovetz, John Schultz, Michael Aaron, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Games are a robust test for large language model agents: they require sequential decisions, adversarial or cooperative interaction, state tracking, reasoning under uncertainty, and long-horizon planning. However, a one-time game benchmark can go stale as models, prompts, environment implementations, and evaluation policies change, and refreshing it is costly: full round-robin evaluation scales poorly in the number of model pairs, and the resulting outcomes live on incompatible per-game scales. We present a framework for continuous evaluation of LLM agents in game settings, organized around three coupled components. First, an adaptive scheduler that maps pairwise Bradley-Terry uncertainty to reduced per-pair match targets, concentrating budget on comparisons that remain unresolved. Second, a Unified Leaderboard (ULB) that reduces heterogeneous outcomes to pairwise evidence and fits a game-balanced Bradley-Terry model by minorization-maximization with bootstrap intervals, so that high-throughput games cannot dominate the aggregate ranking. Third, a game skill-profile methodology that scores each game along diverse skill dimensions, making the capability coverage of the suite explicit and turning game selection into a design decision rather than an artifact of availability. In this paper, we specify the research design and outcome reduction of the current implementation and evaluate the framework on a ten-game suite spanning perfect-information board games, imperfect-information games, and social/language settings, reporting LLM capability profiles, evaluation-cost savings across model-refresh cycles, and an empirical Pareto frontier relating model performance to inference cost. As deployed, the scheduler saved 61.7% of episodes; it counted each episode in the two team games as four records, overstating their savings, and the other eight games save 61.9%.
  </details>

- **[Engram: Multi-Stage Memory for Agentic Tuning of Heterogeneous Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28356)**  `arXiv:2611.28356`  `cs.AI`  
  _Huanran Li, Nhat X.T. Le, Kevin De Angeli, Yao Xuan, Alireza Vahdatpour, Djordje Gligorijevic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can now tune neural networks, yet without cross-session memory they spend costly trials rediscovering known lessons. Reuse is difficult because production models differ in architecture, training setup and inference budget. We present Engram, a memory system that links past trial outcomes with the hypotheses and rationale behind them and assesses whether this evidence applies to the current model. An online agent curates evidence at the decision points where it is needed: Initial Guidance supplies validated lessons at the start, Session Retrieval supplies related past sessions before each batch of hypotheses is generated, and Trial Candidate Review checks each trial candidate against matching past trials. Between sessions, an offline agent distills validated lessons, making the memory self-improving. On two ads models, Engram reaches the 32-trial result of a no-memory control with 47% fewer trials and also ends with a better model on average.
  </details>

- **[SMAD: A Semantics-Guided Multi-Agent Framework for Unsupervised Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28348)**  `arXiv:2611.28348`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qinghua Liu, Junpeng Wang, Mingzhi Hu, Menghai Pan, Shubham Jain, Yuzhong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series anomaly detection (TSAD) lacks a one-size-fits-all detector for heterogeneous data and anomaly types. Existing automation is limited by implicit objectives, potentially unreliable historical knowledge, or high computational costs. More fundamentally, it selects how to detect anomalies without explicitly defining what to detect. We propose SMAD, an LLM-based multi-agent framework that translates anomaly intent into executable, testable detection pipelines. Rather than selecting detectors against an implicit target, SMAD establishes a natural-language semantic contract grounded in measurable time-series properties, specifying target event patterns, expected score responses, and validation questions. This contract governs both detector construction and evaluation: LLM agents propose pipelines, while contract-matched synthetic interventions provide label-free evidence from the target series to assess semantic coverage and guide iterative self-refinement. A semantics-aware ensemble integrates validated candidates and attributes each alert to contributing detectors and supporting evidence. On univariate and multivariate TSAD benchmarks, SMAD improves detection performance over the strongest baseline by 11.5% and 9.6%, respectively, while outperforming general-purpose coding agents at 2-5x lower language-model token cost.
  </details>

- **[Demystifying the Autoresearch Harness: How Context and Skills Shape Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28244)**  `arXiv:2611.28244`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kelvin Kiu-Wai Tam, Newt Hue-Nam K. Nguyen, Tianshi Zheng, Yunxiang Mo, Weihan Li, Zhongwei Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoresearch agents, which autonomously design, run, and refine experiments, are accelerating scientific progress, particularly in AI research. However, existing agents rely on harnesses that combine multiple mechanisms, making it difficult to determine how individual components contribute to performance. We systematically study harness design for long-horizon, self-evolving autoresearch agents along two dimensions: context management, which determines what an agent retains from its past attempts, and skill management, which governs how it stores and reuses lessons from those attempts. Across MLE-Bench and KernelBench with gpt-oss-120b, gpt-6-luna, and gpt-6-sol, we isolate the effects of individual harness design choices by evaluating each against a recency-truncation baseline within a shared agent framework. For context management, we find that preserving individually accessible records of past attempts is more effective than maintaining a rolling summary, with context folding performing best across all six settings and improving upon the baseline by up to 19.2%. For skill management, selectively discarding persistently unused lessons is more effective on average than consolidating them or promoting them to standing rules. Building on these findings, we combine context folding and skill deletion in MLEvolver, which achieves a 72.7% medal rate on MLE-Bench Lite and a 94% $\mathrm{fast}_1$ score on KernelBench Level 3, substantially outperforming AIDE and OpenEvolve under the same inference budget. Overall, our study distills design principles for preserving experimental history and curating reusable lessons in autoresearch harnesses, paving the way for agents that turn accumulated experience into sustained progress.
  </details>

- **[FinEvo: Recursive Self-Improvement for Long-Horizon Financial Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28239)**  `arXiv:2611.28239`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chengjun Pan, Shichun Liu, Zekai Zhou, Shihan Dou, Jiahang Lin, Chengsong Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in large language models (LLMs) have expanded their role in high-value productivity tasks, including financial analysis and modeling, while enabling models to play an increasingly active role in their own improvement. This motivates two complementary lines of inquiry: can financial modeling capabilities provide a foundation for model self-evolution, and can self-evolution, in turn, advance financial capabilities? We introduce FinEvo, a self-evolution framework that integrates autonomous experience construction with financial capability improvement. FinEvo prepares financial models, iteratively evolves challenges through self-play, and learns from the resulting interactions using executable rewards, relying on the same model throughout. An end-to-end execution with Qwen3.6-27B shows that solving feedback supports both increasing reasoning demands and identifying specification issues that executable checks can miss. Learning from these experiences raises whole-task success from 22.73 to 29.09 on held-out within-source tasks and from 13.13 to 19.19 on SpreadsheetBench 2 Financial. Comparable gains across task sources and evaluation settings, together with improvement on a task category absent from training, suggest that FinEvo strengthens underlying financial modeling capabilities shared across tasks. These findings support self-evolution as a path toward broader financial competence, advancing the empirical exploration of recursive self-improvement (RSI) in professional domains. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoPILOT: A Visual Agent Harness for Real-Time Gameplay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28202)**  `arXiv:2611.28202`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mark Zhao, Pengcheng Wang, Rui Yang, Jiarui Yao, Xirui Jiang, Yuxi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal foundation models can play video games well when the game waits for them, but often fail when it keeps running. We find that much of this gap lies in the harness, which decides what the model sees, what it remembers, and how its outputs become actions over time. Existing harnesses such as VISTA improve the first two, yet turn each model call into at most one action, so the agent acts only as often as its model thinks. We introduce AutoPILOT, which changes the third: in each model call, the model can send a primitive action, start an open-loop macro, or write a closed-loop controller that keeps playing from the screen without further model calls until it hands control back. To evaluate agents in real-time games, we build AFK-Bench, 120 diverse browser games grouped by how they respond to inaction and playable in real time or with the clock paused. With GPT-6.1 Sol under the same budgets, AutoPILOT reaches 68.6% real-time success against 38.3% for VISTA, and on GameWorld it keeps 80.0% in real time while VISTA drops from 84.1% to 42.9%. Its gains hold across models, and it reaches VISTA's final success rate at a fifth of its API cost. Our results suggest that how a harness turns model outputs into actions over time is a central yet underexplored part of agent design, and we hope AutoPILOT and AFK-Bench will serve as a foundation for agents in a world that does not wait.
  </details>

- **[WePM: Hierarchical Conflict Reconciliation for Unified MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27365)**  `arXiv:2611.27365`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiqiang Yuan, Qiwei Yan, Liwei Yang, Jinzhe Liu, Yang Liu, Zhongyan Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bringing multimodal intelligence from the cloud to users' devices keeps sensitive data local and reduces inference cost, but deploying a specialized model per capability duplicates vision-language capacity under tight memory and compute budgets. We formalize heterogeneous capability density (HCD), measuring how broadly and effectively a model serves heterogeneous task families per unit of resident inference memory. High HCD across representation, generation, and decision raises two challenges: these capabilities need visual evidence at different spatial granularities under a fixed token budget, and separately collected task corpora yield conflicting updates to shared parameters, while cross-task paired supervision is costly and ambiguous for open-ended inputs. We present WePM, a family of pocket models unifying representation, generation, and decision within one vision-language backbone. Multi-Granularity Visual Budgeting (MGVB) allocates a fixed visual-token budget across global, regional, and fine-grained banks; Unified Listwise Knowledge Distillation (ULKD) enriches separately collected data with soft teacher guidance, avoiding cross-task paired annotation; Stable and Efficient Multitask Optimization (SEMO) regulates each task's contribution through explicit loss weights and exposure schedules, with sequential gradient accumulation under memory constraints. In the joint representation-generation setting, WePM outperforms all baselines across five evaluation groups, raising the Joint score from 38.15 to 52.74. In single-task evaluation, WePM-4B attains the best average score over 30 embedding, 39 generation, and seven typed-decision benchmarks (72.86, 62.41, 61.11). Against the separate-model stack with the highest HCD, WePM achieves slightly higher balanced performance with 61.9% less resident weight memory, a 2.64-fold higher HCD.
  </details>

- **[MixFold: Sub-4-Bit Mixed-Precision LLM Inference via Output-Channel Grouping on Mobile NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26976)**  `arXiv:2611.26976`  `cs.AI`  
  _Vinnam Kim, Seong Hoon Seo, Junhan Kim, Jaeseong You, An Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sub-4-bit quantization allows mobile devices to run larger LLMs, but its memory savings do not always translate into faster decoding on mobile NPUs. Their matrix units support only a few native integer datatypes, and converting unsupported weight formats on the vector unit can erase the gains from fewer bits. Mixed precision faces an additional constraint: each matrix-unit tile uses one datatype. MixFold executes 3-bit and perfectly symmetric 2-bit weights using native integer operations without expanding the weights to wider datatypes during decoding. MixFold then assigns datatypes to output-channel groups aligned with the matrix-unit tiles, enabling finer-than-layer assignment without disrupting matrix-unit execution. We compare MixFold with $\mathtt{this http URL}$ Hexagon backend on the Qualcomm Hexagon NPU of a Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5. Across the Qwen3 model family, MixFold achieves comparable or higher accuracy than $\mathtt{this http URL}$'s sub-4-bit formats at matched bits per weight, while decoding 1.16-1.41 times faster and generating 1.5-4.3 times more tokens per joule.
  </details>

- **[AI agents can redesign engineering parts with more than a thousand CAD parameters and nonlinear simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26003)**  `arXiv:2611.26003`  `cs.AI`  
  _Alexandra Starostina, Alexander Hermann, Mahish Guru, Kartik Bali, Roland Aydin, Christian C. Cyron_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation-driven design of real engineering components has to meet three conditions at once. It runs on few solves, since a nonlinear finite-element simulation of a 3D part takes hours; its design space has many values, the hundreds to thousands of values of the part's CAD model; and a better design often needs a redesign, coordinated changes of the part's proportions rather than local polishing of its surface. Automated optimisers meet at most two of these conditions. Classical optimisers, Bayesian or derivative-free, need a person to cut the design down to a few parameters, and on all the values they need far more solves; industrial shape optimisers move every surface node of the mesh in one solve per cycle, but only in small steps that polish rather than redesign. We ask whether a general-purpose large language model (LLM) can meet all three by joining what it knows with what the simulation shows. GPT-6 Astra edits a magnesium stent through a CAD geometry parametrisation of about 1,650 values, with a budget of 15 simulations; every proposal is built in CAD, meshed and simulated with large-deformation plasticity and contact, and the LLM reads the full results of every solve. Within that budget it lowered the objective, a high percentile of the strains in the stent's bends, by 32%; on the same budget, the industrial shape optimiser Tosca improved the original design by a tenth and then stopped improving. An earlier GPT-6 Astra design, 25% lower in the same objective, was checked in an independent, higher-fidelity model of the full deployment with crimping and damage: it was 14% less strained than the original after the full chain, and its most damaged spot began to fail later and carried 63% less damage at full expansion. LLMs can thus join their learned knowledge with simulation data to adjust more than a thousand design values and reach valid designs with real improvements.
  </details>

- **[ROAM-GRPO: Enforcing Reasoning Diversity in GRPO via Routed Off-mode Approach Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25926)**  `arXiv:2611.25926`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jeff Duan, Matt Thomson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) paired with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has become the dominant recipe for post-training LLMs on complex reasoning tasks, but has been shown to collapse the policy into a low-diversity mode. Repeated LLM outputs are nearly identical, and pass@k accuracy converges or degrades as a result. We propose ROAM-GRPO (Routed Off-mode Approach Mining for GRPO), an augmentation to the GRPO algorithm which incentivizes exploration of distinct reasoning strategies and aims to increase output creativity during evaluation. ROAM-GRPO detects well-solved problems during training and transfers them to an "off-mode" track. The model is prompted to produce distinct solution strategies to such problems, which are verified for logical validity and distinctness by an LLM judge and subsequently self-distilled into the policy. Training Qwen3.5-2b on a 1,000-problem subset of the MATH lvl5 dataset, ROAM-GRPO implementation increases peak pass@16 by 4.7 percentage points and correct embedding cosine distance by approximately 1.5-2x on a difficult evaluation set.
  </details>

- **[AppSci-Bench: Can LLMs Build AI Agents and Make Them Better?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25360)**  `arXiv:2611.25360`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sanjari Srivastava, Manpreet Kaur, Cheng Chang, Sofía Ramós Anton, Oumaima Essamadi, Rishu Garg, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used not only to power AI agents but also to build them. While many benchmarks assess the quality of LLM- powered agents, few ascertain how good LLMs are at building them. To this end, we introduce AppSci-Bench, where we construct a setting akin to how human Applied Scientists build agents for businesses - through eliciting task requirements from stakeholders, exploring the business's infrastructure, and scientifically improve the target agent by designing experiments and internal benchmarks. AppSci-Bench features seven realistic domains - five new ones (Slides Generation, Order-to-Cash, Compliance Review, Customer Call Insights, and CRM Insights) and two domains recast from tau3-bench (Yao et al., 2024; Shi et al., 2026b)(Airline and Banking) - each with a held-out evaluation suite customized for that task. We evaluate seven frontier models and find AppSci-Bench challenging: the best model, Claude Opus 5.0, only reaches a mean-minimum score of 59.1% across 3 build attempts; other models do significantly worse. Models struggle with eliciting task requirements (only 65% for Opus 5.0), build representative internal evaluations (models saturate their own evaluation 65% of the time but fail to solve the held-out task), presenting challenges for future research. The benchmark code is available here:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[EverydayBench: Evaluating Satisfactory Completion of Live User-Facing Digital Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25146)**  `arXiv:2611.25146`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fangrui Huang, Bingyang Wang, Chuhan Li, Yijiang Li, Arpandeep Khatua, Austin Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Everyday digital tasks, from selecting products and planning trips to conducting research and creating artifacts, require AI agents to deliver useful outcomes rather than merely retrieve web information. Yet evaluating these outcomes on the live web is challenging, as a single request may admit multiple valid results, and changing web information can alter which results remain valid over time. Although existing benchmarks address aspects of this setting, they do not fully integrate predefined success criteria, current evidence verification, and deliverable-quality assessment for agent-selected outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce EverydayBench, a benchmark of 77 human-authored tasks spanning eight domains of everyday life and work. Each task specifies success criteria before execution without prescribing an answer or workflow, allowing agents to select their own outcomes. To evaluate submissions under fixed success criteria, EverydayBench combines (1) deterministic checks of explicit constraints, (2) verification of claims against agent-cited webpages retrieved at evaluation time, and (3) rubric-based assessment of final deliverable quality. We further validate the protocol through human judgments, repeated scoring under fixed evidence, and controlled grounding failures. Across 23 reported agent-model configurations, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Fable 5.1 tie for the highest strict pass rate at 54.55%; a controlled GPT-6 Sol effort sweep ranges from 29.87% at low effort to 45.45% at max effort. Reliability varies substantially by task type: GPT-5.6 Sol leads lookup, Fable 5.1 leads planning, Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1 tie on comparison, and GPT-6 Astra and Sonnet 5.5 tie on customized deliverables. Together, these findings highlight the difficulty of delivering complete, well-supported, and usable outcomes for everyday user requests.
  </details>

- **[TeleChat to Xing4: Advancing Frontier Agentic Models on Domestic Chips and Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22115)**  `arXiv:2611.22115`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xingchen AGI Lab, Baotian Hu, Bingkai Yang, Boyang Wang, Changzai Pan, Chao Lyu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Xing 4, a series of large language models that extends the TeleChat lineage through coordinated advances in model architecture and training infrastructure on Ascend. This report describes the design principles and systems engineering behind the series, spanning architecture selection, large-scale pretraining, and reasoning and agentic post-training. We study how sparse activation, attention design, model depth and width, and speculative decoding balance model capacity with computation, memory access, and execution overhead. Our pretraining infrastructure builds on MindSpore HyperParallel, combining topology-aware hybrid sharding, efficient distributed optimizer execution, and coordinated management of long-sequence workloads and activation memory. CPU affinity and core isolation further reduce host scheduling interference. Together, these mechanisms support the pretraining of a 285B-parameter model on 8,192 Ascend 910C cards, providing a concrete setting for the architectural and systems studies. For post-training, we introduce TeleGym, a unified agent execution platform that connects execution, evaluation, and trajectory construction to policy optimization. Disaggregated asynchronous execution, bounded oversampling, and partial rollout accommodate heterogeneous task durations while preserving trajectory continuity across policy updates. Together, these advances establish an Ascend-oriented training foundation for the continued development of Xing 4 across model scales and increasingly demanding reasoning and agent workloads.
  </details>

- **[CompoundingKernel: Self-Improving from Zero to First](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19230)**  `arXiv:2611.19230`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qian Qu, Hongyang Wang, Wenbo Chen, Yichuan Li, Jingguo Liu, Xian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present CompoundingKernel, a self-improving agent system that writes GPU kernels by search. As of 2026-11-11, its generated kernels rank #1 on the public L1, L2 and Quantization boards of SOL-ExecBench (NVIDIA B200). They have held #1 on L1 since 2026-10-27, albeit a short loss on 2026-11-07. All 235 problems have a site-verified correct kernel; on the 209 public-board problems they are 4.40x faster than the benchmark's reference baseline. The search starts from zero: agents get each problem's PyTorch definition but no reference kernels or official scores. A per-problem archive outside the model holds every correct kernel and its lineage. An LLM controller reads it, picks a parent or a new design, and gives each parallel child agent one direction. Checks outside the model decide correctness. At equal wall time, it beat a coded UCB style tree on 35 of 38 problems by making 2.4x as many correct attempts. Over a thousand agents ran at once, and one warm restart fix raised their evaluation rate 3.6x. We find that the rate of reliable measurements, not the number of agents, limits how far such a search scales. Our code and kernels are available onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PhysioWave-T: Token-Efficient Wavelet Foundation Models for Biosignals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08969)**  `arXiv:2611.08969`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yanlong Chen, Simone Benatti, Luca Benini, Cuntai Guan, Yawei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present PhysioWave-T, a token-efficient wavelet foundation model family for EEG, ECG, and sEMG. Dynamic scale folding recombines $J+1$ wavelet subbands before tokenization, reducing the sequence from $(J+1)CS$ to $CS$ tokens for $J$ decomposition levels, $C$ channels, and $S$ temporal patches. Route-specific front ends and learned channel-name embeddings allow one Transformer per modality to handle diverse montages and sampling rates. Unseen configurations reuse the pre-trained backbone through a lightweight front end. We pre-train a 30M-parameter EEG model on over 15M windows spanning 19 to 128 channels, alongside ECG (30M) and sEMG (12M) encoders. Across four EEG routes, scale folding cuts Transformer latency by 4.0--7.2x and peak memory by 6.8--12.7x relative to scale expansion without accuracy loss. Our EEG encoder leads on eight of nine metrics against BENDR, BIOT, LaBraM, and EEGPT and surpasses LUNA-L on wearable seizure detection with fewer parameters. Reliability-aware latent fusion (RALF) combines summary tokens from the independently pre-trained encoders using per-sample reliability weights. On SeizeIT2, event sensitivity at 11 false alarms/h is 61.4% for EEG alone, 74.4% for RALF, and 78.9% for weighted-logit fusion. RALF raises window-level AUPRC from 0.321 (EEG alone) to 0.399. All six RALF training variants outperform weighted-logit and concatenation fusion on this metric. PhysioWave-T also achieves state-of-the-art sEMG accuracies and the highest macro-F1 on PTB-XL Super and Chapman-Shaoxing. Code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reglos: A Self-Evolving Personal Memory Agent with Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06049)**  `arXiv:2611.06049`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sen Yang, Boqin Yuan, Jiaduo Lin, Zehong Huang, Wen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personal agents draw on past conversations to tailor their responses to each user. Their memory systems store and update what each user says, and coding agents now also rewrite the harness that turns this memory into the model's context. Such patches risk overfitting, because one tested only on the failures it targets can fix those cases and help nowhere else. We present Reglos, a personal memory agent that tests whether harness changes help beyond the failures they were written to fix. Its self-evolving memory adds to each user's append-only record with every conversation, as new preferences supersede old ones and facts can be forgotten. Its harness improves through recursive self-improvement, in which a coding agent proposes patches from failure traces and a critic from a second model family screens each one for benchmark gaming. After the harness is frozen, each further patch is tested on users the loop has not seen. With the same backbone behind every system, Reglos outperforms state-of-the-art memory systems such as Mem0, MemOS and EverOS, reaching 67.7% and 84.0% on PersonaMem-v2 and LongMemEval-S with Qwen3.8-27B, and 62.1% and 87.2% with GPT-5.6 Luna.
  </details>

- **[AI Data Analysis: A New Approach - Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00807)**  `arXiv:2611.00807`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jianfeng Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI data analysis today is three fragmented worlds: LLMs understand semantics but cannot compute; BI aggregates precisely but is stateless; ML processes large-scale data on a stateful kernel but lacks semantics. This paper proposes a combination mechanism, named Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis, that fills a still-open gap - the multi-engine joint context: the data context is embedded into LLM conversational analysis, where the LLM serves only as the semantic, reasoning, and orchestration layer (not computation) and connects four engines through the 1M context window - Python and R ML kernels, SQL over relational databases, Cypher over graph databases - yielding two multiplications, "semantics and precise computation" and "relational and graph", under enterprise-grade governance. The novelty is a four-dimensional coordinate system: horizontal (engine kinds), vertical (state flow - the watershed), spatial (business generalization), temporal (iterative convergence); a deterministic grounding method (one-shot injection ofthis http URL+this http URL, M+S) carries the spatial and temporal dimensions. Empirically, all four dimensions are substantiated: horizontally, four engines are quantified - SQL, Cypher (96.5% on CypherBench movie), ML in Python and R (TabReD weather); vertically, state flows across engines and grounding is reused across kernels; spatially, SQL generalizes across three business databases - AdventureWorks 91.3%, financial 95.3%, formula_1 94.6% (average), about 20-23 points above single-shot SOTA 74.45%; temporally, accuracy converges monotonically as notes accumulate. The core invariant - the separation of semantics from computation plus a cross-data-domain persistent context - does not expire as LLMs and agents evolve or engine forms change.
  </details>

- **[JockeyBench: The Racing Analyst's Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19589)**  `arXiv:2610.19589`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhongwei Xie, Xingsu Li, Jingqiao Yu, Zihao Wang, Hong ChiTat, Zhaowei Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating LLM agents in noisy sequential forecasting is challenging: strong performance may reflect a capable initial predictor rather than successful online revision, while accurate forecasts may still fail to deliver economic value after costs and market frictions. Existing benchmarks rarely isolate revision gains, compare reusable model-written predictors with direct LLM forecasting on the same tasks, or assess forecast quality alongside research costs and realized returns. To address these gaps, we introduce JockeyBench, a leakage-controlled replay benchmark built from 12 years of Japanese horse-racing records. Agents acquire evidence at a cost and seal probabilistic forecasts before outcomes are revealed. To isolate the effect of revision, we pair each adaptive code-as-policy agent with a frozen copy of its initial predictor. We also compare code-as-policy, which executes reusable prediction programs, with LLM-as-policy, which forecasts each race directly. We evaluate 29 models on 324 races. The strongest initial predictors outperform a conditional-logit baseline trained on 20,730 races. The two top-ranked models retain their initial predictors throughout evaluation. Revision helps weaker predictors correct overconfidence, but updates driven by noisy outcomes can hurt performance. Code-as-policy generally produces more accurate forecasts at lower research cost, while models that struggle to write reliable predictors benefit from forecasting directly with LLM-as-policy. Yet all 29 models, including Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, have negative mean returns in retrospective settlement. JockeyBench thus offers a demanding testbed for current LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Cross-Modal Hallucinations by Correcting Modality Misrouting in Omnimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18141)**  `arXiv:2610.18141`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Mi, Licheng Pan, Jiayi Zhu, Qirui Liu, Yitong Qiao, Changtong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omnimodal large language models (OLLMs) are susceptible to cross-modal hallucinations under audio-visual interference. We find that, for a substantial fraction of incorrect joint predictions, the question-relevant unimodal branch can still recover the correct answer, suggesting that task-relevant evidence can remain recoverable from the relevant modality even when it is not reflected in the joint prediction. We refer to this recoverable failure pattern as \emph{modality misrouting}. We propose CaRe (Conflict-aware Residual Intervention), a training-free inference-time method for identifying and correcting modality misrouting. CaRe uses a text-only modality probe to identify a candidate modality-specific counterfactual branch, and flags potential misrouting when its prediction conflicts with the original joint prediction. For such cases, CaRe computes a residual direction from the discrepancy between the counterfactual and fused representations and uses it to steer the original multimodal state toward the relevant modality evidence, while retaining both modalities in the original audio-visual inference path. The intervention strength is adapted based on the confidence of the counterfactual branch. Experiments on CMM and AVHBench across two OLLMs show that CaRe consistently outperforms the evaluated inference-time hallucination-mitigation baselines.
  </details>

- **[AdaC-GRPO: Towards Mastering Long-Horizon Visual Sudoku Puzzles via Adaptive Curriculum Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13184)**  `arXiv:2610.13184`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sida Li, Yuanxin Liu, Linli Yao, Shicheng Li, Xu Sun, Haoli Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR) enhances reasoning in large language models, its efficacy in long-horizon multimodal tasks remains underexplored. Visual Sudoku puzzles pose a representative challenge, requiring multi-step deductive reasoning over both local constraints and global dependency chains. To this end, we introduce FG-Sudoku, a fine-grained benchmark categorized by grid size and required deductive steps. Evaluations reveal that frontier MLLMs fail on complex long-range dependencies (e.g., 9*9 puzzles). During post-training, this near-zero success rate causes severe reward sparsity, halting standard RLVR. We propose Adaptive Curriculum Group Relative Policy Optimization (AdaC-GRPO) to overcome this. Unlike rigid or prior-based schedules, AdaC-GRPO dynamically constructs training batches by tracking the model's "Zone of Proximal Development" via online rollout accuracy. Experiments demonstrate that AdaC-GRPO enables Qwen2.5-VL-7B to approach the performance of Gemini-2.5-Flash while bridging 62.2% of the overall accuracy gap to Gemini-2.5-Pro, with its generalizability further validated across InternVL-3.5 and Qwen3-VL.
  </details>

- **[PLCWorld: Benchmarking LLM-Generated PLC Programs in Closed-Loop Plant Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02982)**  `arXiv:2610.02982`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunji Kim, Yunseok Lee, Hyunwoo Seo, Jaerim Choi, Woojin Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Programmable logic controllers (PLCs) coordinate industrial equipment through sensor inputs and control commands. Evaluating LLM-generated PLC programs requires observing device and workpiece states to assess task completion and safety. We introduce PLCWorld, a closed-loop execution environment and benchmark coupling Structured Text (ST) execution with simulated plant responses and sensor feedback. Grounded in control relations from industrial PLC programs and engineering documentation, PLCWorld contains 100 synthetic tasks and 473 registered task-condition pairs across Motion Control and Material Handling, with difficulty defined by control-dependency scope. It reports Task Success and Safety Violation separately. Execution Gap measures the fraction of cases with task failure or observed safety violation among those assigned to programs passing a common submission-profile and I/O check. Validation combines practitioner review, reference and alternative programs, 542 targeted counterexamples, alignment checks, and independent ST runtime comparisons. Across six LLMs and four adapted generation-and-verification workflows, direct GPT-5.5 achieves 83.96% Task Success on Easy cases but 24.51% on Hard cases. The workflows show no consistent plant-level gains, and failures persist after upstream acceptance. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SimpleEvol: An Agent-Loop Framework for LLM-Driven Automated Heuristic Design with Minimal Human Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37172)**  `arXiv:2609.37172`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jianghan Zhu, Cong Zhang, Rongjie Zhu, Chi Zhang, Zhiguang Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for automated heuristic design (AHD), enabling iterative generation and refinement of heuristics. However, the dominant paradigm embeds LLMs as narrow, fixed components, such as crossover or mutation, within heavily hand-engineered evolutionary frameworks. We argue this misapprehends LLMs. It treats them as specialized tools rather than general reasoners, constrains them to low-level operations, and underutilizes their autonomy. Moreover, the extensive human priors in these frameworks violate the bitter lesson principle that general methods scaling with computation surpass hand-crafted solutions. This raises a key question: which AHD framework designs best convert stronger LLM capabilities into better heuristics? To address this, we propose metrics for LLM-driven AHD framework handcraftedness (AHI) and intelligence conversion efficiency (ICE). Evaluating ten LLMs across three challenging combinatorial optimization problems, we obtain a notable finding that frameworks with fewer human priors consistently yield higher ICE. Based on this finding, we propose SimpleEvol, an agent-loop framework for AHD which removes nearly all human priors and allows the LLM to operate autonomously. SimpleEvol consistently achieves the highest ICE, often by a large margin. Our results challenge the trend toward complex AHD pipelines and point to a lighter and more model-centric alternative, suggesting that reducing human priors is a more effective strategy to scale up with model intelligence. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SkillCome: Group Contrast Skill Optimization with Dual Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37128)**  `arXiv:2609.37128`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haolin Li, Feng Hong, Ang Li, Chilin Fu, Weichang Wu, Ya Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill evolution improves the capabilities of large language models by analyzing trajectories generated under a given skill and modifying the skill accordingly. Existing approaches typically generate a single trajectory per question. However, this provides insufficient optimization signals since it requires inferring effective skill edits from a solitary path. It is difficult to pinpoint which actions caused the failure in a failed trajectory, or to determine which actions in a successful one should be incorporated into the skill. Furthermore, they rely on a local batch of trajectories for analysis, making the optimization direction susceptible to noisy evidence. To address these, we propose SkillCome, a Skill-evolution method based on group Contrast optimization with dual memory. For each question, SkillCome generates trajectories and performs group contrast analysis to precisely identify key behavioral divergences between successful and failed trajectories, offering reliable optimization signals. The dual memory system further accumulates evidence from historical steps to track patterns shared across different groups, leading to more generalized optimization directions. Together, SkillCome builds a systematic optimization process that transforms experience from observed successful trajectories into reusable skills. Extensive experiments on six benchmarks spanning question answering, reasoning, and agentic tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our method. SkillCome consistently outperforms baselines across five models of varying families and scales, with gains up to +5.69 points. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Stale Does Not Mean Unsafe: Measuring Commit-Guard Precision for Tool-Using LLM Agents in Simulated and Transactional Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29522)**  `arXiv:2609.29522`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zihao Zheng, Jiayu Long, Baichuan Li, Junyi Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language models act on state that can change while they plan. A version check detects that change, but an audit update and a revoked approval have different consequences for a pending action. We study how commit guards distinguish changes that preserve an action's safety conditions from changes that invalidate them. ShiftGuard pairs both types of race across sixteen infrastructure operations and replays identical proposals under alternative guards. The primary simulator grid contains 3,456 frozen proposals from three models, with a separate 1,152-proposal replication on a fourth. We implement the same operations on PostgreSQL and score persisted effects with a requirements-based checker. Among benign races in the scripted PostgreSQL benchmark, the runtime-held version and global-epoch guards block 80% and 100%, respectively, while the row-local predicate guard blocks none. The predicate guard records zero unsafe writes on the declared benchmark conditions, and all five additional hazards outside its declared contract escape it. A version guard using the model's expected version permits unsafe writes on 4.9% of primary replay decisions. The runtime-held version guard records zero in the same replay. These results identify two design choices for agent tools: bind freshness tokens to runtime observations, and represent safety conditions explicitly at the point where the effect is applied.
  </details>

- **[TwinCheck: Evidence-Grounded Negative-Twin Verification for Stateful Tool Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26911)**  `arXiv:2609.26911`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaxuan Dai, Tianyi Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A single locally plausible tool call can derail an otherwise successful agent trajectory. Suspicion alone does not justify intervention, because the replacement itself can introduce the very failure verification is meant to prevent. We introduce TwinCheck, an inference-time verification policy that considers replacement only when the trace satisfies an evidence condition tied to a local failure hypothesis. It constructs a trace-grounded counterfactual alternative, a negative twin, and replaces the actor's proposal only if the twin passes structural checks and the pairwise verifier prefers it in both candidate orders.
